mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i shall mention the part of europe's cultural heritage which is not on the unesco list and which is hardly on our own lists. it is the fact that almost every square metre of the european union is cultivated land, both agricultural land and forestry land. all of our current biodiversity is the result of over 10,000 years of struggle and toil. it is the fruit of a collaboration between man as farmer, nature, the climate and technology. this is what gives us the europe we know, the immense diversity and variety. i believe, of course, that cultural heritage is the responsibility of the individual buildings and nations, not primarily of the union. there is, however, one area in which the union has full power over cultural heritage, namely agricultural policy. if i had a choice, i would replace the current eu agricultural policy with a policy for cultural heritage and biodiversity. i would like to use subsidies to preserve mediterranean terraced farming, to preserve the biologically rich and stunningly beautiful mountain meadows that surround the rivers and lakes of the forested scandinavia. in short: a good cultural policy for europe would be a modified, modern and considerate agricultural policy.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled an opinion on the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the court's report on the ecb covers the period of transition from the emi to the present-day ecb, which is also the subject of the report. some of the problems identified in the report are specifically linked to the transition. criticism is directed at project follow-up, which focused more on the amount of money spent than on the actual progress of the work. however, the main criticism from both the court of auditors and the committee on budgetary control concerns the extra bonus received by the bank's employees in addition to their normal bonus, which significantly exceeded both the established maximum levels and the budgeted costs. the methodology for deciding on this additional cost has also been criticised. the ecb has commented on this and, inter alia, stated that it has addressed the criticism of project management and that it has improved its procedures. the ecb also acknowledges that there is a lack of clarity about this additional bonus. we are also pleased that the ecb is now allowing olaf to carry out internal investigations, but it is very unfortunate that this is delayed. i am responsible for the opinion of the committee on economic and monetary affairs on this matter, and our conclusions are simple and concise. first of all: although this was a difficult transitional period, there is a great deal of substance in the court's remarks on, for example, project management, which must be taken into account in the future. secondly: the type of bonus system discussed here is common in the banking world, but the levels that were in place here and the way the system was handled really deserve criticism. the most worrying thing is that the bank's press officer, in the swedish media, among others, is dismissing the criticism of both the court of auditors and the european parliament and claims that it is being laughed at in the banking world, which is serious. if the bank, already criticised for its lack of transparency, does not take the criticism seriously, there is a risk that confidence will be shaken. however, my committee believes that the criticism is not so serious that it needs to affect the confidence in the ecb or lead to a question of the efficiency of the institution. on the contrary, the bank deserves a lot of credit for the smooth transition from the emi to the ecb.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i would first like to thank mrs oomen-ruijten for an excellent report. there have been many attempts in the past to use the problems of frontier workers as a sort of punching bag to harmonize the whole of tax legislation. but oomen-ruijten knows that frontier workers do not even make up half a percent of the workforce, that their problems are different in different border regions and that therefore different regional solutions are needed if the situation of frontier workers is to improve. i can speak from personal experience, for i have been commuting between denmark and sweden for 25 years. now that we have a bridge between copenhagen and malmö, everyone expects the border area to increase considerably and the two cities to become a metropolitan region. in 1997, on the initiative of denmark, the eu-supported principle of taxation in the country of work was introduced. this means that people can settle in malmö and have access to health care, childcare, education and everything else, while paying all the taxes to the danish state. this is completely unsustainable if we are to have a common öresund region. the alternative advocated by swedish politicians is the oecd model of border taxation, which advocates that tax be levied in the country of residence. but even such a principle is problematic, since the tax funds much more than the social welfare of families. in fact, in öresund, the best thing would be to pay the municipal tax where we live and the state tax where we work. i think that would put an end to tax planning and border scams. but even such a model would require some sort of fine-tuning to create full justice, because these are complicated things. it is therefore essential that solutions are designed in the regions concerned themselves, based on the basic principle that cross-border workers should pay tax where they have their tax-funded benefits. only then can we achieve long-term sustainable solutions. in the case of the sound, such a principle is absolutely necessary if it is to become a single region.

in writing. - (da) the joint motion for a resolution contains many interesting ideas, but it does not measure up to the seriousness of the situation caused by nato for the civilian population of the balkans, the soldiers who were sent on mission there and their relatives. about 20 soldiers sent to the balkans have already died of leukemia and that number will rise. american soldiers in kfor were advised not to inhale depleted uranium. they were advised not to touch any object that may have been in contact with depleted uranium, were asked to leave areas that may have been contaminated by dust from weapons containing depleted uranium, and were also asked to wash their uniforms frequently. we don't think it's the custom of the u.s. military to issue such a warning without cause. a number of analyses have also shown the high toxicity of inhalation of depleted uranium. the consequences for the civilian population living in areas that have been subject to nato warfare have not yet been fully analysed. the future will tell us more. we therefore believe that it is not enough to simply re-analyze the consequences of the use of weapons containing depleted uranium. we already know enough. and we believe that the precautionary principle should be applied in all circumstances, including to persons who present themselves as candidates for international missions and to the civilian population living in war zones. these weapons must be included in the provisions of the geneva convention on chemical and nuclear weapons. we must stop all production of these weapons and destroy all stocks. we demand an immediate end to the use of weapons containing depleted uranium!

madam president, ladies and gentlemen, and mr president-in-office of the council! the swedish presidency's programme contains several points which we in the united left support. we believe that the priorities enlargement, environment and employment are both important and right. there are also other positive aspects of the programme which are worth highlighting. this includes action against trafficking in women and efforts to achieve lasting peace in palestine. as regards the enlargement negotiations, we believe it is crucial that the eu takes account of the special needs of the applicant countries and that they are not offered second-class eu membership. in relation to this objective, the outcome of nice is a failure. there was no real reform of the eu to facilitate enlargement. on the contrary, the decision-making process became more complex. it is also totally unacceptable that candidate countries such as the czech republic and hungary were directly discriminated against by receiving fewer seats in the european parliament than eu countries with the same population. this is an embarrassing mistake that must be corrected. my first question to the swedish presidency is therefore: are you prepared to address and correct this error in the accession treaties now to be negotiated? some eu countries want to exclude citizens of the future eu countries from the union's labour market for a long time. such discrimination is not acceptable. the key point is that everyone working in an eu country must respect the country's labour market agreements and laws so that free movement does not lead to dumping in terms of social conditions. a genuine left-wing policy must also put employment first in economic policy, instead of the dogmatic monetarism which today dominates the eu and monetary union. such a policy would also require democratic control of the european central bank. but for today's eu governments, regardless of their political colour, the priority has been to adapt to emu rather than to welfare and the fight against unemployment. this policy must be reviewed. there is a growing demand in various parts of the european union that unbridled capitalism must be managed by political decisions instead of the continued deregulation and free speculation we see today. one of the demands is a tax on foreign exchange transactions, a so-called tobin tax. several members of the swedish government have expressed their support for the idea. this is your golden opportunity to take the initiative during your presidency. my second question to the presidency is therefore: what initiatives for a tobin tax are you planning during your presidency? the most important environmental issue of the next six months will be the negotiations on the kyoto protocol to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. it is extremely important to reach an agreement, but it is even more important to reach an agreement with substance. we believe that such an agreement must be based on real reductions in emissions, not on the rich countries which already emit the most being given the right to buy their way out through emissions trading. in the recently concluded settlement on the release of genetically modified crops, sweden's actions were a disappointment. sweden actively opposed public registers of gmo crops. we hope that sweden will recover and not act to lift the de facto moratorium on the area. the eu legislation in this area is still wholly inadequate from an environmental point of view. unfortunately, it seems that the swedish presidency is not willing to do anything to fundamentally change the eu's irresponsible refugee policy, which, with the schengen agreement as a tool, is creating new tragedies every day. on the contrary, we have seen how sweden accepts deterioration, most recently by tightening up the responsibility of the transporter. this means a kind of privatisation of the asylum procedure, whereby the fate of many asylum seekers is effectively decided by airport staff outside the european union. the biggest disappointment in sweden's actions is probably the militarisation of the eu. sweden is not only giving up its freedom of association and independent policy, but is also now to take active action to implement the militarisation of the eu. the eu army will also include turkish troops. the troops who can commit atrocities in their own country one day or occupy a neighbouring country can serve in the eu army the next. the eu has also been working with turkey to develop a common defence policy. my third question to göran persson is therefore: can you promise me that turkey will not have any such influence? i look forward to the answers to my three questions on the possibility of changing the discrimination of hungary and the czech republic under the nice treaty, on what you are going to do to make the tobin tax a reality and on how you see turkey's involvement and influence over the eu's military. with these words, the united left wishes the swedish presidency good luck. the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking all my colleagues in parliament and in the committee for the support i have received in the preparation of this report. the safety of our roads is something that concerns us all, whether we are road users ourselves or have lost a family member or friend to death or injury. road transport is the most common and fastest growing mode of travel and transport of goods and cargo, but also the most dangerous. every year, more than 42 000 people die on our european roads. that's over 800 people a week, and over 115 people today alone. road traffic accidents are the leading cause of death for children and young people - yes, for all people under 45 - in the eu today. millions of people are also seriously injured each year, many becoming disabled for life. it is a matter of great human suffering and great social cost. we would never tolerate these deaths in air or rail transport, where we have a very different safety concept. so to reduce road fatalities we need to get the same thinking in the road, because it is also a complex man/machine system that has to tolerate human error. the long-term goal should be that no one is killed or seriously injured. to achieve this, we need to take a holistic approach, with ultimate responsibility for the design of roads and the surrounding infrastructure. traffic drivers have a responsibility to follow the rules and laws that have been put in place. the commission communication sets out a priority of measures for the remainder of the programming period. i agree with the commission on the importance of several of these measures. this applies, for example, to the support for euroncap, information campaigns, legislation on speed controls in light trucks and, not least, legislation on safer car fronts. in my report i then focused on the next programming period, which should extend over a longer period proposed for the period 2002-2010 to allow for a more long-term approach. but if the commission is serious about its ambitions to reduce the number of road deaths, then the whole question of road safety must be given priority in its work, because this is where action is needed. first of all, a credible strategy for road deaths over the next ten years must contain concrete numerical targets for reducing the number of deaths. the committee proposes the objectives set by parliament three years ago. but eu-level objectives are not sufficiently concrete for the member states, where much of the work is done. therefore, each member state must itself draw up its own road safety programmes, which not all have today, with strategies for how to achieve the national targets. of course, this may vary from country to country, depending on tradition and culture. but then the commission should monitor how the states are complying with their strategies and participate in the exchange of experience and best practice. we also need european legislation in some important areas. alcohol kills about 9,000 people on the roads every year. the trend also shows that more and more young people are driving ratton-type vehicles. the introduction of a directive with a maximum blood alcohol level of 0.5 per mille is estimated to save about 1 000 lives. unfortunately, the commission has withdrawn its old proposal for a directive - the one which parliament supported in 1997 and which the committee still supports. the commission has said that the bottling plant is a matter of subsidiarity. but how can that be? it cannot be less dangerous to drive with alcohol in the blood in the united kingdom, italy, ireland and luxembourg, where the limit is 0.8 per mille, than it is in the rest of europe. we have expertise in the field of road safety in the eu. i think we should use it in the very areas where it has real effect, and it has in the area of alcohol and driving. i expect the council and the swedish presidency to take action here. i cannot see how we can be credible as european politicians if on the one hand we say that road safety is important and on the other hand we are not prepared to take important decisions. i understand that the commission has come today with a recommendation of 0.5 per mille but i must say that i think it is remarkable that it comes today, just when parliament is due to debate the matter and before parliament has voted. the commission should therefore have waited for parliament's debate. in addition to this and safer car fronts, the report contains a number of other important proposals for road safety measures. i will raise issues such as daylight saving, driver training and telematics systems. finally, a question which i consider to be of the utmost importance, but which unfortunately was not dealt with in committee, is the need for better working conditions for professional drivers. mr president, i would like to thank you. the european parliament has always been a force for improving road safety and we must continue to do so. the lives and health of many people, but especially young europeans, are at stake.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur for her great commitment to this issue. last year, almost 43 000 people were killed on the eu's road network. one of the main causes of this is the use of alcohol. you can never, ever get away from that. six years in the european parliament have given me great respect for the fact that we have very different views on many issues, but we should all agree that alcohol and driving should never, ever go together. yet, people are killed every day on the eu's road network because of this combination. we can also have very different views on whether we should have common rules within the eu or whether each country should solve this problem by itself. let me point out two things to anyone who is hesitant to ask this question: one is that no eu country would accept that another member state tolerated an airline captain flying a plane with 300 passengers, a sea captain responsible for the safety of 1,000 people or a train driver drinking alcohol before taking charge of his or her passengers. why, then, do we not make the same demands of the person who drives on our roads? the second point i want to make is that 95 percent of the traffic accidents that lead to someone dying actually happen on the roads. i only wish that we would take the problem of these 95% as seriously as we take the problem of the 5% of accidents that occur under the other transport laws. that is why i think the report is a good basis for further work from outside, and that is why i will also vote in favour of it tomorrow.

mr president, i would like to thank you. in the women's history encyclopedia, under the heading patriarchy, it is written: social systems based on hierarchical relationships between men, structured in such a way that decision-making processes are controlled from above and that women as a gender are given a subordinate position. the ideological justification is provided by a value system designed by male thinkers and based on men's understanding of reality. the material basis of the system is a division of labour by sex, which at all levels of the hierarchies assigns the unpaid or less paid tasks to women. one prerequisite for this is control of women's sexuality and fertility. this control in turn requires both structural violence against women and self-oppression by women". mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to return to the question of how we exercise self-repression. it is not only in this house that representatives of the epp in the first place - it is also happening far out in the social circles - discuss quotas and have a panic fear of gender quotas, for some reason unfounded to me. they say that women should be given credit for their work, but i would like to ask the question that obviously employers and top male politicians do not usually ask: what merits are we looking for? we know that women and men today are very much different parts of a whole in society. we in the united left have no problem whatsoever with supporting karamanou's report one hundred per cent. we're waiting for the ketchup effect. now we have waited 150 years, so we don't want to wait even two more years for something to happen.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i want to thank you for a good report. the equal opportunities for men and women to participate in decision-making at all levels should be taken for granted by everyone, but unfortunately this is not the case. what can be done? we need greater equality in the home, access to good childcare, shared parenting, better incentives in education systems to break down traditional gender roles and flexible working hours to meet individual needs. we also need ways of working in politics, business and the world of organisations that are not based on men's priorities. although politics is still too much a man's world - in some countries more so than in others - the problems are even greater in business and trade union organisations. this is unacceptable and must be given increasing attention. gender equality is not a women's issue and can only be achieved through cooperation between both sexes. therefore, men must be involved in the work of gender equality in a completely different way than today. among other things, more men should be active in the work on gender equality in this parliament. we need more women in decision-making positions, but - and i agree with the theory of - not because they are of a certain gender, but because it is unacceptable that so much competence and experience is lost.

united states, and a wto panel has been set up, saying that our evidence was insufficient. this was the background to the commission's evaluation of the hormones used. there are six of them. according to that evaluation, there were different levels of risk. all hormones carry certain risks; above all, they are carcinogenic. one hormone, estradiol 17-beta, was worse than the others. the commission is therefore now proposing a legislative amendment to prohibit completely the use of 17-β-oestradiol, both for feed and growth promotion purposes and for therapeutic purposes. for the other five hormones, it is proposed to extend the provisional, temporary ban in force. mr president, i would like to thank you. i and the committee on the environment fully support the commission's positions. it is very urgent to move forward with this. of course, it is also important to continue to examine the five other hormones to see how dangerous they may be, but it is positive that estradiol 17β is now definitively banned. so this study has given us a clearer argument, which hopefully means that those who have a different view will respect our position, our prohibition and the import ban currently in force. the arguments put forward are a little different and i would like to mention here some of the arguments which i have put forward in the report and which have been accepted by the committee on the environment. they may not be so heavy legally, but they are very heavy politically. on the one hand, there is the environmental argument. it's dangerous to use hormones in this way. in the united states, where they are used, they have been found to spread to the environment outside of animal production. on the one hand, it is for animal welfare reasons. we must consider whether it is reasonable to give animals supplementary feed which means that the animals grow abnormally. is this not a stress that brings us into conflict with animal welfare and the ambition to treat animals well? in general, in such contexts we should assess the ethical reasons. this is becoming increasingly important in a society where knowledge is increasing and where science is finding out more and more about different production methods. therefore, we must consider whether this is in accordance with the basic ethical rules that we humans have. the question is, does such a far-reaching change change the very conditions of life for humans or other biological creatures? we probably shouldn't have that right. mr president, i would like to thank you. i welcome the amendments that have been - mr. speaker, i have to go. in an ever-expanding internal market, differences in systems are becoming more and more of a problem. in the railway sector, the 15 eu member states have 4 different electrical systems and 15 different signalling systems. in 10 years' time, we may have 25 or 27 member states. the differences in systems will then probably be even greater. this is not a problem that will be solved simply because we come up with new and seemingly modern solutions. since we inaugurated europe's longest combined bridge and tunnel linking sweden and denmark this summer, one might think that the problem of lack of interoperability was also solved, but it is just the opposite. there is one signalling system in denmark and a completely different one in sweden. to make it really awkward, we have left-hand traffic on the rails in sweden, while the danes drive on the right. there are various reasons why there are actually such great differences between the countries of europe, and they all have to do with history. one is that european countries have electrified at slightly different times. another is deliberate - it would never be easy to drive a train straight into another country. in a europe today where we are talking instead about the four freedoms and where people, goods, capital and services are to be able to move freely across all borders, any obstacle becomes a problem, but obstacles of various kinds need to be removed. we need to harmonize and become, as they say, interoperable in different areas. the commission's initiative to introduce the same system in the railway sector is therefore an important step. the first step is obviously the transeuropean railway systems. it is important that this work be characterized by two insights. the first realization is that this actually has to take some time. it will cost considerable sums, especially if we eventually also have to move from the transeuropean networks to the other networks. in addition, we must, in the long term, do the same with the countries of central and eastern europe. the second insight is that solutions can be found, at least in the short term, without having the same system in all countries. the fact that trains can already cross borders in europe shows that this is possible, and it works because new technologies can also be built into the locomotive.

altered by the clear improvements brought about by directive 90/220 which we are now discussing. in the conciliation, we in the gue group, together with the greens, have fought for the improvements that have actually been made. this includes phasing out and antibiotic resistance markers, greater transparency and better risk assessments. we have also achieved at least half a victory in the area of public records of crops, but there is still a great deal of uncertainty about whether member states really see themselves obliged to make public records of all commercial crops. the council of ministers and the commission must clarify this. it is not good to adopt laws which are subject to different interpretations from the outset. we have not yet seen the new rules on labelling, traceability and liability that the commission has promised us. until we know what they look like and until they are fully implemented, we cannot consider lifting the moratorium. we do not believe in place is, in our view, reprehensible and contrary to the precautionary principle. it is also strange that in this way parliament and the council of ministers are trying to pre-empt the debate. the risks of gm crops. this applies both to the risk of spread to other species and to possible effects on human health. in such a situation, forcing new commercial crops is foolhardy and will lead to mr president, commissioner verheugen and mr danielsson, representing the presidency! turkey is the key to the establishment of good relations between the christian and muslim worlds. this fact can never be underestimated, not least in view of the fact that turkey is officially a secular state, while its citizens are overwhelmingly muslim. at the same time, modern-day turkey is also the second bible land where the apostle paul established his first christian congregations. my first question to the commissioner and to the council is therefore: in what way are you prepared to contribute to promoting religious dialogue between christians and muslims? my second question is: what is being done to strengthen religious freedom in turkey, throughout the middle east and in north africa? many of us wonder, not least the millions of coptic christians in egypt. i would like to mention a specific case in turkey. on 22 february, the trial of syrian christian priest yusuf akbulut, which began last december, will continue before the state security court in diyarbakir. the charges relate to section 312 of the criminal code, which was used to convict several politicians and writers, including yasar kemal. it forbids acts that "openly incite and stir up the people" to hostility and hatred by pointing to differences in religion, race, social class, or geographical area. what yusuf akbulut has done is to be quoted in the huriyet newspaper on 3 october 2000 as welcoming the recognition by the us congress of the 1915-1918 genocide of armenians. in this context, he added the fact that other christian peoples, such as assyrians/syriacs, were also murdered during the genocide. for these allegations, he faces several years in prison. the trial raises a number of issues ranging from the unreasonableness of such a diffuse and for freedom of expression and opinion particularly questionable paragraph as section 312 of the criminal code to the testimony and background of the prosecution. we in the christian-democratic parliamentary group of the epp-de are following the case. my third and final question to the commissioner and to the swedish presidency is as follows: are you prepared to keep a close eye on the yusuf akbulut case?
mr president, i would like to thank you. sometimes in the debate in my country it is said that the eu cannot be changed, but i think that the political agenda we are discussing at the moment shows that it can be changed. in 1995, when i was new to this parliament, we discussed almost exclusively stabilization policy. that area still exists, but the stabilisation policy has been supplemented by issues of growth, employment and social welfare. it is a changed reality that has made this possible, but it is also a matter of political priorities. this has been done via amsterdam and lisbon and is now continuing in stockholm. it is sometimes said that the issues we are dealing with today - employment and social welfare - are purely national. yes, for the most part, they are local, regional or national issues. but by setting common objectives to meet common challenges in the future, we are creating added value through european policy. we do this through pure legislation in areas such as health and safety at work and labour law, and through the open method of coordination, which means that we set common objectives on various issues. the process of monetary union and the objectives for inflation, interest rate developments and budget deficits have shown that this was a successful path. now we are doing the same thing with employment levels, exclusion and demographic issues. the lisbon process has three components: economic policy, employment policy and social policy/social cohesion. it is important to strike a balance between these three areas, all of which are equally important in the process. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr bullmann, for pointing out in his report this balance and the importance of maintaining it. i would like to mention two points in particular: education policy in the knowledge society, which is extremely important, and the importance of tackling the demographic challenges already now, as has been said. in stockholm we'll focus, because we can't do everything at once. so let us focus on some key issues to start the process! indicators are not an end in themselves, but merely a means to achieve the objectives set. i would also like to emphasize the role of parliament. we have a role in legislation but we have no clear role in the open method of coordination. now that this is becoming more and more important, we must have an institutional agreement in which the role of parliament is strengthened. however, one ingredient is missing here - we mention it in our report and hope that the gothenburg summit will take up the same aspect - namely that ecological aspects should also be linked to this process and to these issues.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the lisbon meeting sent a clear message to the people of the eu: europe will become the most competitive economy in the world. this is a real ambition which will now be followed up effectively in stockholm. but it must not be as in so many other eu documents - words without concrete results. we need concrete proposals now! the 14 million unemployed have had enough of these grandiose resolutions. i must admit that we liberals are taking a somewhat wait-and-see attitude until we see what the results of the stockholm meeting will be. i would like to stress, however, that bullmann has done a good job, but the report has become very comprehensive and the number of proposals has caused the focus to be lost. we would have preferred a concentration and a clear list of priorities. we fully support the commission's criticism of the slow pace of reform in europe. in this context, the council in particular deserves criticism, but indeed parliament too. let me highlight some of the key measures to give europe a boost: further liberalisation is needed in the energy markets, the postal and telecommunications sectors - an area where danielsson can show its very best side. the commission has also proposed a reduction in taxes, particularly on labour, better conditions for smes and a more flexible labour market. in addition, we need to create a single financial market - an area where so far far far too little has happened - and a connected e-europe. furthermore, biotechnology needs to be stimulated. a more liberal europe means a more cohesive europe and a more secure social europe, which in turn means that people are more willing to take risks and to try new things. europe needs a policy where jobs are created and where citizens can determine their future through their own choices. it is good to set objectives and to have continuous reconciliation and relevant statistical indicators, but this is never a substitute for concrete proposals. as a swede, i would like to hope that the stockholm meeting will be the turning point, that decisions will be taken there that will pave the way for a competitive and liberal europe. finally, i would like to say to mr danielsson that a dynamic europe also requires a common currency. don't forget this one! more and more people are wondering when sweden will meet its commitments.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i agree with mr bullmann when he stresses that there are a number of positive signals from the lisbon summit. i am thinking in particular of the call for full employment, but also of the emphasis on dynamic growth, innovation and lifelong learning. i wonder, however, how this relates to the maastricht model's emphasis on stability, inflation control, low-wage policies and social savings. is this policy of the swing lane abolished by the lisbon decisions? no, it's obviously not. the commission's recent action against ireland shows that the maastricht model is still alive. ireland is in many ways a model example of the plant protection policy that the lisbon summit is intended to promote, and which is now being punished by the eu. my conclusion is therefore that the eu has two conflicting economic models: one that is still a political reality and one that is about dreams and visions of what will come after this. there is a 'left-wing dream' and there is a 'right-wing dream', both of which seem to fit within this lisbon concept. i hope, of course, that the swedish government at the stockholm summit will emphasize and give priority to the left-wing model which stresses the importance of full employment and a socially responsible development policy and rejects the tendencies towards liberalization, deregulation, flexibilization, etc. but we'll see what happens when we get to stockholm.

mr president, i would like to thank you. we all remember the ghastly container with 58 dead chinese found in dover last summer. recently, we saw drowned people wash ashore on the spanish coast. such tragedies occur every day all over europe, though not always in such spectacular forms. the leagues that profit from exploiting the desperate inclinations of people and smuggling them across borders under terrible security conditions must of course be punished. this is an inhuman activity. this requires joint action at eu level. but, mr president, the initiative proposed by france only addresses a small part of these extremely complex issues of migration and refugees. despite beautiful declarations of global responsibility, eu member states have not been able to agree on a common asylum and migration policy. the only thing they seem to agree on is repressive measures. the smugglers will of course be punished, and airlines that take on board people without valid documents will be fined. it is unreasonable to leave it to flight attendants to assess people's protection needs. you also have to ask why people would risk everything they own to be smuggled into an unknown country under uncertain conditions. is it really possible to enter the eu legally when we require visas from 130 countries? i don't think it was. the eu is building a fortress europe. the liberal group would have liked to see a more comprehensive programme on migration issues. this calls for a common policy. the report drawn up by the rapporteur is an important modification of the french proposal, as it attempts to take account of the nuances. the leagues will certainly be punished, but there are people who are hiding refugees for no profit, because they are convinced that the refugees are risking their lives and limbs if they are sent back to their home countries. relatives, friends or individual organizations should not be punished for this. you have to make a distinction. it is also important to stress that the purpose of this proposal for a directive is not to make it more difficult for people to seek asylum or immigrate to the european union. with the amendments tabled by the liberal group, we will support ceyhun's proposal in the hope that the council will meet and agree on a common european strategy on asylum and migration.
in writing. - (sv) the proposal for a council directive on assistance for illegal entry, movement and residence is another proposal in the legal arsenal of repressive measures. although member states have agreed to establish a common migration and asylum policy, every effort is being made to table proposals that are clearly aimed at making it more difficult for people to enter the european union, whether they are refugees or jobseekers. many europeans choose to hide people, often unknown to them, who have been smuggled or have managed to cross the eu border on their own. people who act, without any profit motive, for people who fear for their lives, should not be punished. this kind of humanitarian aid is perfectly legal today, in sweden among others, and should not be criminalised. europe needs a common migration and asylum policy with a humanistic approach. we have a duty to help people who come here seeking refuge, whether they are refugees from war zones or asylum seekers fleeing political repression. europe must also change its attitude to legal immigration and open its borders to third country nationals wishing to find work in the union. the basis is that people must be able to travel freely within the union without being stopped by a restrictive visa and work permit policy. a positive policy towards asylum seekers and immigrants will help us to tackle organised crime and the human tragedies of smugglers.

(sv) madam president, the president of the commission has said that 'we are afraid of being able to punish even the best pupils in the classroom', as was the explanation on the serious representation of the eu in ireland. actually, this is a curious idea. serious but natural is the best student in the classroom as a model - when you're in school, you're like that. the irish economy is performing excellently. last year, growth was 10 per cent, unemployment was below 4 per cent and, in addition, the country had a considerable budget surplus. a poor man has been transformed into a celtic tiger. a warning from the eu to ireland is, in addition to being hasty, because it ignores a situation in the country. ireland is a small country dependent on external trade, whose economy is closely linked to that of britain and the united states. for this reason, the fall in the exchange rate of the euro affected irish inflation more than that of other eu countries. inflation fell from 6% in november to 3.9% in january, and all indications are that it will continue to decline. the netherlands currently has a higher rate of inflation. as the commissioner himself recognised, a policy for ireland does not jeopardise the functioning of the emu. it is a risk referred to in article 99 of the treaty and therefore an argument for the ground. ireland accounted for only 1% of the eu's total gdp. for ireland, the threat of inflation comes mainly from the outside, but the eu and the commission require internal measures to reduce inflation. the eu's attitude is politically insensitive and does not contribute to strengthening confidence in the european union and emu, mainly in the countries that are forging monetary cooperation. the hostess asked mr solbes mira: why are you not in ireland?
(sv) madam president, ladies and gentlemen, a criticism of the union's economic policy in ireland is controversial. on my part, i believe that there are good reasons for supporting the recommendation of the ecofin council. as the guidelines of economic policy, around which all the eu member states are united, their objective is to prevent us from falling back into runaway inflation and our catastrophic budget deficits of the 1980s, which brought with them a backlash, a crisis and unemployment. ireland is the first member state to begin to seriously fall short of its inflation targets. having regard to the background to the crisis of the 1990s, the eu's reaction is understandable. the situation in europe is now improving. nobody is minimizing the fantastic growth of ireland. on the contrary, they constitute one of the best examples of how community cooperation can really contribute to significantly improving conditions in a member state. but we cannot forget the experience of the crisis of the 1990s. we cannot hesitate to believe that the fight for growth and full employment can and must be accompanied by a clear responsibility for price stability and budgetary equilibrium. why can't ireland go its own way? indeed, the problem is that we live in an internationalised and interdependent economy, in which the evolution of one country influences the evolution of another. while for just a small country to violate the directives that it itself has approved, a problem can seem small. but there are worrying signs that other countries may be doing so, which is why it is important to give the alert a flag. no meu país, a suécia, there are those who seek to use a criticism of ireland as an argument in anti-european propaganda. i'm not home! we are entering emu, we are losing our economic independence. the eu claims to have the power to decide on our taxes. i consider this propaganda to be absurd. the criticism of ireland is not that of the tax cut, but that the cut is taking place in a situation which could contribute to an overheating of the economy and to a reversal of inflation. a eu made a recommendation - there is no instrument of power to control ireland's fiscal and budgetary policy; that is, despite everything that the swedish opponents of the european union say.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the bse crisis shows, among other things, how quickly trust can disappear. it's going to take time to restore trust. one reason is that bse is only the latest to have occurred. it is also about long-distance transport of live animals and many other things that need to change as part of a change in the whole of the eu's common agricultural policy. for all these reasons, far-reaching measures will be required, which may have a very long-term impact and at several levels, if confidence is to be restored. on the whole, i think the commission's proposal is a good one. it's dramatic, but it's still sober and balanced. above all, it is necessary. the commission is talking about reducing production and thereby avoiding overproduction and meat mountains. it will require a transitional period, but the ambition is right and marks the beginning of acceptance of more market-based economic principles. the commission also talks of a review of the cap in 2002. i believe that this work should be able to start as early as this year. the precondition for the eu to come forward must be changes to the cap, although this will of course require a few years of transition. we can no longer produce for the meat mountains, but we must produce for consumption. the question is whether the commission's proposal is sufficient to restore confidence - probably not. that said, more will need to be done. i think that some of the points in the commission's proposal are not so good. this is the cost-sharing between the eu and the member states - currently 70% for the eu and 30% for the member states. this is part of the legal basis but it is something that must be changed as soon as possible. instead, the costs should be allocated on a 50-50 basis for at least two reasons. on the one hand, it is a question of budgetary reasons, to create the possibility of completing this whole operation. more than 40% of the eu budget is already spent on agriculture. it's not possible to go beyond this level. on the one hand, it is actually reasonable to have a different distribution. we have long known about the problems and their causes. yet, different countries have done very different things, and those who invested to avoid this crisis have borne the cost of it themselves. it is reasonable that those who have done little, and perhaps in some cases nothing at all, are now themselves bearing a greater share of the costs necessary to deal with this crisis.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to congratulate mr gemelli on his report. the commission's proposal represents an important step forward in presenting a more focused programme of development aid. the activities can only be effective if they are more concentrated and result-oriented. the six priority areas identified in the commission's proposal are well chosen. the resolution also contains a number of important additions and comments. parliament stresses the environmental aspects of all development more than the commission proposal does. it stressed, inter alia, that a large proportion of the rural poor majority is highly dependent on the natural resource base and that strengthening natural resources, forests, pastures, agricultural land and water resources must therefore be an important component of any poverty reduction strategy. another important comment from parliament concerns the commission's fifth priority, transport. the parliament's comment on this is important, namely that the focus should be broader and thus not just on roads but also include access to information and communication technologies and modern energy carriers. the poor countries were never given any real chance to participate seriously in the development of industrial society. the risk of large regions being left out of the new economy emerging around information and communication technologies is now high. helping to develop capacity around these technologies must be an important component of any development strategy. in general, there is a tendency to take technological issues too lightly in poverty work. i hope that the commission will take constructively to the various recommendations in the gemelli report, particularly on those points where we point out the need to look at the priorities more broadly. in any event, we shall have the opportunity to return to these questions in connection with the budget.
in writing. - (sv) the european union's strict visa policy is a major obstacle for people wishing to travel to europe. the tightening of visa requirements in recent years has inevitably led to an increase in human trafficking and so-called illegal immigration to the member states of the european union. people who have entered the european union through trafficking networks, or by any other means, have often experienced tragic situations along the way. once they arrive in the union, they are deprived of all rights and social protection. the list of countries whose nationals require visas to enter the eu includes more than 130 states. the countries which are exempt from this obligation are not even among the 50. this practice, which makes the requirement to have a visa the rule rather than the exception, is not worthy of a democratic europe. the basic rule must be that people who wish to enter the union have the right to do so freely, and not the other way around. europe must change its attitude towards immigration. in a few years' time, the union will face a pressing problem of labour shortages. it is therefore important that we accept and welcome people who are seeking a better future in the eu member states. a more generous visa policy is also a necessary condition for us to be able to pursue a common humane policy towards refugees and asylum seekers. a policy that accepts immigration can help us to tackle organized crime and the human tragedies caused by smuggling networks.
in writing. - (sv) i have today voted in favour of the haug report on the proposal for supplementary and amending budget no 1/2001 presented by the council, particularly in the wake of the bse crisis. this is despite the fact that the other swedish social democrats intend to vote against the proposal. this difference in attitude is not due to opposing positions on agricultural policy. on the contrary, we fully agree that radical reform is needed, including, in the long term, increased responsibility for the costs of bse and other crisis phenomena on the part of the member states. this is a view which is also reflected in the report. the reason for this difference in vote is rather that i was involved in the work on this matter in the committee on budgets and i see no other way than today's negative vote to influence agricultural policy. the additional budget does not mean that we will go beyond the budgetary framework, but that the margins provided for by respecting the ceiling of this budget will be reduced. it is also a matter on which parliament has no right to co-decide - which is itself questionable and is called into question in the report. a negative vote does not therefore result in any real change today, but only in a delay. the 'no' vote will therefore delay the provision of the resources needed to carry out the tests and eliminate the sick animals in the context of the bse crisis, as will the second part of this supplementary and amending budget, namely the extension to montenegro of the mandate of the european agency for reconstruction in kosovo/serbia. in its report parliament sets out specific requirements for a reform of the agricultural policy. these demands are in line with the view expressed by my social democratic and liberal colleagues and myself in an article in the göteborgs-posten last wednesday. parliament will now make a strong demand for this, and all swedish social democrats are very happy about that. so this is not a fundamental difference in our view of agricultural policy. on the contrary, i am in full sympathy with the action of my swedish social democratic colleagues. we simply have different views on the effect of voting against this particular report.
in writing. -the report discusses the budgetary consequences of the bse crisis. the tests, market interventions and the purchase and destruction scheme for untested bovine animals over 30 months old will be co-financed by the eu and the member states. the report rightly stresses the importance of reforming the common agricultural policy to achieve 'environmentally, economically and socially sustainable agriculture in the sense of agenda 21'. it also stressed the responsibility of the member states in dealing with the bse problem and the need to consider alternative financing methods in the event of an outbreak. it is high time to move away from agricultural production which is aimed solely at generating the greatest possible profits and which does not take sufficient account of the quality of the products and the interests of consumers. swedish agriculture has not yet been affected by the bse crisis, which is clearly due to sound animal husbandry and feeding practices. we believe that the member states must take on a larger share of this problem, which is largely due to shortcomings in national legislation. we therefore support amendments nos 4 and 5 tabled by mr mulder, which provide for an equal (50-50) distribution of the compensation paid to farmers in connection with the bse crisis between the eu and the member states. we also support amendments nos 1 to 3 tabled by mr graefe zu baringdorf, which stress the need for agricultural reforms and greater responsibility on the part of the individual member states. we support the report with these few reservations.
in writing. - (sv) we have voted against amendments nos 23, 25 and 53. our position towards the european union is fundamentally positive. as swedish liberals, we see european integration as an opportunity to solve problems that have an impact beyond our borders, such as those related to electronic networks and communication services. however, we also believe in the value of the principle of subsidiarity, which means that a decision should be taken at the level closest to the people it affects. we think it would be too far-fetched to ask member states to merge their national supervisory authorities and to require them to set up advisory offices for the public. it seems to us also quite un-liberal to force member states to make certain companies give up their rights of way to others at their request. we have voted against the three amendments in question, as we consider these to be matters of national concern.

in writing. - we voted against the kirkhope report on the liability of carriers and we call on france to withdraw its initiative on the harmonization of penalties for carriers bringing third-country nationals into the territory of the member states without the necessary documents. the council's initiative is one of those fragmented proposals whose sole purpose is to prevent people from travelling to the european union. we cannot accept that the responsibility for passport controls should be delegated to private transport companies. it is not wise for airport and port registration staff, air hostesses or drivers to take it upon themselves to decide who can enter the european union. this is precisely what will happen if the provisions of the schengen convention and the kirkhope proposal on sanctions are implemented. the right to asylum under the geneva convention must be a cornerstone, both in the context of schengen cooperation and in the common asylum and immigration policy of the european union. the sensitive sanctions proposed by the council represent a major threat to the right of asylum. the many people who are now fleeing war and oppression to the external border of the european union would never have been able to get here if new or stricter sanctions were imposed on the carriers. the european union must not build walls to the outside world to ensure freedom of movement within its territory. the european tradition of a generous refugee policy, in which the right to asylum is guaranteed, must characterize the continuing cooperation within the framework of schengen and the common policy on immigration and asylum.
in writing. -{sv) we swedish christian democrats, while recognising the problematic nature of the proposal, nevertheless choose to refer it to the council and the commission, asking them to draw up a proposal which includes more than a general solution concerning the european union's asylum policy. the current proposal is problematic because the examination of the asylum application risks being left to the carriers, without the carriers having the necessary knowledge or administrative competence. the right to claim asylum may therefore be impaired, especially given that refugees often have difficulties in obtaining a passport and a visa.
in writing. -(sv) a common policy on the temporary protection of expelled persons is positive, especially to ensure that all member states follow up and take care of people forced to flee war-torn regions. it is important, however, that this temporary protection, which is valid for a maximum of two years, does not imply a threat to the right to seek asylum. the right of asylum under the geneva convention, and therefore the right of permanent residence, must always be protected. provisional protection in the member states should not prevent individuals from applying for asylum upon arrival in the member state responsible or during the period of validity of such protection.

- mr. speaker, i have to go. i would like to begin by congratulating the president on having managed the opening of this debate so well that we actually have a document to discuss at all. but i would also like to express my disappointment that members of this parliament, especially members of the committee on the environment, do not have the capacity to accept the democratic process which began three years ago and which is now to be completed, but are trying at the last minute to destroy this document. i would also like to thank the rapporteur for the work she has done, even though i have not always understood the path she has chosen, how she has come about and why the ambition seems to have fallen below the level it was at the beginning. we know that our use of fossil fuels is not a sustainable use of energy. that's something we all agree on, actually. we have known about this problem for at least 30 years and now it is time to do something about it. i've said it before and i'll say it again: parliament gave its support to an acidification strategy three years ago. now is the time to make this strategy a reality. that is what this directive is about. it is not just about acidification in sweden, norway or denmark. it is about half a million people who die every year in different parts of the world because of the use of fossil fuels. this is all those who suffer from chronic diseases, such as chronic bronchitis, due to the burning of fossil fuels. it is therefore not just a question of tightening up the limit values, but also of the health of our citizens. this is something that members should not forget. it is indeed possible to maintain a good level of energy service and at the same time work towards a better environment. we're on our way. we have the chance to set the pace if 314 members have the courage to take a stand tomorrow in favour of the more ambitious alternative. it is hardly surprising that the council is putting forward a compromise in a text which is more watered down than the one parliament approved at its first reading and had wished to see. it is also clear that we should make the same demands again at second reading in order to put pressure on the council and secure a good negotiating position in the inevitable conciliation. we know that we must act today if we are to see any results in the foreseeable future. we must dare to set strict limits for all types of installations, old and new, large and small. the council's limits do not go as far. the council seems to have lost faith that we can develop new technology at all. it is essential that the existing old installations are covered by this directive and that they are also subject to strict limit values. nothing would go as planned if too many exemptions were granted. nothing would remain of the high ambitions. therefore, small installations must also be covered by the directive. it is also important to point out that the directive sets a framework for the liberalisation of the energy market that we are witnessing today. without such a framework, the energy market in europe is in total anarchy. minimum environmental requirements are necessary to create common rules of the game. the requirements we now place on ourselves in the union are also requirements for future members of the union. today and tomorrow we have the opportunity not only to stop dangerous emissions but also to improve public health and lay the foundations for a successful enlargement of the union in the future.
thank you, mr. speaker. much of what was said in the previous debate is also highly relevant to this directive. these two documents form the basis of the acidification strategy we adopted three years ago. when measurements were taken monday in my own home, the acidity level was higher than it had been for a very long time. acidification is still a problem that we cannot help but notice. i would like to begin by thanking mr myller, who has done an incredibly solid job, and the colleagues in the environment committee, who have helped to bring us a proposal that now looks really good. the problem is that the path between committee and plenary in this parliament is sometimes quite bumpy. the closer we get to the day of decision, the more fearful some of us seem to become. once again we see the conflict between industry, employment and the environment emerge. many former environmentalists are turning into national and regional conservationists. they're embracing their old coal-fired power plants back home. the responsibility and the long-term vision of how we can have a good living environment also in the future are suddenly gone. at the first reading in parliament we supported the emission ceilings proposed by the commission. in this context it should also be noted that the ceilings and figures presented by the commission were in fact based on the countries' own reporting to the commission. there were no numbers that were plucked from the air or made up. some today seem to deny these figures. the figures were not overly optimistic and it also turned out, as myller said earlier, that the economic calculations were in fact in line with reality. when the council then comes forward with its views, it is not entirely unexpected that the result will be much smaller, very close to the figures of the gothenburg protocol. in this context, i would also like to recall that the commission - - mr. speaker, i have to go. the acidification of forests, soil and waterways is the single biggest environmental problem in my country. almost all acid rain comes from the combustion of fossil fuels used in energy production in other european countries. acidification is thus an 'imported' problem in our country. the natural environment in large parts of the northern eu has a low buffering capacity. therefore, insurance losses are very harmful to denmark, sweden and finland. great efforts are made annually to reduce the damage by calcifying lakes and streams. without having access to the exact figures, i would venture to say that over the years sweden has invested more in reducing acidification in economic terms than some member states have invested in reducing acidifying emissions. in some areas, the sensitivity to acid rain is extremely high, such as in southwestern sweden, in some mountainous areas of northern sweden and, not least, in southern norway. i am addressing all of you, members of parliament from different member states. to save valuable wildlife in these areas, we need your help. we appeal to you to put the matter in a european perspective and help us with the major acidification problems. reducing emissions from combustion is also a major health issue in several eu countries. many environmentalists in my country put their hopes in the european union. the un has proved to be a poor organization for pursuing european environmental problems. i believe that taxation is a national issue. national parliaments must choose the best path forward to meet the proposed national emission ceilings. i cannot therefore support the proposal for eu taxes on emissions. i would also like to comment on the unemployment argument. overall, good environmental and health conditions do not lead to unemployment. in the long run, efforts to improve the environment and health are a good investment. costs are reduced and the efforts are leading to an improved economic situation. the situation in russia today would be much better if it did not have to inherit the environmental disaster and the ill health that the collapsed communist system left will not be followed at all. parliament should therefore re-adopt the level of ambition against acidification set by the commission in the so-called emission ceilings. with parliament and the
