achieve results in this important environmental field.
the rapporteur. - mr. speaker, i have to go. the commission's proposal for an action plan to increase energy efficiency is welcomed. at the same time, as rapporteur, i can say that the plan in its original form has many shortcomings. i hope that the proposals contained in parliament's resolution will be a valuable addition and that mr de palacio, as the commissioner responsible, will adopt our proposals. the examination of the action plan has made it very clear how energy saving is neglected as a policy area and in society in general. many people are probably aware that we need to change the focus of energy policy, that we need to reduce our heavy dependence on fossil fuels. however, when asked how this will be done, the answers are usually few. some are proposing renewable energy. very few would say that the transition would be achieved, not least through more efficient use of energy. the reason, of course, is that knowledge of what can be done is lacking. furthermore, there is a lack of sufficient economic incentives to facilitate investment in more efficient energy use. one big problem is that the energy savings are invisible. when we invest in a wind farm, it is clear to everyone what the money is used for. if we invest the same amount of money in energy conservation, it's perceived as much more diffuse. i therefore hope that the proposal to make all the eu institutions, that is to say the buildings in which we operate, symbols of good administration, will be adopted. we could start with this house. the potential for energy saving and household use is very large within the eu. in my view, the commission is being too cautious in its assessments. at least 30 percent of today's energy use could be saved by rational behavior. if the environmental costs of energy production and consumption were also included in prices, the potential would be even greater. it is against this background that the report calls for the internalization of environmental costs and the tackling of the problem through various types of taxes, not least a tonne-kilometre tax on heavy trucks and a tax on bunker oils. similarly, it is important to promote fiscal incentives for energy-efficient vehicles and buildings, or equipment that contributes to better housekeeping. there is much to be done in this area to increase incentives for energy conservation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this is particularly important in the current situation of liberalisation of electricity markets. the liberalization is excellent, but it has led to such low prices that, for example, there have been problems with the backpressure. the report calls for a series of new directives to stimulate energy efficiency in various areas. this applies to the construction sector, cogeneration, various types of electronic equipment and the transport sector. another important proposal is to link intelligent energy use to the so-called lisbon process. the lisbon summit set the goal of making the eu the most competitive and productive economy in the world. such a programme is significantly delayed if environmental aspects are not covered. these could be subject to a requirement whereby energy efficiency increases annually within the union by, for example, 2,5%. even if environmental aspects were not taken into account, there is a strong case for putting energy efficiency on the agenda in the context of the lisbon process. a more efficient use of energy and natural resources would make an excellent contribution to the competitiveness and productivity of the union. mr president, i would like to thank you. we must not forget the outside world either. if these aspects are important to us, it is also important that they are brought to the fore in the discussions with the applicant countries in the enlargement process, which of course also applies to our assistance. in the course of the report, i have become aware of the importance of working longterm and systematically on these issues. that is why i have drawn up, together with three members, mcnally, turmes and van der laan, an initiative, 'energy intelligent europe', which has already received the support of many representatives from different political groups and member states. the aim is to stimulate the debate on energy efficiency in various ways and to propose initiatives that should be taken by the eu and the member states to make our use of energy more intelligent. it is therefore appropriate to return to these questions.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i've been looking at futurum, a website for debate and dialogue that just opened, and it's nice. this morning, 76 citizens from different countries had chosen to take part in the debate. of course, there could have been many more, but it's a good start. the question, of course, is what will happen to these debates. will they live their own lives until 2004, when the council will again be shutting itself behind closed doors in marathon negotiations on a new treaty? the foreign minister's speech indicated that this would not be the case. but it is important to tell citizens already now how these views will be taken up and channelled so that there is a real broad debate and not just empty words. i am sorry if we are too loud in this house, but this is really important for us across the group boundaries. the citizens, like us, would need to know how to channel this. will there be some kind of convention where we, as elected representatives at various levels, can really contribute to the formulation of the new treaty on the basis of these views?
mr president, i would like to thank you. i have two points of view. the first concerns the method: i think it is of central importance that the power over the treaties is vested in the national parliaments. if power is transferred to the european parliament, this will be a decisive step towards turning the eu into a state, which i am opposed to. i am therefore also sceptical about a convention method. if such a method is to be used, it will be a convention of national parliaments to discuss the treaty. my second point is about the content. i believe that it is not enough to discuss the points raised in nice. the debate should instead focus on a democratic reform of the european union, which would bring full transparency to legislative work and reduce the number of officials, especially in the commission. today, there is a unique situation where civil servants have the decisive influence over the content of legislation, which is a democratic problem.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the parliament's representative in nice summed up the result as a disaster. it is impossible to understand how one can, with calculated courage, incorporate a future conflict between large and small countries in the treaty. it is even more difficult to understand how the council can refuse to introduce a reference to the rights to which all the member states have previously subscribed. how can the citizens understand that their elected representatives do not want to affirm the rights they are supposed to stand for politically? a weak treaty could be a danger for an enlarged eu where many different legal traditions are to be brought together. we want to see greater ambition in reaching decisions on who is to do what and who is to decide what and how, and on rights and democracy. the debate with citizens must be directed towards the key issues for the future of a more mobile europe. it takes a democratic convention, not just a website, to create an open debate and open decision-making. the presidency gives the impression of avoiding the core issues of the european debate. this is tantamount to evading its european leadership responsibility. take the opportunity to contribute to a democratic europe with a democratic european parliament, which i hope the presidency will respect.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the lisbon decisions were special: on the one hand, they were characterised by a clear right-wing economic policy of deregulation and privatisation. on the other hand, they were characterised by a left-wing policy of full employment, which thus became for the first time an official objective of the eu. the social agenda, the social community, the fight against poverty, lifelong learning for all, etc. were all natural elements of a left-wing policy. i was quite surprised at the time. i wondered how one could at the same time reconcile or balance, as lindh said, right-wing and left-wing politics. the picture is now clearer and i see that the disagreement on economic policy persists. right-wing politics are clearly dominating the stockholm summit. stability policy, liberalisation and deregulation of the internal market are at the heart of the debate. the social profile is very weak. the stockholm meeting was to focus on employment, but there are no proposals for any significant measures to tackle the high level of unemployment which currently affects 14-15 million people. they want to empower women to enter the workforce. it's good, it's good. it also wants to increase employment for the elderly, raise the retirement age, etc. however, the proposals are primarily aimed at increasing the employment rate, not at reducing unemployment. that's two different things. the 15 million unemployed in europe today, who are already in the labour force, do not benefit much from the addition of new groups to this labour force. my group considers this very serious. the swedish government should of course also be concerned, but unlike france or portugal, it has chosen not to create its own profile, but instead to implement as loyally as possible what the commission and the large member states want. when the swedish government once led the campaign for sweden to join the eu, it was said that the objective was not only to preserve swedish welfare but also to pursue a social welfare policy throughout europe together with other left-wing forces. now it's the other way around. the right-wing policies are being promoted within the eu and swedish welfare policy is in danger. this is happening despite the good economy, despite a majority of left-wing governments in the council and despite a left-wing majority in the swedish parliament. even under optimal conditions, we have not come close to a social europe in the last year. what if the economic situation deteriorates and we get more bourgeois governments in the council? the swedish presidency has run out of time. in conclusion, i wonder if there really is nothing that the swedish government could give priority to at the forthcoming employment summit for those who are unemployed here and now.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the lisbon process is supposed to create the most dynamic economy in the world. open coordination has been chosen as the method, and i thought i would share my experience with you. sweden has participated in a number of summits and processes. since the essen summit in 1994, the importance of lower taxes on labour and greater flexibility has been discussed. in practice, sweden has done the opposite. as with its voluntary exclusion from the euro, it ignores what was agreed. instead, they increase the tax burden and continue to inhibit entrepreneurship. in stockholm, we should compare and learn from each other. what does it look like? yes, there is talk of an increased employment rate, but the truth in sweden is that despite three years of economic boom, more people are now out of the workforce than in the early 1990s, when we experienced the worst recession since world war ii. at the same time, the real unemployment is hidden in labour market policies and temporary mass education initiatives that do not lead to quality jobs. in addition, a shocking absence for sickness affects the statistics. if we add the latent jobseekers and the underemployed, unemployment in sweden is 17 per cent of the labour force compared with just over 5 per cent officially reported. the lisbon document talks a lot about knowledge. in terms of knowledge, sweden is in danger of melting down, despite all the talk about lifelong learning, because we are already missing the start. 34 percent of our high school kids are missing out on full grades in math, swedish and english. at the same time, the quality of education is being challenged as free schools and increased competition are leading to the opposite of the lisbon intentions. the swedish university is in crisis. sweden certainly has many people with a university degree, but it lacks people with real excellence. that's why the socialists are lowering taxes for wealthy immigrant specialists and raising taxes for resident low-educated swedes. talking and planning is one thing - especially if you get to do it in meeting rooms in other parts of europe - but implementing and changing requires more conviction. the commission is now calling for action, not least for national structural reforms and increased liberalisation at eu level. instead of making meaningless compromises and drawing up beautiful texts, i shall continue to criticise the european model, which has so far failed to create enough new jobs, but which condemns too many to longterm unemployment, exclusion, poverty and marginalisation.
in writing. - (sv) the council's compromise proposal is likely to result in an increase in emissions for the four forms of air pollution covered by the directive. for the european union as a whole, these ceilings are higher by 6% for sulphur dioxide, by 10% for nitrogen oxides, by 17% for volatile organic compounds and by 10% for ammonia. so far, no analysis has been made of the environmental consequences of the council's proposal, nor has there been any assessment of the cost or economic benefit. however, it is clear that this compromise will not reduce emissions sufficiently to meet the partial environmental target for 2010. the text of the so-called gothenburg protocol to the convention on transboundary air pollution was signed in december 1999, six months after the commission presented its proposal on national emission ceilings. all the member states have signed this protocol. in some cases, the ceilings indicated in the protocol are lower than those proposed by the commission. the council's compromise is therefore also, in some cases, below the values proposed by the commission and can therefore be said to be anything but ambitious. the levels of pollutants and critical levels of acidifying air pollution will therefore continue to be exceeded in large areas of the union, and a significant proportion of the eu population will remain exposed to pollution levels below who air quality guidelines. there is no justification for further lowering the level of eu ambition in this area by delaying the content of the directive as the council has just proposed. that is why we voted in favour of amendment no 10 and followed the line taken by the rapporteur.

the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. as the rapporteur points out, biotechnology offers enormous positive possibilities. this is likely to be most evident in the medical field, but opportunities exist in a number of other fields as well. however, biotechnology also raises a number of new and very difficult questions to which we must respond. these include ethical issues in the medical field, environmental and consumer issues in the cultivation of genetically modified crops, and global justice issues in the area of patent law and the dominance of large multinational companies in the industry. these issues must be taken very seriously if a credible overall policy is to be established in this area. the committee on the environment's opinion points to a few key areas. patent law must respect the united nations convention on biological diversity, which establishes the right of developing countries - indeed, all countries - to their genetic resources. there must be a strong independent and open public research in this area, so that research is also carried out on what is not of immediate commercial interest, as well as on the problems and risks of the technologies. it is also about transparency in research. as regards the controversial issue of the cultivation of genetically modified crops, it must be noted that the adoption of directive 90/220/eec does not mean that there is sufficient legislation in this area today. we still lack the necessary rules on labelling, traceability and liability. such rules must be a prerequisite for considering the introduction of large-scale commercial cultivation of genetically modified crops. we also point to the risks of not respecting genetic secrecy and of abusing the possibility of taking genetic fingerprints, for example by denying people employment or insurance. that's how inequality in society increases. we believe that it is crucial that these types of problems are also highlighted, precisely in order to develop the positive potential of biotechnology.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. purvis' report is an excellent document. there are great opportunities in biotechnology, but there are also considerable risks. we live in a democracy. it means that the fears and the agonies of our citizens must be taken seriously. we can only do this with a lot of open information and debate. however, we must not let these fears of the risks of the new technology overshadow its potential opportunities for man and the environment. they say we can reduce pesticides in agriculture. i would rather draw attention to the possibility of using biotechnology to design new pesticides and chemicals that work where they are supposed to work and break down when they are supposed to break down. we must have a truthful and open attitude to both opportunities and difficulties. in the field of food and agricultural production, we must have a profound respect for the citizens' self-evident right to information about the food they eat. fundamental to a democracy is the informed free choice for which the citizen is responsible. i am very pleased that paragraph 62 is included in the report, which deals with the protection of the individual's privacy in relation to the use of genetic information. properly used, biotechnology can contribute to ecological, economic and social growth. used incorrectly, it can be dangerous, like any other technology.

- mr. speaker, i have to go. ladies and gentlemen, the roth-behrendt report is excellent, and i welcome the limitation of animal testing. the commission is also proposing a total ban on the marketing of cosmetic products tested on animals, to take effect five years after the adoption of the directive, even if alternative test methods are not available. this line may risk temporarily limiting product development in the cosmetics industry, but it will greatly increase the incentives for the development of alternative testing methods that do not involve animal testing. the proposed animal testing ban is not enough, however, as the industry can simply place its experiments outside the eu. that is why the marketing ban is also necessary. such a ban prevents producers in non-eu countries from gaining market advantages by allowing them to continue using animal testing. on the contrary, competition would force the development of other methods. as a liberal, i have a strong sense of individual rights. i take the liberty of including the right of animals not to be subjected to unnecessary suffering. i also feel strongly about the consumer's free and informed choice. it is therefore important that citizens are given the opportunity to express their views on matters by means of detailed and accurate labelling. i would ask you, ladies and gentlemen, to pay particular attention to amendment no 39 in this connection, which proposes that animal-tested products should be labelled with the words 'tested on animals'. with a marketing ban and a ban on animal testing and the other measures that the environment committee has advocated, i think we are making good progress, which i am glad of.
- mr. speaker, i have to go. our group thinks that roth-behrendt has done a very good job. we support all the important parts of the report. we think that this question, in view of the main issue of animal testing, has been dragged out far too long. this decision to effectively ban animal testing for cosmetics should have been taken years ago. it is not justifiable - ethically and in the eyes of the voters - to test cosmetics on animals. it's partly a luxury management. nor is there any doubt about the general opinion on this matter. i also believe that if we are to force manufacturers to find alternative methods - they have had many years to start using such methods - then they must know that there is a fixed number of years before the ban comes into force. we know that alternative methods are coming in many areas, and that there are thousands of ingredients to choose from in cases where these methods are not fully developed. we therefore support a comprehensive ban on these tests. however, for such a prohibition to be effective, it must be combined with a marketing ban. only a combination of marketing and testing bans can be really effective, which is why we naturally support it too. as a counter-argument, we are told that the wto could object to this. we believe that this is not at all certain and that it is simply not acceptable that the wto should prevent the introduction of stricter animal welfare and consumer protection rules. that is what we must fight for: the right to stricter, more progressive legislation! - mr. speaker, i have to go. pvc has unique qualities as a plastic. it's strong and durable, which makes it suitable for a number of applications. however, pvc also has other characteristics, namely that it creates an unusually hazardous waste. chlorine is one of the main components, lead and cadmium are used as stabilizers, and phthalates are added to soften the material. this creates very system of responsibility for recycling and the costs of recycling must be introduced for manufacturers. dangerous stabilizers, heavy metals such as lead and cadmium, must be banned as soon as possible. it is important to combine this with a stop on imports in order to make the measure comprehensive. in this context, the question of voluntary agreements comes into play. a voluntary agreement cannot mean a stop to imports. this means that it is actually the most modern industry that benefits from having an import ban and legislation in this area, because they get protection for their more environmentally friendly production. it will be more efficient and more environmentally friendly than a voluntary agreement. we want the use of phthalates to be monitored and preferably mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for a good report and for the sensitivity she has shown in taking the arguments of the various political groups and listening to the disability movement and disability organisations. she has done an exemplary job as rapporteur. people with disabilities are discriminated against today. the free movement, which is the foundation of our cooperation, is much more difficult to exploit for people with disabilities when it comes to seeking employment and, for young people, studying in other countries. the discrimination in this area is clear. the same applies to the labour market. the unemployment rate among disabled people is very high. the access to information that we often discuss today is also more difficult for people with disabilities to achieve. we need to take an offensive stance. all of this is preventable. disability is not a static phenomenon. it's the environment that's broken, and it can be changed. today, for example, we have sign language interpretation. we can design buildings differently, we can design workplaces differently and make them more accessible to people with disabilities. this is possible because it is about changing the environment. we can change. moreover, the change must affect all policy areas. it is not just social policy, transport policy or labour market policy that can change, but all policy areas. in the labour market, where unemployment is about three times higher for disabled people, it is important that when creating indicators to improve the chances of different groups of people getting work - the elderly, women and others - we also include people with disabilities. many have said that a key element of integration is changing attitudes in society. i come from the school world myself. it is important that people with disabilities are not hidden away, but included in the school world so that everyone can receive education and training on equal terms and so that we see that people with different conditions can work together. it's very important to change attitudes. finally, it is important to integrate disability organisations and voluntary organisations into the work. let the year of disability be the starting point for an offensive in the formulation of new directives and a new action programme, so that we can have a europe without barriers and with equal opportunities for all.

mr president, i would like to thank you. in my view, the stockholm summit was focused on developing the internal market, that is, on the economic liberalisation mentioned earlier. social issues, especially unemployment, remained almost untouched. the european union is also committed to the development of a european employment strategy. however, there are two different forms of employment policy. the first is to increase the supply of labour by increasing the labour force, i.e. by increasing the employment rate, the second is to reduce the number of unemployed. these are two different policy areas. in stockholm you devoted all your efforts to expanding the workforce by bringing new groups into the labour market. this would be achieved by reducing the number of women who are pregnant at home in europe and by keeping older people in the labour market for a few more years. i am not in any way opposed to measures which increase the supply of labour, but no one can claim that they are of direct benefit to the unemployed. on the contrary, the 14-15 million unemployed are facing increased competition for jobs by the emergence of new groups, while their wage situation is being squeezed. it would have been possible to combine these two areas of employment policy, in which the aim is to increase overall employment and combat unemployment, by also proposing a reduction in the normal working time to 35 hours per week, or six hours per day. it has been argued that a reduction in working hours does not lead to new jobs, but this is not the case for low-wage jobs where there is a very clear link between reduced working hours and more jobs. furthermore, a reduction in working hours is a pleasure for all those who are already employed and makes it easier to combine work and family life. i am actually calling for action on the unemployed. it is not too late, as there are still a few months left of the swedish presidency. i would like to call for measures to be taken specifically for the unemployed and to help them here and now.

mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, let me thank the rapporteur for an excellent report. as a shadow rapporteur, it is always nice to have a good dialogue with a rapporteur who is receptive to the views and proposals being put forward. it is of course important to safeguard the interests of air passengers in this matter. i therefore think that the final result of the report is entirely positive. the amendments adopted by the committee are in no small part from the ppe-de group and have strengthened the position of passengers. we already have a regulation setting the limits of airline liability in the event of death or personal injury. this was a very important step in view of the level of the 1929 warsaw convention, which is definitely too low. the aim of the present proposal is to bring eu rules into line with the new montreal convention, which will hopefully enter into force at the end of 2002. the revised regulation will bring significantly improved protection for european air passengers. however, when they fly with foreign airlines, they may still face limited liability under the warsaw convention, as it will still apply to countries that have not ratified the montreal convention. this brings us to the constant difficulty, which always occurs in international conventions, that in a transitional period we will have two overlapping systems. it is therefore of the utmost importance that our member states ratify the montreal convention as soon as possible.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. in the public debate i often hear that it was the farmers who fed their cows to cows that had died by suicide. unfortunately, they did, but farmers had no way, at that time and with the system we have, of knowing exactly what they were feeding their animals. when we discuss the feed and food chain, we must ensure that the whole chain, from a to z, is permeated by a number of concepts: openness, transparency and information. we have a chain with many links and with large distances between each link. the same goes for feed. there is no reasonable possibility today for a normal farmer buying feed, whether that farmer has a small or a large operation, to know what is in it. he has no laboratory at his disposal, and with the present state of the feed, neither the appearance, smell, nor taste can give him any information about the contents. this requires much greater transparency and much more precise information for farmers. i think that this proposal is too complicated and that in the long term we should be able to find common principles for feed and food which are somewhat simpler and somewhat more understandable both for the farmer and for the final consumer of the food, such as a descending scale declaration of content, exact information on request. however, given the current situation and the fact that we are at the beginning of this food safety process, the eldr group will vote in favour of this report.

the council. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your contribution to the debate on the future of the european union. this is a day i never dreamed of, to stand before you at this crucial moment. the european parliament's plenary session today and tomorrow is an important event for the institutions of the european union and the member states, but above all it is an important event for the citizens of europe. at this meeting parliament has the opportunity to take a decisive step towards bringing the european union closer to its citizens. for the first time in the almost 50-year history of the union, it is within the bounds of possibility to adopt treaty-based rules on public access to parliament, council and commission documents. i would like to thank the european parliament for its energetic and persistent work to bring about new and better rules on transparency. during my visit, i have had the great pleasure of meeting several of you personally, and i know the great efforts many of you have made on this important issue. too many citizens in today's eu feel that the administration of the eu institutions is dusty, old-fashioned and over-bureaucratized. to tackle this, we need a modern, efficient and functioning union, where citizens' transparency and participation are the obvious foundation on which all work rests. there are various ways of improving the transparency of the eu institutions, but by far the best and most effective way is to have generous rules on access to documents in combination with open registers. such rules increase public confidence in the institutions and allow citizens to participate in the decision-making process. the air is cleared when everyone knows that all the documentation on the decision-making process is available for review, and usually also accessible. the european parliament early on stressed the importance of greater transparency in the eu institutions. in a resolution of january 1999, parliament described the importance of increased transparency as follows: the move towards greater openness and transparency is essential to the success of the future of the eu, as it helps to clarify the eu's political choices, promotes a wider and more balanced input into the political process, reduces the extent of corruption and abuse of power and generally promotes greater acceptance of eu decisions among european citizens.i am convinced that the european parliament's resolution set a standard for the implementation work now completed, which the commission was obliged to take into account when drafting its proposal for a regulation. for example, the parliament's statement made it practically impossible to exempt documents received by the institutions from the right of access. i am speaking here today as the representative of the council, but i am also the prime minister of the swedish government. when the treaty on the multi-country accession of sweden to the european union was signed in corfu in june 1994, the european council stated that the new member states were at the forefront of transparency and open governance and welcomed the additional stimulus that could come from the new member states in these respects. sweden has tried to live up to these hopes, for example by taking the initiative at the previous intergovernmental conference to inscribe in the eu treaty basic rules on openness and transparency. as the presidency of this six-month period, we have felt that we have an obligation to lead with full force the work of implementing the rules actually written into the treaty of amsterdam. the treaty of amsterdam established a right of access for citizens to european parliament, council and commission documents. this means that citizens have a treaty right to access these documents. the new rules now before parliament will have much greater stability and impact than the current rules, which the institutions can change or even abolish at any time by acting unilaterally. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. the work of the last few months has been very intense. all the parties involved have invested enormous time and resources in the work to establish good transparency rules. the representatives of parliament have, in particular, been vigorous and persistent in their efforts to bring about such rules. i would like to thank in particular the committee chairman, mr graham watson, the rapporteurs, mr michael cashman and mr hanja maij-weggen, and on behalf of the commission, mr michel barnier. there are several reasons why both parliament and the swedish presidency have pushed the negotiations so hard. first of all, there was a political commitment to be ready with the new transparency rules by may this year. it is therefore our duty to do everything possible to meet these expectations. in the end, the credibility of the eu is at stake. the council and parliament must show the citizens that efforts are really being made to bring about good rules within the promised time. secondly, we need new and better regulations. the rules which citizens have had to accept so far have a low standard status, since they are based on the internal rules of procedure of the institutions, and they have major shortcomings. for example, the rules do not cover documents submitted to the institutions: nor is there any right to extract parts of documents. of the institutions, only the council has so far set up a public register, and the current rules make it time-consuming to get documents out. another example of the shortcomings of the current rules is that the list of privacy grounds is not exhaustive. the proposal on which parliament has to give its opinion is a clear improvement on the current legal situation. it is a proposal that is timely. in many cases, documents will be available directly via the internet. this has long been a demand of the european parliament and this demand is now being met in the new proposal. documents previously available will also be made available on request. the time available to the institutions to respond to an application has been reduced. access shall be granted as soon as possible and at the latest within 15 working days. the institutions are obliged to provide the citizen with all the services he needs to access a document. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment from harmful substances. another improvement is that the new rules apply to all documents held by the institutions. no exception is made for certain categories of documents, such as documents relating to defence or security matters. this improvement is also the result of the initiative of the european parliament. of course, the new rules in this section are designed to guarantee confidentiality in cases where this is justified for defence or security reasons. the compromise text on which parliament is now to take a position is thus a step forward for the europe of the citizens and for democracy. the new rules will significantly improve citizens' access to eu documents. i am convinced that in the long term the new rules will also bring about significant positive changes in the institutional management culture and in the attitude of officials towards the public. the new rules will help the union to gain confidence and democratic legitimacy. access to the documents of the institutions is the lever needed to achieve these objectives. as the presidency, our task has not been to transfer to the institutions the national rules on the publication of documents, either in sweden or in other member states. rather, our task has been to blend the best of the different member states' management cultures and historical experiences, taking into account the institutions' own experiences and the need for special adaptations. we have succeeded in this in the council, together with the commission and representatives of parliament, and it is now up to parliament to finally adopt a position on a text which is the result of 15 months of intensive, hard and finally fruitful negotiations. however good the new rules may be, they must be gradually adapted to new requirements. it is therefore good that the european parliament, in the course of the negotiations, took the initiative of a provision on the review and evaluation of the new rules. the proposal requires the commission to present a detailed report on the practical operation of the new transparency rules by 31 january 2004 in good time for the next parliamentary elections . mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. anyone who has followed the dramatic negotiations of the last few weeks knows that the proposal for a regulation, which has been drawn up with the responsibility of all the institutions concerned, is balancing on a knife-edge. i am convinced that this plenary session can be hailed as the meeting at which parliament took advantage of a unique opportunity to bring the union closer to its citizens. it is a historic decision, also because it is not the member states in an intergovernmental conference but the institutions themselves that are now responsible for the future of the union. the very fact that the institutions were able to reach agreement on a particularly difficult issue, but one which was crucial for the citizens' confidence, and within the time promised by the treaty of amsterdam, gives hope for the future. the european parliament now has the final say. i am convinced that future generations of europeans will remember what happened on 2 and 3 may 2001.
commission. - mr president, madam president-in-office of the council, ladies and gentlemen, the establishment of clear standards for public access to documents is absolutely essential, not only for the proper functioning of the community institutions but also, as has just been said, to strengthen their democratic legitimacy vis-à-vis the citizens of the union. the commission therefore considers the draft regulation we are discussing today to be particularly important and significant. i am pleased to note that after many months of intensive work, the representatives of the three institutions have succeeded in producing a text which, in my view, is both clear and balanced, and which has received the assent of the committee on civil liberties of your assembly, the committee of permanent representatives of the member states and the commission itself. it is now up to your assembly to decide freely on the outcome of this work. at this point, mr president, i would like to thank very your colleagues - who have successfully tried to reconcile the views, and we know that this was not easy in the first place, and to make the draft regulation compatible with the different administrative traditions of the member states. as the rapporteurs have just pointed out, the draft regulation is a considerable step forward from current practice and thus brings the community system closer to that of the member states which are most advanced in terms of transparency. of course, ladies and gentlemen, the text is probably not perfect. likely, he will not be able to satisfy everyone fully in every respect. i have just heard mrs hautala use a symbolic expression about russian dolls. i do not think, madam rapporteur, that if you open all the boxes you will find mr solana. i think mr. solana has a much higher dimension and personality, much more important, than what you showed earlier. but the truth is, once you start opening the boxes or the russian dolls, and you realize there's something in there, then it's gonna be hard to stop. that's probably how your image is accurate and symbolic, on that point at least. in the commission's view, ladies and gentlemen, this text is in fact the balance between the specific characteristics of each institution and the practices of the member states. from this point of view, quite frankly, it is the best compromise possible. the commission therefore formally accepts all the amendments tabled today by the rapporteurs and approved on 25 april by the committee on civil liberties, chaired by mr watson. i hope that the european parliament will also follow the advice and recommendations of your rapporteurs so that we can adopt this regulation and make it really operational as soon as possible. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the nature of the documents produced and held by each institution, and the volume of documents they deal with, really reflect their specificities, their roles, which are both different and complementary. and of the three institutions, the commission is by far the one which deals with the largest volume of documents, documents of a very diverse nature precisely because of its role as initiator of legislation, guardian of the treaties and guarantor of community law, of respect for community law as well. the commission is fully aware that it will therefore be the main responsible body for implementing the regulation on public access to documents. it is precisely because of the dual objective of ensuring the widest possible public access to documents and ensuring compliance with community law that the commission has decided on the system to be used in respect of the sensitive case of infringement proceedings. the commission wishes to state here, and will also state to the council, that in a spirit of compromise and as proposed by the rapporteurs, mr cashman and mrs maij-weggen in particular, it can accept that infringement procedures are not among the exceptions provided for in the regulation, as it considers that this approach does not prejudice the exercise of its responsibilities in respect of monitoring compliance with community law. i believe that by implementing this regulation we will be doing a service to the institutions and the citizens of the union. it is with full awareness of the importance of this text that i want to assure you that the commission is determined to apply it and to apply this regulation diligently and effectively, not just because it is its duty, but because, i am deeply convinced, and it is in this spirit that i have personally worked alongside you and the presidency of the council since president prodi entrusted me with this dossier, i am deeply convinced that this text is intended to considerably strengthen the democratic legitimacy of the union's institutions.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, and all those involved in this process! the european parliament will shortly be voting on the proposal for a regulation on public access to eu institutional documents, drawn up jointly by parliament, the council and the commission in the framework of the so-called tripartite cooperation. this new regulation is a step towards a more open, more democratic eu by making the work of bureaucrats more transparent to the public. the starting point is that all documents should be accessible to the public. of course there are exceptions to this general rule, just as there are to the swedish principle of publicity, but the exceptions have been considerably reduced compared to the original proposal, and are now limited to the strictly necessary to protect mainly military and security interests, business secrets and information on the private life of individuals. each case shall be examined individually and a new examination shall be carried out in response to each new request from the public. the refusal to disclose a document may only be made if it is established that disclosure would harm the interests of citizens. in previous proposals, it was sufficient to state that disclosure could potentially harm those interests. for the convenience of the public, there shall be a register of references to all documents and, as far as possible, both the register and the documents shall be accessible via the internet. these are some of the progress achieved in the tough negotiations of recent months. there are still critics of the compromise. they should bear in mind that most eu countries have nothing close to the swedish principle of publicity, and that some of them find the issue incomprehensible and its importance overstated. to vote against the proposal because it has not achieved 100 per cent of what was desired would be frivolous. does it make sense to go hungry just because you haven't had a three-course meal? the requirement to review the proposal before the 2004 european elections is a prerequisite for citizens' support. this will allow for the text to be improved later in the event of political opposition, so voters can influence the issue, including through the way they vote. the result achieved has been presented as a victory for the swedish presidency, and since the swedish government has had openness as a profile issue, it would have been a clear failure for the government to have to leave the walkover to the next presidency. i would like to complete that picture, not take away the victory of the swedish presidency, but complete the picture. prime minister göran persson has made a name for himself as a proponent of the intergovernmental model, a model with little transparency for citizens. a theoretical but relevant question in the context of the transparency debate is what the outcome would have been with persson's intergovernmental model. how much transparency would the citizens have had then? the original proposals of the commission and the council were totally unacceptable from the point of view of transparency and from the point of view of the citizen. the answer is given: parliament is the institution that has stood up for openness and has been able to be used as a lever in the negotiations in the council. without the parliament's hard work for greater openness and the co-decision procedure, such a good result would not have been achieved. i would like to conclude by expressing my warm thanks to michael cashman, hanja maij-weggen, graham watson, heidi hautala and cecilia malmström, to commissioner barnier, who has done a great deal of work in the commission, and finally to the committee secretariat.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the two rapporteurs, and i would like to thank the commission, the presidency and the council for a constructive work. the first question to ask is whether this is the best proposal that parliament can come up with. the answer is no! there are details in this document on which we have comments and which we wish would be better. this applies, among other things, to questions concerning sensitive documents. there may also be comments on the way the process has been conducted, that it has sometimes been conducted behind closed doors, which is generally the case with the conciliation procedure. although there have been some treatments, the procedure is not particularly open. the second question we must ask ourselves is whether this proposal is a success, even a considerable success, in relation to the situation we have today. my answer is an unconditional yes! that's a considerable success compared to the current situation. i cannot reasonably understand those who say that it is a setback. that all incoming documents are now public - they are not today - is that not a success? the fact that in principle all documents should be registered - that is not done today - is that not a success? that the rules of confidentiality that we have, that we recognize must exist and that exist in all the countries that have reached the very top in this area, are relatively few and, moreover, quite visible is that not a success? the answer is an unqualified yes! furthermore, the processing time must be short for an individual seeking access to a document. the internet will be used to a greater extent and the processing time will be limited to 15 days, which means that the process will be relatively quick. if the individual is not satisfied with the decision, there shall be a right of appeal and review. this, if anything, is a success! in addition, the national rules will apply in those countries which have a better legislation than the one now proposed. there will therefore be no retreat for those countries which have more open legislation. all of this is a considerable success. it would therefore seem very strange if this parliament were to vote against a proposal which does not go all the way to the finish line. however, we are almost there and have the opportunity to follow up this in annual trials. in addition, an audit will be carried out in a few years. we in parliament will of course say yes to this proposal! citizens want more transparency in the institutions. this proposal represents a clear improvement in the transparency and thus also in the democratic process within the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it should be a happy day when the european parliament votes on eu rules on transparency and access to documents, but there are too many crumbs in the cup of joy. the reason for this is the wording of sensitive documents which makes it possible in the future to classify not only sensitive military documents but also more general documents on security and perhaps also on other policy areas. the wording on sensitive documents is too general and too lax. as for the documents which will be classified in the member state of sweden, for example, will the commission not be the one to decide in future? if so, it is a serious infringement of the swedish constitution. i also agree with what has been said about the procedure itself, namely that it is very difficult to understand that negotiations on such a sensitive and important matter have taken place between the committee, the council and the commission, out of the view of ordinary members and the public, in an informal process which i would describe as very dubious.
mr president, i would like to thank you. transparency and the right to access documents are central elements of a living democracy, but the proposal we are now going to vote on on access to documents in the eu is a disappointment. in some areas, it is even worse than the practice already in place in the eu. the negotiations on the proposal itself have also been a display of a lack of transparency. the normal parliamentary procedure has been disregarded and significant parts of parliament, including our group, have been excluded from influence. respect for national rules on transparency is not guaranteed by the proposal. an eu regulation is superior to national rules. in addition, paragraph 14 of that regulation states that the member states must be loyal to its content. article 5 further states that in cases of uncertainty, member states shall ask the eu institutions for guidance in deciding whether eu documents may be disclosed. it is therefore not true that the swedish principle of publicity is guaranteed by this proposal. on the contrary, the court of justice of the eu has the final say in disputes arising in this area. extensive and partly vague exceptions are made to the rules on access to documents. whole areas such as security policy, internal documents and documents from member states are in practice excluded. the so-called "sensitive documents" don't even have to be reported in the diaries. these exceptions greatly undermine the improvements made by the proposal, such as the requirements for diaries, access to electronic documents and time limits. in this debate, the european parliament's access to classified documents has also been dealt with, alongside the public's right to documents. it's an unfortunate confusion of issues that should not have happened. i do not see that the advantages of this proposal clearly outweigh the disadvantages. it is not time to finish this work, it is not finished. the settlement should proceed as usual.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when the present commission took office in autumn 1999, all the commissioners promised that 'there would be new times'. the work would be promoted by openness and transparency and the mistakes of the santer commission would not be repeated. the general debate in 1999 was very much about openness and transparency. several of us took the decision to make the eu more open. transparency and publicity are essential elements of democratic and efficient administration, and if citizens can follow the decision-making process in the eu machinery, trust is also increased. we also know that there is a clear correlation between high levels of transparency and low levels of corruption in an administration. the regulation we are voting on tomorrow is a compromise between transparency-friendly countries in the council of ministers and parliament. in many ways, it's a step forward. it provides european citizens with a coherent set of rules on how they can access documents. it sets out important principles, which several of my colleagues have been keen to see. however, it also contains a number of shortcomings which we liberals regret. there is a kind of rubber clause which concerns so-called sensitive documents which must be dealt with in a special way. this is an unfortunate rule and one that can be interpreted arbitrarily. in addition, there is some uncertainty as to what would apply in relation to national law should a conflict arise. in some member states, such as sweden and the netherlands, there are more far-reaching rules on access to documents. in sweden, the tradition of openness goes back to my party friend, the liberal anders chydenius, in 1766. what happens if a document is secret in brussels but public in stockholm? the government and the swedish prime minister have said that swedish law applies then, can you trust that? the presidency has a very great responsibility, namely to ensure that dutch legislation, but above all the swedish constitution, is not eroded and that no self-censorship is instigated by the swedish authorities. it is obvious that there is a fear in the commission and in some member states that the public will be over-informed. those of us who believe in openness must constantly fight to prove otherwise. transparency is not dangerous, it is democratic and effective! the liberal group sees this as a compromise which makes some progress but also contains some shortcomings. we are voting yes tomorrow as the first step in the long journey ahead of us in opening up the eu institutions to citizens. this is where further efforts are needed, and we will test the limits of legislation. we are also taking the lead in bringing about even better rules in a few years' time, because after all, transparency is the future.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we all know that euro notes and coins will be introduced on 1 january next year. twelve of the national currencies of the member states are disappearing and being gradually replaced by the new single currency. the names kohl and delors should be mentioned in this context. it is strange that not all member states have yet realised that the internal market cannot be fully effective in the long term if it is fragmented into a variety of national currencies. we all know the benefits of the single currency, that is, economic and monetary cooperation. the use of the euro on the european continent has already brought economic stability and benefits, promoted economic development, increased consumption and investment and created new jobs. i believe that the single currency will stimulate greater mobility to an extent that will lead to a whole new europe in ten years' time. the euro is a great step forward in european integration. the euro will become one of the largest and most important currencies in international trade. in a few years, 300 million people will have this new national currency, which will be as usable outside the eu as the dollar is today and can be used for direct payments without change. the euro is already an international reserve and transaction currency and will remain so to an even greater extent in the future. the importance and importance of the euro as an international currency will unfortunately also make it attractive to organised counterfeiters. the increasing sophistication of equipment for such operations has reduced the complexity of counterfeiting. against this background, it is important to have comprehensive and effective anti-counterfeiting protection in place well in advance of the euro's entry into circulation. the potential for the euro to spread is enormous and extends beyond both the eu's external and internal borders. the conditions for protecting the euro against counterfeiting must therefore be adapted to this new dimension and also be equipped with a legal framework which will enable us to get the support of all the eu member states, institutions and bodies and the international organisations concerned. the three proposals for legal acts on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting dealt with in the report are complementary. unfortunately, the legal basis is based on two different pillars, the first and the third. in the long term, it would be desirable to include provisions based on article 29 teu in title iv of the ec treaty, so that adequate decisions can be taken in a uniform manner to solve the problems that cannot be solved by other measures. the first draft act is an initiative of the government of the french republic and is based on title vi of the ec treaty. it is therefore a question of criminal law protection and of europol's units having access to the information and data they need to carry out their work. the amendments are intended to incorporate some of the texts which have been omitted and to correct certain terminological and other ambiguities. the second, and in some ways more interesting, proposal for a council directive on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting is based on article 123 (4) of the ec treaty. this legal basis allows the scope of the regulation to be extended only to those member states which have adopted the euro as their single currency. the proposal is to set up a system of information exchange, cooperation and mutual assistance. my main amendments concern the need to have a counterfeit analysis centre at eu level and a national centre in each member state. it also mentions the need to establish a permanent technical and scientific centre for the analysis and classification of counterfeit coins and, not least, the need for the exchange offices, which deal with banknotes and coins on a daily basis for their customers, to carry out adequate checks on their authenticity. otherwise, the citizens are the ones who suffer, and they are left totally unprotected when they receive counterfeit coins and notes. there is no way they can compensate for such a loss anywhere else. how can a citizen carry a monitoring device the size of a large pc in his pocket? even if someone is willing to pay for a device - it's not that big of a sum, about the price of a pc - it's not practical. i therefore think it is much more appropriate to check banknotes and coins at the exchange offices. this is a position that parliament should definitely take in order to protect the citizens. the third proposal is based on article 308 of the ec treaty and is aimed at the member states which have not adopted the single currency, namely the united kingdom, denmark and sweden. they must also be covered by the safety regulation we are working on, otherwise it will not work. i am sure that the citizens of these countries realize that they too must be involved in these decisions. however, they must also realise that if they are not members of the club, they cannot participate in the decisions on the same terms as the members. however, those who join the euro can do so on the same terms as other members. unfortunately, those who don't join the movement don't have the same influence. whether or not to join is indeed a decision that one must have the strength to make, and on this point i would like to call on my own country, sweden, to try to gather the strength to make this decision as soon as possible. it is not about anything other than political leadership. we can compare this with france, for example, which, at the time of the maastricht treaty, approved it with a smaller majority than sweden. france, however, has managed to make its decision, unlike sweden. this is something i deeply regret. i would like to conclude by saying that the proposals contained in the council guidelines are aimed at ensuring the effective protection of the euro and i therefore welcome the proposal as a whole.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. we shall now deal with the question of synthetic drugs. europe is a world leader in the production of synthetic drugs. it's not something to be too proud of. large quantities of lsd, amphetamines, ecstasy and other synthetic drugs are produced every day on our continent, either for domestic consumption or for export to third countries. this large-scale and often organised production is a disgrace to europe and undermines our credibility in the international fight against organised crime. this initiative against synthetic drugs should therefore be warmly welcomed. the proposal is based on a highly developed analytical method, applied by some laboratories. the production process and production methods of synthetic drugs give them certain common characteristics, which make it possible to identify a link between synthetic drugs seized at different times and in different places. such information can help the police to identify illegal drug production centres or networks. the method provides the police and the judiciary with an excellent tool to call in the drug factories. the first decision concerns the establishment of the system of specific forensic profiling. member states are obliged to send samples of certain batches to certain laboratories. unfortunately, today such facilities exist only in sweden, germany, and the netherlands. the idea is that as soon as a laboratory in a member state has the necessary competence, it should be included in the system. this broadens the scope of competence throughout the union. the second decision concerns the actual transmission of samples of controlled narcotic substances. member states are obliged to allow the consignments to pass through their territory. the consignments must be transported in a manner which ensures the highest possible security to ensure that the samples transported are not misused. it should also be stressed that these are milligrams, that is to say, extremely small quantities, which can hardly be misused because they are so extremely small. it should be pointed out that the initiative only concerns the fight against the large-scale organised production of synthetic drugs, which is prohibited throughout the eu. so it is not at all a question of the sensitive debate on what should be allowed on the street, for personal use, etc. this debate is not at all concerned with this, but with the organized production of synthetic drugs. it should also be stressed that the proposed system is not definitive. it's not perfect in every way. i know that. it would be preferable for all countries to have their own laboratories for forensic profiling, but as the technology is only developed in a few countries, this is not the case at present. at the same time, it would be a grave mistake not to take advantage of the opportunity to acquire the production units that exist within the union. as regards the transport system itself, i think it would be good, as some countries, such as france and spain in particular, have also been in favour of, if these samples could be transmitted electronically. i share that view. the report also states that the transport mode must be continuously reviewed. in my view, however, it would be completely wrong to use such arguments to prevent the introduction of the system now. this parliament will certainly be extremely important in pushing forward the issue of electronic transfer, but i really hope that spain will not use it as an argument to stop the transfers. that would be deeply regrettable. fortunately, the committee recognised this and the report was adopted unanimously. i regret that there are some countries in the council which have not realised that it is just as well to start now. these are actually slowing down the law enforcement. as i see it, it is simply irresponsible to put the cart before the horse in the fight against organised crime simply because we do not have the perfect system. this is the same argument we heard earlier when we discussed openness and transparency and where sweden offered europe a compromise. now there is every reason for us to do the same thing together. i hope that both parliament and the council will support this initiative, and i would like to thank the young nils hänninger of the ministry of justice in sweden who has put forward this idea. i would also like to congratulate the swedish presidency on this decision, which is likely to be the first binding decision in the field of drugs.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the european parliament has been given a very important role in the european union. roth-behrendt has done an extremely good job for a long time in fighting bse, or tses. we have now finally reached the point where we may have some sort of order and stop the chains of infection that we know exist in this context. i would like to take this opportunity to ask us to think more long-term. sadly, mankind still does not know why, where, and when prions go "mad", when this prion disease occurs, and for what reason. the situation requires a certain humility on our part. at the moment there is another discussion on animal by-products, and it now looks as if a new round of debate is about to begin. we tried to make a cow that's a vegetarian an all-eater, and it gave us bse. at the moment we are at a point where i suspect that out of fear, panic or for other reasons, political populism perhaps, we will try to make the pig a grazing animal, a vegetarian. it would also be good if we looked a little grand at our own history. our ancestors knew very well that you should not eat animals that have died by suicide. it is in modern times that we have become so far removed from biology that we have begun to engage in such experiments. i would like us to be calm and quiet, to show a little humility in the face of the food disasters that have befallen us.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this regulation aims to coordinate and facilitate the movement of pet animals within europe. the case may at first sight seem trivial, but we swedes remember it more than twenty years ago, when the retired swedish ambassador to portugal refused to leave his home in lisbon because his life partner, an old angora cat, would have to be quarantined for a long time to be allowed to enter sweden. these rules are primarily intended to protect against rabies, a disease that has been associated with great mystery and fear in folk belief. it's not for nothing that the disease is called water phobia. ireland, the united kingdom and sweden have been free of rabies for several years and the proposal for a regulation is therefore primarily relevant to those countries, even though the incidence of rabies has decreased throughout europe. however, we must take into account that the rules are designed to provide adequate protection against the spread of rabies primarily to rabies-free areas. after mad cow disease and foot-and-mouth disease, rabies cases in the uk, for example, due to changes in eu regulations, would create great distrust of the eu organisation. during the course of the case, the legal basis has been amended. the reasons for the amendment are that the regulation aims to protect public health. however, animal diseases, including those affecting companion animals, can also be very aggressive and cause great suffering. one example is aggressive forms of canine disease. i would therefore like to ask commissioner byrne: do you think that the safeguards provided for in other ec directives are sufficient to allow member states to take action against infectious diseases which can cause great suffering among pet animals?
