in writing. on international journalists' day, 3 may, the european parliament voted rules which will conceal many eu documents. the eec/epc group is dissociating itself from the outcome of the closed negotiations and today we voted against the compromise between the european parliament and the major political groups, the council and the commission. the rules on transparency in the eu were discussed behind closed doors and the result shows unfortunately that the eu institutions see citizens as opponents rather than allies. the rules on which documents can be excluded from public access are so vague that it will not be difficult to withhold almost all documents without exception. internal documents are not defined and may be withheld if their disclosure would significantly affect the decision-making process. member states requiring the classification of documents also require that they not be registered, without it being clear which rules will govern this procedure. documents relating to the common foreign and defence policy may be withheld from the public in their entirety, as may documents relating to internal security. the rules will have an impact on all member states, with the result that the national ability to achieve greater transparency in member states' political processes will now be subordinated to the common sets of rules of the union. public debate on this subject was almost non-existent, and the closed procedure under which the rules were established did little to contribute to the project of bringing the union closer to the citizens. the result is that the rapporteurs have been hiding in the shadow of the swedish presidency in the hope that transparency and the public interest would be covered thanks to the long tradition of transparency in public administration in sweden. but it is clear that the swedes have engaged in a conciliation struggle which has secured consensus in the council rather than safeguarding the interests of the citizens. in other words, the swedes advocated the legalization of the regulation rather than defending the interests of the citizens, and today the european parliament has followed their example. the commission and the council can now relax, while the european parliament, for its own interests, has managed to give the public access to certain confidential documents without any guarantee that any document will be released. we cannot say that this is in the public interest, and it is now becoming clear that the citizens were hostages in a battle to defend the interests of the european parliament itself, in which the european parliament ultimately chose to sacrifice the hostages.
in writing. - (sv) we voted for the cashman report in favour of new, clearer rules for public access to public documents of the european institutions. the compromise which the european parliament is adopting today represents a major step forward towards greater transparency and greater efficiency in the institutions. in addition, the new rules are significantly better than the current situation with regard to public access to documents, which is not regulated by any law. at the same time, we see a number of problems with the new rules. sensitive documents classified as confidential, for example in the name of general security, are exempt from the principle of transparency and do not have to be entered in the registers. the author of a sensitive document is the one who decides whether the recipient can make it public. these rules are in total contradiction to the principle of swedish-style transparency, which has marked our administration for over 250 years. the swedish presidency has assured that the special rules on sensitive documents will only apply to documents relating to foreign and security policy. nevertheless, we see a major risk that they will eventually also include documents relating to other areas, for example cooperation under the schengen agreements and the common policy on refugees. we are also concerned that the swedish constitution, which has its roots in 1766, is threatened by ambiguous european rules. the introduction to the text states that the aim is not to amend existing legislation in the member states. at the same time, the commission obtained at the last minute that the reference to national legislation be deleted. we hope, however, that the commission will keep its promise and defend the right of the member states to take the decision independently to publish documents. throughout the decision-making process, the commission has opposed progress in transparency and the right of citizens to have a look, in favour of confidentiality. this shows that this outdated institution remains marked by bureaucracy and continues to balk at change. it is to be hoped that the new regulation on public access to documents will be an important step towards even clearer rules, rules which can form the basis of a modernised administration, where transparency and the right of access are self-evident. the regulation provides that a review of these rules will take place by 1 january 2004. we shall vote in favour of the report today, convinced that it is just one more step towards a more open european union. the european people's party will continue to work to advance this issue.
in writing. - s{v) i abstained on the vote on public access to european parliament, council and commission documents. the compromise, worked out under the swedish presidency, has the effect of introducing the principle of transparency for the first time in the eu, and of adopting accessibility rather than confidentiality as a general rule. from a swedish point of view, i am afraid that this text agreed by the three eu institutions will lead to a weakening of our principle of transparency and our legislation, which dates back to the 18th century. this concerns in particular article 5 on the need to consult a european body in the event that the swedish authorities are uncertain about whether or not to apply the confidentiality rules. i am afraid that in such a situation the swedish authorities will censor themselves and will only reluctantly go against the directives given by the union, because they do not want to come into conflict with the european institutions. if so, european legislation on the principle of transparency could lead to a decline in public access to documents in my country.
in writing. -{sv) following a short but intensive procedure in the committee on civil liberties and human rights, the european parliament adopted a compromise proposal which was negotiated by a few members, the council, the swedish presidency and the commission. we are aware that this is a difficult and sensitive subject in many respects, not least because certain documents must be classified as confidential and kept out of the public eye, and the rules on openness and transparency differ widely from one member state to another. we regret, however, the way in which this matter has been dealt with in the committee on civil liberties, in this case by a procedure which is not in parliament's rules of procedure, or which has not been practised before. the result was, from the first reading, a conciliation procedure between the european parliament, the council and the commission, without members or the public having the opportunity to follow the proceedings. this is a curious fact, given that the subject was precisely one of transparency and the right to look. the greens/efa group believes that the issue of transparency should have been dealt with in a longer procedure, which would have given european citizens a better chance of following the development of the case.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as we all know, tobacco smoking causes very serious adverse effects on public health. it is important to note that these negative effects affect not only smokers themselves but also those who are exposed to smoke, allergy sufferers, and many others. smoking also leads to a sharp increase in health-care costs in many countries. as politicians, we have a duty to warn of the dangers of smoking, to reduce the harmfulness of tobacco products and to try to tackle misinformation. i believe that the compromise we have reached with the council of ministers is a clear step in the right direction in this area. in the gue/ngl group, for example, we are particularly pleased with the requirements for lower tar content, nicotine and carbon monoxide limits, clearer warning texts and the requirement for companies to account for additives. we regard the results in these areas as major progress. in two areas we would have liked to see action taken more quickly, but we believe that at least we are on the right track. this concerns the ban on misleading brand names, which does not specify a year for implementation, which we would have liked to see. that's a future mission. there is at least a commitment to that in the future. we would also have liked to see more rapid progress on banning the export of tobacco products which are not authorised under eu rules. it is a little inconvenient that it is possible to export things which we consider to be hazardous to health in our own countries. we would have preferred to see a simultaneous ban in the eu and an export ban on these products, but at least a ban has been set for 2007. the swedish snus has been discussed earlier in this conciliation, and we believe that a good solution has been found in that area as well. it means that the exception for sweden, to keep the traditional snus, is maintained without opening up the market in other eu countries. we believe that other eu countries have the right to refuse this product if they want to give it up, as many of them certainly do. on the whole, we are very satisfied with the outcome of the conciliation and believe that the rapporteur has done a good job. if you want to be a little critical, you can say that the eu has a problem with credibility in terms of tobacco policy as a whole. perhaps a billion euros a year is still spent on subsidies for tobacco cultivation. if we are to have more credibility in the fight against the harmful effects of tobacco smoking, perhaps it is time to start phasing out these subsidies.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the council adopted most of the proposals adopted by parliament at first reading. we got through the main points of principle, such as the fact that the user level, i.e. those who challenge the animals, should also be covered by the control. this is a success, as about two thirds of all animal feed in the union is produced on farms. what the council, and possibly the commission, has not taken note of, however, is that we have replaced the word 'contaminated' with 'contains foreign substances'. this isn't really a play on words. the term 'contaminated' must be defined scientifically as substances and/or bacteria which have been introduced from outside, whereas 'contains foreign substances' also includes those which are naturally formed, for example during storage, when fats oxidize and mycotoxins are formed. there are a lot of substances that are clearly unsuitable in feed, but they don't come from outside. they have arisen in natural processes that should not have occurred. you must not let the hay mould, it will be dangerous. i have accepted all the amendments which call for it not to be called 'contaminated' but 'contains dangerous substances'. we have also adopted amendment no 24 from the first reading, which deals with community control. in this case, the council referred to a settlement reached with bart staes after we had started work on the report at first reading. i have now amended the amendment to incorporate the wording of the tse regulation adopted at the last plenary session in brussels. we should strive to create clear, uniform and easy-to-understand legislation. i have therefore chosen to copy verbatim the wording of the tse regulation on community control. it is easier for those who are to administer and enforce the laws if at least the standard provisions which appear in directive after directive are worded in the same way. i think it's very important. bart staes will be tabled an oral amendment in this area which involves some extension, and which i can accept. it is vital that we have a comprehensive control system throughout the union. it is not a matter of much concern, though it is hard work, to establish good laws throughout the food safety chain that we are now working on. the big problem is control and compliance. the good laws, my dear colleagues, are a good thing, but they are only words on paper unless they are monitored and enforced!
- (sv) mr president, with the two proposals we are now considering, an important step has been taken in reducing the amount of waste from electronic equipment. instead of throwing away all the household appliances and computer components, as is now the case, we are laying down the legal basis for individual collection and recycling in all the eu member states. the basic principle of the recycling proposal is that the recyclers are paid. it is not sufficient that the costs of collection and recycling have not been passed on to consumers. these costs must be included in the total cost of the product. it is therefore important that the costs of collection and recycling are integrated into the price of the product as a separate, visible contribution. only when these costs have been integrated into the price are the actual environmental costs reflected in the price. it is also important that every company pays the cost of its products. such a scheme does not constitute an obstacle to practical cooperation in the field of collection and recycling. individual economic responsibility is however more efficient and fair than a common economic responsibility. an individual system for new waste favours producers who use the best materials and make products with a simple construction so that they are easily recyclable. the environmental benefits of products are a product development incentive. the rapporteur is aware that the various political groups have succeeded at an early stage in reaching compromise on several important areas. we greatly appreciate it and we believe that the position of the parliament in the further negotiating circle with the council has been strengthened. we support the objective of increasing the number of products collected and the number of products that can be recycled. as regards the second proposal for a directive on the restriction of the use of dangerous substances in electronic equipment, we think that the environment committee could have gone further with its amendments, particularly as regards the ban on fire retardants. recently we have read alarming reports on the increase in these substances and the dangers they may pose to health. we also believe that member states should already have the possibility of imposing stricter requirements on such products. the commission has recently turned a blind eye to the swedish authorities' insistence on strict environmental standards in the area of dangerous substances when purchasing 400 000 computers. we find it totally unacceptable that the commission and the eu procurement rules prevent member states from imposing stricter environmental requirements on product development in this area.
- (sv) mr president, i too join in the many congratulations which we would like to extend to our own shadow rapporteur, mrs van brempt, for the excellent but difficult work she has done. i think we have achieved good results, although in certain areas we have to meet stricter requirements. the most important is, however, the establishment of producer responsibility, which in my view means the greatest environmental gain. i also regard your proposal as a step forward for all progressive and environmentally conscious companies that are already working in this way. they are given the opportunity to develop their ecological ideas and products in an environmentally friendly way, which is comparable with other companies. mr de roo and mr sjöstedt have spoken well on the subject of dangerous substances, but i would like to mention the bridged fire retardants. it's a pity we couldn't get on the train any further. the scientific evidence that these substances are dangerous and have an effect on health has also been scientifically demonstrated. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. it is curious that we are uncertain about your problem and are limited to two types of fire retardants. we know that these substances are dangerous. we know that children get attention deficit disorder and other types of disorder such as weight loss can occur when these substances are in the body, even though they have never been asked to get these substances in their body. the directive on dangerous substances is based on article 95 and the directive on waste electrical and electronic products on article 175. i can't understand that. when we discussed a similar directive on concept vehicles, most members saw this directive as a model for the future, but the memory left many clearly in the dark. i would like to see this issue brought into line with the theme of this evening, namely sustainable development. here again, the internal market is determining how far we want to go in the field of the environment, and countries that want to go further are being punished. that's not right.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i think there are many good things in the commission's report and i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on a good job. there is a lot of talk about solidarity and security. that is precisely what we should be talking about in the case of pensions. nevertheless, there is great concern about the pension debate. my colleagues have expressed concern about some important points. in my home country, sweden, we have already begun to adapt to this type of change, and a significant part of our future pension system will be linked to a supplementary pension based on what happens in the capital markets. in sweden, most people have started buying and selling shares. over the past year, many of them have experienced firsthand the risks of buying stocks. many have lost large portions of their savings and are now wondering whether the same thing will happen when you have a pension system that is heavily dependent on what happens in the capital market. there's a concern that it's going to be an unfair system. above all, there may be a concern that it will be an insecure system. our group has tabled an amendment for tomorrow's vote in which we ask that at least the impact of the new system be evaluated and then the effects on solidarity in society be examined. what about security? how are the women? several speakers, including jensen and lancker, have pointed to women as a particularly vulnerable group, and in many countries i believe that women could be put in a difficult position by the system now being introduced. i would also like to mention our amendment no 13, which calls for a general increase in the retirement age as a way of solving future pension problems. it is tempting to try to increase not only the level of employment and the employment rate but also to reduce the pressure on pension systems. however, it requires that people are able to work until a high retirement age. in some countries this may work because there is already a relatively low retirement age, at least for some of the retired population. but in my country and in the other scandinavian countries, where there is already a relatively high retirement age of 65-67 years, there is now talk of raising the limit to 70 years. i think that's unrealistic. most people are worn out by a long working life long before they reach retirement age. in that situation it is stupid and inhumane to try to pressure people to remain in the labour market. finally, i would like to say that i find it regrettable that neither the commission nor the report we are discussing today makes any mention of the applicant countries. it is a problem for the future, but when we talk about pensions, we are talking about the future. there should have been a discussion, of course, about what a system of the kind we are now trying to introduce will mean for the poor candidate countries.

in writing. - sv) i voted against the report for the following reasons: article 308 of the treaty does not constitute a legal basis giving the european parliament the right to deal with the matter at this time. the statutes are discriminatory from a democratic and financial point of view by setting high thresholds for who wants to be considered a european party. this does not guarantee justice for minorities, nor does it guarantee their protection. finally, there is the obvious danger, in the direction of american policy, of recognising the right of companies to give financial support to those recognised as european political parties. the ratification of the treaty of nice by the parliaments and governments of the 15 member states will take place at the earliest by the end of 2001. only then will the question of the financing of european political parties be dealt with in depth by the european parliament. today, the decision is rushed. this decision will be examined by the luxembourg court of justice in order to determine whether article 308 of the treaty can actually be regarded as a legal basis. i am convinced that today's decision will then be declared unconstitutional and annulled. article 308 reads as follows: 'if action by the community should prove necessary to attain, in the functioning of the common market, one of the objectives of the community, without this treaty having provided the necessary powers of action for that purpose, the council, acting unanimously on a proposal from the commission and after consulting the european parliament, shall take the appropriate measures.' for a party association to be eligible for tax-funded grants at european level, the european parliament considers that it must obtain at least five per cent of the votes in at least one third of the member states, or be represented in the european parliament, the national or regional parliament in one third of the member states. the commission does not want a relative number but a definite number of member states (five, regardless of the size of the union). the democratic threshold is set very high, far too high, by the european parliament, and that is striking. large groups of citizens, who have representatives in this institution, can be excluded from this tax financing of political parties at european level. the wording of article 3 on the vision of european political associations and national parties shows a vision of the future which i cannot share in any way: 'of the party or its national components'. the european parliament has therefore defined national political parties as future 'national components', a kind of regional organisation of what will be called european political parties, which deserve to be supported by the funding of european taxpayers. the christian democratic party of sweden, led by birger ekstedt at its launch in 1963 and by alf svensson from 1973, and founded by generous, persevering pioneers who were true to the primary meaning of the christian conception of man, ethics and the idea of the family for society, represents something much more important and essential to democracy than what is described as "a national component".
by hand. the pace of the imposition of sanctions on israel, madam minister anna lindh! in the edition of today (16 may 2001) of the french newspaper "libération", one of the most well-known pacifists in israel, the writer david grossman notes that, for those who do not believe in miracles or appreciate catastrophes, there is only one way to alter the situation in the middle east, namely "international pressures". it also affirms that it is israel, as occupier, which bears the principal responsibility, but that it is israel which most accurately represents its external pressures. since the us never does anything that israel dislikes, there is an important international role that the us represents. the eu is by far the largest donor of financial and administrative aid and the largest supporter of the palestinian authority. the politicians of the eu claim that the union should be represented by a political correspondent. why should we not accept the challenge, doing what the united states does not want to do, but what both the palestinians and the israelis who want peace are asking us to do, namely, putting concrete pressure on israel? in the light of international law, there are formal grounds for the application of economic sanctions against israel, as a commission confirmed violations of the eu association agreement, in that products originating from illegal israeli settlements in the occupied territories are sold to the eu, indicating as country of origin 'israel'. is it really true that a new eu intervention force is not designed to protect the interests of the eu super-power, but, as the foreign minister anna lindh used to say, serves only to make peace in the world, because it does not propose to deploy in the middle east, along the only line of legal separation between israel and palestine, which is the 1967 border? unfortunately, when today the minister, mrs anna lindh, presented to the european parliament the eu's policy on the middle east, nothing was heard. foremost among them are the words, but nothing concrete, nothing that could in any way put an end to the bloodshed and illegal occupation of palestine by israel. but tragically, sweden's own voice has remained silent on palestine. the work of the former swedish foreign minister, sten andersson, was not taken up by his successor, anna lindh, but by his danish colleague, mogens lycketoft, who spoke out against the violations of human rights and international law by israel. the minister anna lindh therefore seems to have capitulated to the forces that dare not confront israel and the usa, both as president-in-office of the eu and as swedish minister for foreign affairs. it is a pity.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the treaty of nice is usually marketed as an enlargement treaty. i mean, it's not right. the institutional issues of enlargement have not been resolved by the treaty of nice. moreover, most of what was decided in nice has nothing to do with enlargement. as the report points out, the distribution of seats in the european parliament must be rearranged, as there is direct discrimination against hungary and the czech republic. it can be done in the accession treaties, just as these matters can be regulated in general in the accession treaties, as they did for example for sweden, austria and finland when we entered the european union. the treaty of nice is really about something else. it is about strengthening the power of the large eu countries at the expense of the small ones. all the applicant countries, with the exception of poland, are therefore also losers, since they are small countries in the european union. the decision-making process in the council of ministers is becoming more complex, not easier. the other main features of the treaty of nice are the increase in the supremacy of the state and the abolition of the right of veto in several areas. there are also some distinctly federalist elements, such as support for european political parties and the new process of appointing the european commission, which makes the commission increasingly resemble a government with a prime minister. the real content of the treaty of nice is that an enlarged union will be dominated by the same large countries that dominate today's european union and that the march towards a federalist state formation in the eu continues, even if the pace of development is not as fast as some in this house would wish. it is therefore not difficult for my party and for me to vote against both the treaty of nice and the report on which we are going to vote tomorrow. it is important to note, however, that there are also some positive elements in the report. among other things, open ministerial meetings are required. it would have been reasonable to have taken such a decision in nice, i.e. to open the legislative assembly to public scrutiny. the distribution of seats in the european parliament must also be changed, which is a necessary democratic requirement. but we don't believe in the convention method. we don't really see the point of having a convention to work out the next treaty amendment. if, after all, there is a convention, we believe it should be a convention of those who decide on the treaty, namely the national parliaments. we hope that the swedish presidency and the swedish government will not shrink an inch from the view that it is the national parliaments which should have the right to decide on the treaties and that it is they who should guide the development of the treaties. what would you have done in nice? we would have liked to see the eu democratized, the power of officials and the commission reduced, the power of national parliaments increased, the council of ministers more open and transparent, the dominance of the free market over environmental and social considerations changed, and the dogmatic monetarism which is the basis of monetary union reconsidered. now we see how the social democratic governments have begun to fall around the eu. nothing really has happened to the foundations of the eu during the time we have had a 'red-green' dominance in the eu. the opportunity is about to pass, and the foundations of the eu have not changed in either the 'red' or the 'green' direction, which we regret.
mr president, i would like to thank you. obviously, there is a lot to be said about the treaty of nice. it is positive that the formal obstacles to enlargement have been removed and that the union is now constitutionally ready for its important task of unifying europe. we now hope for a clearer timetable and, hopefully, a date for the summit in gothenburg in a few weeks' time. but there are some major problems with the treaty: decision-making is not becoming easier, more efficient or more understandable to citizens. it also leaves much to be desired in terms of democracy. too many decisions will continue to be taken behind closed doors in the council - without democratic control, transparency or participation by parliament. the method of the igc is a bit like the papal election. for several days, the delegates are locked in to negotiate while the outside world eagerly awaits the white smoke, a sign that the compromise is complete. in the meantime, nobody knows what's going on. but now the next treaty will have to be prepared in a different way, together with the citizens. it is important to remember these solemn speeches when the plans for laeken are being drawn up. maybe it's a convention after all. it is no secret that the majority of this house is in favour of it. this is the most open and democratic way of preparing proposals. now, however, rumors are swirling that there will be a convention without government representatives. what's it gonna be then? a discussion club, an alibi that the ministers then go over in the usual way? that's not a good solution! it is also time to turn our backs on nice and look ahead. we do that in the european parliament too, perhaps not so much today, but we will do it. some heads of government do too. i also hope that more heads of government and ministers will take part in the debate on the future of europe. the foreign minister spoke of the concept of federalism and the different emotions it evokes. that's true, and the content is of course more important than the concept. federalism, however, with its various interpretations, is a vision of a more democratic europe. different visions of the european project are necessary for the european debate, so that citizens will eventually know what issues to take a stand on.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to add my voice to the praise that many members have paid to seguro and mendez de vigo. i share corbett's view that if the rapporteurs had not done such a good job, there would have been a high probability that parliament would have recommended a 'no' to nice. although i share the criticism, i think that a no is much worse than a yes. the reason is that the important enlargement process would then have been delayed, which is something i do not want to happen. i agree with much of the criticism. the main criticisms are that the treaty of nice is more difficult to understand, which also affects the ability of citizens to participate in the european debate. it's a difficult treaty that's hard to understand. in addition, it risks becoming inefficient by making it more difficult to take decisions by qualified majority. it is true that more areas are covered by qualified majority voting, but these are not so heavy. the really important areas were not included in this form of decision-making, which is unfortunate now that the union is enlarging. this is something we have to change. we need more decisions by qualified majority and the co-decision of parliament. the second criticism concerns the transparency and the way in which the treaty was negotiated. i've heard some people compare the negotiations to a papal election where you lock yourself in a room and then come out with a compromise. no outsiders were allowed to participate in the process. let's not do this again. let us start the convention, and let it be the starting point for the next igc. that does not mean that we should not have national debates with organisations and others. in this context, i also think it is important that we take responsibility as politicians. we must not make the institutional debate the main issue, but we must start with the issues: what do we really want to do with the eu? how is this to be done? what tools will we use? we need to start the debate on the slow motion.
mr president, mr president of the council! the nice intergovernmental conference will make possible the enlargement of the eu. that was also its main function. the treaty will be ratified by all 15 national parliaments of the member states in 2001, no matter how dissatisfied and critical the majority here in the european parliament is. therefore, we swedish christian democrats believe that paragraph 42 on the european parliament's consent to the accession treaties for the candidate countries could be worded much more positively and welcomingly. how will paragraph 42 be received in estonia, poland or cyprus? my party cannot accept paragraph 25 either, which opens the door to the introduction of a european taxation system of its own - and with qualified majority voting rules. all historical experience shows that new levels of taxation and new levels of decision-making on tax collection lead to higher taxes and less money left for citizens. another key issue for the decisionmaking powers of each national parliament and government is defence policy. the christian democratic party of sweden cannot therefore accept paragraph 34, which calls for defence policy to be included in the so-called closer cooperation. at the same time, my party would like to welcome two historic improvements proposed in the report. firstly, it is time to improve the transparency and control of the council's work by the citizens and the national parliaments, and to make the council's meetings open when the council acts as legislator. in order to be able to speak about democracy and not just about diplomacy within the eu, we must have this openness, that is, we say yes to paragraph 11 and amendment no 68. finally, the principle of subsidiarity needs to be clarified. we therefore say to the ppe amendment 77 on the delimitation of the powers of the eu.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i can forgive the previous speaker for speaking too long on this subject. five minutes to debate sustainable development in a 25-year perspective is not much, and it's an art to be able to stick to that time margin exactly in the second. the question is what do we mean by "sustainability strategy"? is this a new political ploy we are trying to invent here in parliament? no, it's not that. it's a legacy that goes back further. we have a mission since the decision was made in rio almost 10 years ago that every nation should maintain a sustainability strategy. what is sustainability? what are we really talking about when we talk about sustainability? we're discussing the future of future generations. we discuss what kind of development, what kind of life we want for future generations, in other words how their lives are to be managed in terms of social issues, ecological development and the economy. however, we must bear in mind that it is easy for us who live in a relatively small, rich part of the world to say that we want to leave behind a legacy with a situation as good as the one we have. the desire of the vast majority of the world must be that their children have a better life than they have. the process in parliament of producing this document, which is nevertheless to be a kind of background, contribution to the gothenburg summit, leaves a lot to be desired. parliament has had to run a kind of parallel race with the commission in order to produce, or at least to have the opportunity to express its views on how this development should be. it's not satisfactory. the commission may have had plenty of time to do so, but parliament should be given a real opportunity to discuss these issues - not just from an environmental point of view. the whole of parliament, committees dealing with social or economic issues and all other committees actually concerned should be involved. otherwise we will confine the question to an environmental issue, which it is not. as regards the policy areas which it would be desirable to deal with in such a document, particularly in view of the direction that the question is to take in gothenburg, i think we must start from the ecological perspective. both the social and the economic aspects are of course important, and we know that they govern our lives. however, without a well-functioning planet in ecological terms, it will be very difficult to discuss economic and social issues. it simply requires that we have an ecology in balance. the commission's text, which we believe is based on the current economic systems, may not be entirely satisfactory. it's like you haven't understood the beauty of the crow song. it's about using the economy that exists. it's about trying to set the boundaries you want for the market. it is a question of finding the instruments that we ourselves can use in our own budget and in our own procurement. if we are to develop a sustainability strategy for the european union, we must take account of the problems within the union, but it is absolutely impossible to isolate the union from the rest of the world. we have to see ourselves as part of the planet, of the global context. what we do in the union affects our environment. we should not export things we do not want ourselves. we must review our trade relations and our agricultural policy and the negative effects it has on the development prospects of other countries. we also have a responsibility in international trade relations. without the local perspective, there is no sustainability strategy. the beauty of the work on agenda 21 was that it provided and provides opportunities for ngos, local actors, municipalities and citizens to participate in the process and understand what it is all about. finally, it is a matter of individual responsibility and the need for a change in behaviour in all areas. the commission, but of course also the council, must think about how to encourage and make people feel involved, so that this is not just a message from above. finally, i would like to say something about political leadership. it is necessary that the message to be given in gothenburg is clear for this to work. it can't just be an empty manifesto. there must also be evidence that the commission and the council really want to go from words to action.
mr president, mr minister, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. sustainable development involves many factors. it's very easy to write about beautiful dreams and use big words. i would like to see the council in gothenburg dare to point forward. at the same time, i would like to see a bold step taken to look at the eu's own accounts, at its own subsidy system. blokland mentions it in paragraph 5 and hulthén in paragraph 33 of his report, namely that we need to review agricultural subsidies. thankfully, the commission's message is a little more pointed. it says: 'in the 2002 review of the cap, quality should be given more weight than quantity.' agriculture is still europe's modernising force, insofar as it is agriculture and forestry that represent our cultural heritage, our recreation and our biodiversity. it's about chemicals and plant nutrients in water. it's about air and public health. then you have to ask yourself: how sustainable is the european union's own system of agricultural subsidies? is it not time to use some of that money for sustainable development, for rural development, for the conservation of biodiversity, for the removal of chemicals, for food that we can eat with joy and security? what if the european council dared to take such an initiative and pointed its hand and said: "this is where we're going!"
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to say a few words about the commission's proposal on sustainable development. it is clear that it could have gone much further in many areas, but i must say that it was sharper than expected and that it contains several proposals which, if implemented, will really make a difference to current environmental policy. i would like to highlight in particular the demands to restrict road transport, to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, to eliminate harmful subsidies, to change the procurement rules in an environmentally friendly way and to adapt agricultural policy. if this is done, it is a major step in change. the question now is whether these concrete proposals will survive the council meeting in gothenburg. i hope they will do so, and that these proposals will not be removed from the final document, but that they will remain and that the general arguments may be shortened. i think that this can be a real contribution to changing this policy. i wish the swedish presidency good luck with that. however, in one area the proposal is inadequate, namely in terms of international cooperation and relations with the world trade organisation and environmental rules.
mr president, commissioner, president of the council! i should like to begin by thanking the two rapporteurs for their excellent reports. it is of the utmost importance that this issue of sustainable development now stands high on the political agenda. it is a question of solidarity, that is solidarity with future generations, solidarity within the eu with poorer regions and poorer people, and global responsibility, the way the eu acts in trade and development policy towards the rest of the world. sustainable development is relevant to all policy areas. it is known that it concerns transport policy, agricultural policy and energy policy, but it also concerns social policy. i do not share the views of the previous speaker on this. social policy is also part of sustainable development. good social policy and a fair economic policy are important. sustainable development is also about a variety of approaches. it is not that the current methods of legislation are bad. the legislation must continue. the legislation we have in the eu must be better implemented. we must use the tax instruments, as has been pointed out many times by parliament. the new thing is that we are going to integrate the ecological aspects into the so-called lisbon process. the next council meeting in barcelona in the spring will not only deal with economic, social, lifelong learning, investment and industrial policy issues, but will also be a 'sustainability council' by integrating ecological aspects into policy. the important thing is this new strategy which is being developed and which we warmly support in this parliament.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. it has been very exciting to be part of the journey leading up to the decision on strategic environmental impact assessments that we are about to make. the idea behind environmental impact assessments for plans and programmes is old. the original draft directive came in the 1970s. it then covered policies, plans, programmes and projects. however, the commission changed its mind and decided to propose a directive covering only environmental impact assessments of projects. the next step was taken in 1991 when the commission presented a proposal for a directive on environmental impact assessments also for policies, plans and programmes. however, this proposal was born amid a lively debate on the balance of power within the then european community, and it was met with such resistance that it was withdrawn in 1992. the commission did not give up and stated in the fifth environmental action programme that an impact assessment of policies, plans and programmes is necessary to achieve sustainable development. this is in view of the debate which took place earlier today. at the same time, member states were invited to extend the environmental impact assessment directive to include plans and programmes. in 1997, the commission presented a new, amended proposal, which no longer covered policy areas. that was a pity - for natural reasons, policy areas should also have been included. i tried to reinstate policy areas in the text, but unfortunately this amendment did not receive any support at second reading. i hope it will come up in the future. the purpose of the directive is to ensure that an environmental impact assessment is carried out for certain plans and programmes and that the results of those environmental impact assessments are taken into account during the preparation and approval of plans and programmes which may have an impact on the environment. the introduction of the environmental assessment early in the planning process, so that there is room for adjustment, modification or adoption of alternative plans, is of course an excellent tool for all decision-makers. the directive should of course also be seen as a complement to the environmental impact assessments of projects, but as i said, these come much later in the decision-making process. the settlement of the case started in october 2000 and was concluded in strasbourg on 14 march 2001. although the negotiations have been tough, i am satisfied with the final result, which can be summarised as follows. parliament has passed a requirement for monitoring environmental impact which was not included in the council's common position. this means ensuring that corrective action can be taken if monitoring reveals that the efforts have not been sufficient. it is also necessary to be able to identify harmful effects and, as i have said, to take remedial measures at an early stage if possible. a hard battle was also fought over whether the structural funds, the agricultural funds and the funds for the applicant countries should be included in the directive. in the end, however, we got through a political position that this should happen and that the exceptions that exist today only apply to the programming period ending in 2006/2007. i was not entirely satisfied with this solution. an information note, drawn up by commission officials, confirming that this will of course also apply to the eu's own plans and programmes, is therefore attached. another important point that was well formulated was that on crossborder consultation with third countries and public consultation. i would like to thank everyone involved in the work: the swedish presidency of the council for its tough negotiations, the commission for all its expertise and the colleagues who have taken part in the committee and delegation work, in addition to all the others who have taken part in the delegation. thank you all!
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. at this very late hour, i, as rapporteur, should say a few words about this report. first of all, i would like to thank all my colleagues for the good cooperation in the committee. i would also like to thank the commission for a good proposal for a directive on safety belts and child restraints in vehicles. this is an important question. i would like to reiterate that there are a great many people who die in traffic in the member states of the european union. that's about 42,000 people a year. we would never accept such a high death toll in any other mode of transport. we must therefore take action. the commission is now proposing such a measure. we must also remember that children suffer very seriously on the roads. the most common cause of death among children and young people is road traffic deaths. the question then arises: should the eu legislate on seat belts and child safety in cars? my answer is yes. i think it's very important. firstly, there is a legal basis in the treaty. secondly, there is already legislation, a directive, which must now be tightened up because there is a need for it. we know that seat belts save lives. this is particularly evident in the case of children. accident research shows that the risk of serious injury is seven times greater for children who do not use restraints than for children who do. that is why there is a need for this. in addition, today's traffic in europe is very much crossborder. people move in private cars, but also in commercial traffic. both private and professional traffic is increasing across borders. we saw that in a report today. we also have a sharp increase in tourist flows, which means that there is a need for common rules on basic road safety measures. it is therefore important that we have a high level of safety in all eu countries, so that we know what the rules are, as private individuals and as professional drivers, but also for car manufacturers. it is important for them to have common safety requirements so that the rules are clear. at the same time, it is also important for consumers that safety devices become cheaper, which they will be if the rules are common. the commission proposal is a tightening up of an earlier directive which requires all passengers in vehicles equipped with seat belts to use them, all children to be restrained in approved car seats, except where they disappear, children under three years of age not to be allowed to ride in a car without an appropriate car seat, except in taxi cabs, and the use of rearward facing car seats to be prohibited unless the airbag has been deactivated. these are all good suggestions. the committee agrees that it should also be put forward as a parliamentary proposal. however, we have six amendments. in short, the first amendment means that the commission, together with the member states and road safety organisations, should promote an information campaign. in the second, we propose that the height of a child should be the determining factor in determining when a child can use adult seat belts. amendment no 4 would require manufacturers to indicate which car seats are suitable for which car models. we also propose that rural buses used in urban traffic - it is not very common but it does exist - should not be subject to the requirement of compulsory use. we also want the commission, in cooperation with the automotive industry, to develop safer and more user-friendly systems for airbags. it shall also be included in the report on the implementation of the directive to be submitted by 1 august 2004. the council will examine the commission's proposal at the transport ministers' meeting at the end of june. i am very much looking forward to the fact that we can now reach a common decision in the eu and that we can implement a very important road safety measure which is fundamental to road safety in the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i too want to support all the praise for hautala, who has really worked hard on this. i can imagine that it has not been easy for me as rapporteur to reconcile all the views, even though a majority of the participants were women. i would point out that reality often precedes legislation. i would also like to remind you that legislation is not always enough, but must be supplemented once we have enacted a law. i would therefore like to highlight the need for gender equality plans and the responsibility of employers in a modern society. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament. i think that the last bastion we will soon have left is the employers and their organisations. we've talked a lot about sexual harassment. it is a difficult concept to define, but we now have swedish lawyers trying to work out how to define undesirable, unwelcome behaviour in the workplace by one person towards another. it is exciting to define phenomena which, although old, have only recently been brought to light. we also discussed what we call paternity leave. i would like to point out that you don't choose to be a parent. parenting is something you become when you become a parent. employers question whether you will or plan to become a parent at the time of your employment. work is a part of life, not the whole of life. that applies to men and women, and it applies to children. i hope we can get a large majority behind hautala's proposal.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to thank mr ribeiro e castro for his excellent report. i agree with most of what was said in the debate, not least by the previous speakers. i'd like to make two additions. first of all, i should like to say that i am pleased that the report makes it so clear that the mobility we want in europe must be based on the principle of voluntariness. i would like to emphasize this in the light of the fact that in my own country and in other countries, employment agencies have often been used as a kind of coercive instrument to force, by economic means, a transfer of population between different parts of the country. i hope that such a thing will never be possible at european level. i would also like to stress that i am surprised that no one in this debate has pointed out that we are now discussing technical and practical obstacles to mobility across borders, while large member states are erecting huge practical and political obstacles to mobility between the labour markets of the candidate countries and the current member states. this suggests that there is a political problem, and not merely a practical one, when we talk about mobility in europe.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to clarify point 11 on which we are going to vote. it is not a question of creating new processes, but of adding the ecological dimension to the lisbon process. i know that there have been some reflections and doubts on this point, but it is a question of adding the ecological dimension to a process which already exists.
we, the members of the various parties in the gue/ngl group, did not vote in favour of the report on the post-nice report. we are opposed to the development of the european union into a state with powers superior to those of the member states in all aspects of policy, as the report recommends in practice. instead of turning the eu into a state, we believe that the eu should be democratized. the democratization of the european union must be achieved by reducing the powers of the commission and introducing openness and transparency in the council's legislative practices. the future debate on european union must also concern the role of national parliaments in the future europe. today, the political debate in europe focuses on national parliamentary elections (with the exception of presidential elections in some states) and this can also be seen in the turnout in the various elections. the role of national parliaments in the decision-making process in europe must be strengthened. we also protest against the militarization of the eu. we insist that the eu should remain a civil organisation and that the special status of non-aligned countries should be respected. we believe that the future intergovernmental conference must reconsider the construction of emu and reassess the democratic deficit of monetary union and the right-wing policies it is pursuing. we demand that the stability pact be reconsidered and that article 56 be reformulated so as to bring the ecb under democratic control.
in writing. -sv) the main task of the nice intergovernmental conference was to make possible the enlargement of the eu. all our 15 national parliaments will ratify the treaty of nice in 2001, although a majority of members of the european parliament do not seem to be satisfied with the text. that is why the swedish christian democrats believe that paragraph 42 of the report presented today, which relates in particular to the european parliament's agreement to the accession treaties, could be formulated in a much more positive and welcoming manner towards the applicant countries. how will this point 42 be perceived by, for example, estonia, poland or cyprus? the party to which i belong cannot accept paragraph 25 of the same report, which opens the door to the introduction of european taxation, based moreover on qualified majority decision-making rules. all past experience shows that the creation of new levels of decision-making in tax matters leads to an increase in levies and therefore a decrease in what is left to the citizens we represent. in addition to tax law, another issue which is essential to the competence of all national parliaments and governments is defence policy. the swedish christian democrats cannot therefore support paragraph 31 of the present report, which calls for defence policy to be subject to 'enhanced cooperation' or 'flexible integration'. the christian democrats welcome two proposals in the report which introduce progress which is both historic and absolutely necessary. firstly, it is a question of improving the right of citizens and national parliaments to observe and monitor the work of the council, since council meetings are open to the public when the council is responsible for legislative action. this is a point which i have consistently advocated in the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy and which has received growing support there. it now has the approval of the whole of parliament. if we are to talk about democracy - and not just diplomacy - in the european union, this transparency must become a reality. in other words, we are in favour of adopting paragraph 11 and amendment no 68. the second is to apply and clarify the principle of subsidiarity. there is not one european people in the union, but many peoples. there is not one european language spoken, but a multiplicity of languages. there is no common european nation, but a multiplicity of nations. there is no common european culture, but a multiplicity of cultures. on the basis of this observation and on the basis of fundamental values such as human rights, democracy, transparency, clarity of political responsibilities and respect for cultural and linguistic equality within the union, the swedish christian democrats welcome amendment no 77, which provides for a clear delimitation of the areas of political competence of the eu. this is an idea that fits into the logic that our citizens do not want to see the distance between themselves and the decision-makers widened, nor do they want to see the centralization of european politics increased.
the committee on women's rights has already adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you. when the commission put forward its proposal it said that it had the ambition to go further than the palermo protocol did. i am very disappointed that the palermo protocol has been watered down instead. i think it is extremely important to distinguish between the definitions of refugee, which are not relevant to this particular case, and labour exploitation and sexual exploitation. we now know that about 90 percent of the people trafficked in these cases are women who are intended for sexual exploitation. i understand that the commission and the council, as well as we in this parliament, may find it difficult to cooperate on these issues, especially given the enormous differences in national legislation. in sweden, for the last year and a half, we have had active legislation which also addresses the purchase of sexual services, i.e. customers are criminalised. so we've focused on the man instead of stigmatizing those who prostitute themselves. this is to be seen in the light of the fact that some member states are now legalising brothel activities and thus also pimping. this preserves stereotyped images of women's and men's sexuality and sexual needs. as regards the karamanou report, i would like to say that if amendments nos 3, 5, 6 and 13 are adopted, my group will not be able to vote in favour of the report. the definitions of child pornography that we adopted in the women's committee are much better. in the karamanou report, the definitions are so broad that there is a risk that the maneken pis will have to be covered in the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. 140 years ago, slavery was abolished in the united states and russia. now it has been introduced in europe. according to the un agency iom, half a million women are tricked or forced into europe each year to live as sex slaves. the vastly increased trade is more profitable than drug smuggling and is increasingly controlled by an organized mafia. at the same time, over a million children are forced into child prostitution and the growing child pornography industry each year. these children are increasingly exploited by tourists and businessmen, many of whom are strikingly european. despite this grim reality, the penalties for the sexual exploitation of children, child pornography and trafficking in women are negligible compared with those for drug trafficking. it may be two years in prison, compared with 10 to 15 years for serious drug offences. few perpetrators are brought to justice, and even fewer are convicted. we are debating today an attempt to turn rhetoric and fine words, which we have actually been doing for many years, into concrete action. our four rapporteurs have done an excellent job and in a number of respects they are going further than the commission, to which i would also like to pay tribute. it is absolutely necessary to harmonize legislation and to increase penalties. as regards the sexual exploitation of children, our main demand is that legislation should cover all children under the age of 16, which is fully in line with the customary definition of a child in international conventions. the limitation to 10 years may even worsen the situation of children who have just turned 10 years old, since the demand for these children will then increase dramatically. the lack of legislation is not only about taking action against the criminal but also about the absence of laws protecting the victims. we are already many who support the demand to stop the sexual exploitation of women and children, but more are needed. this calls for more men and more governments, and above all for more concrete action.

mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would like to congratulate mr whitehead, who has done an extremely good job. i would also like to stress the unanimity of the committee on the environment. we know that food crises in the eu have caused people to feel anxious about eating. at the same time, this has led to a focus on food and agricultural policy. now we must get this new system up and running quickly. it is of enormous importance. if we have open borders, which we shall have within the union, and a free flow of goods, it is of course important that food can also flow freely. however, this must not mean that diseases and illnesses also flow freely. of course, a common system cannot mean that regional food is not allowed. if we look at the whole chain of measures, it will require, among other things, scientific advice, risk assessment, risk management, control, including control of controllers in the member states. perhaps sanctions will also be necessary in the future. this new authority will not be able to do all this, but it will provide a new, increased strength at the beginning of the chain in terms of scientific advice and risk assessment. we already have a food and veterinary office in dublin which plays an important role in risk management and control. it is now urgent to establish cooperation between these two authorities. in the end, i do not think the european union will get away with it, but we must have a sanctions system at eu level. however, only the commission can deal with this. it is important that we continue to work to be even more efficient. this is a very important step that we must take quickly to ensure that food is safer. but then we must not forget the issue, we must go further and make the system even better in the future.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the commission's proposal on animal by-products involves a strict separation into three categories. i would remind you that category 1 covers risk material and is covered mainly by the tse regulation which parliament has just adopted for the third time. some amendments go against the tse regulation which we have just adopted. category 1 is for hazardous materials. category 2 includes, inter alia, animals that have died by suicide. the third category includes those classified as by-products of slaughter approved for consumption as a whole. the committee on the environment, like myself, has chosen to support the commission's approach and has tried to improve and, above all, to tighten up the practical regulatory framework. the principles of this report are simple, but the practice is much more difficult. in particular, implementation will be difficult. we therefore call for a strict separation of the material from the three categories. it also requires strict separation of facilities, transport lines, etc. i would add that in future legislation we will probably have to require the same separation of feed mills or lines for different types of feed. it is important to mention that all category 1 and 2 materials must be indelibly marked with colour and odour substances, so that they cannot be re-introduced into, for example, feed production by malice or accident. of course, the same rules as those applicable to domestic production should also apply to exports, re-exports and imports. i am very pleased, together with the committee, that this is the end of 'cannibalism'. this means that pigs can no longer be challenged with their own kind, but i would ask you to bear in mind that the total ban on challenging ruminants with any form of animal protein remains in place and is regulated by the tse regulation, not here. i am personally very pleased that from now on category 2 fat will only be used as fertiliser or, preferably, as fuel oil. i think it is extremely important that we stop at some point the enrichment of heavy metals, pcbs and dioxins. i now hope that parliament will adopt these principles by a large majority. i must therefore turn to amendment no 100 by the ppe-de group. the proposal, which was tabled in committee and voted down, aims to ban all meat and bone meal, with the exception of fishmeal, from all animal feedingstuffs. i'm asking you to think this through. we all know the disaster we caused when we made the idler all-you-can-eat. we can already see the problem with trying to make the omnivore a vegetarian. i plead humility against the very order of nature! we can't manipulate him again! a better way is to vote for the green proposal, now amendment no 64, which requires member states to report by 1 february 2003 on whether they have been able to implement this new regulation accurately and thoroughly. then we'll discuss lifting the temporary ban on meat and bone meal that currently exists. i would also like you to vote against amendments nos 22 and 33. they were actually gonna sabotage part of the cataloging and make it complicated. we do not need to test whether a chicken that has died by suicide should be classified as category 2 instead of category 1. as far as new technology is concerned, i am a firm believer that we should try new technology, never impede its development. i have therefore myself tabled a proposal which was adopted in committee, allowing the use of new methods approved by the scientific committees. this amendment 43 opens up all possibilities for different types of new technology when we get them and when they are approved. i therefore want us to vote against amendments nos 20, 25, 26, 35 and 43 in the parts where they highlight a certain new technology, namely alkaline hydrolysis. it may be an excellent technique, but i do not think it should be written five times in a regulation. it's legal overdoing, to say the least. i would also urge you, ladies and gentlemen, to vote against amendment no 36, which is too restrictive compared with the commission's original proposal. as the text is now worded, serious zoonoses, i.e. diseases affecting both animals and humans, would be exempt from this law. so it would only touch animal diseases. this means that serious infectious diseases such as salmonella, listeria and even tuberculosis would be excluded from this regulation. that would be devastating to public health. amendment no 23 excludes, for example, sheep and goats which have been killed by themselves from the specification as risk material. this is clearly contrary to the tse regulation and is probably impossible in practice. i have chosen to support the amendments on the control of household waste as regards the risks of foot-and-mouth disease and swine fever tabled by the green group, namely amendments nos 102 and 103. it is good if we have clear rules, but perhaps the big question in this context is still not really resolved by these amendments. it is the crash in cannibalism and household waste. i would like to conclude in time by thanking the commission and the council working party for a very constructive, creative and fruitful dialogue and my shadow rapporteurs for their assistance and assistance.
in writing. - (sv) we voted in favour of a report which improves the commission's proposal in many respects and tighten up the legislation. we would have preferred a more restrictive approach to meat and bone meal in animal feed. we therefore voted for amendment no 100, which was unfortunately rejected. the amendment requires member states to ensure that meat and bone meal, with the exception of fish meal, does not end up on the animal feed market.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the gue/ngl group could not support the karamanou report on child pornography. we strongly condemn child pornography. we consider this a very serious and very serious crime. that is why we believe that the commission's proposal is better than the report of the committee on legal affairs. it is important that the definitions of child pornography be as distinct as possible in legislation. they must not be allowed to become so large as to impede the work of the police and other authorities. if guardians admit to gross violations of children, their own or others, they should be treated as criminals and not be treated in any way differently. if there is no obligation to report knowledge of actual offences in this area in the member states, we hope that the commission will call for such legislation to be introduced. however, we do not want to encourage so-called moral panic by encouraging citizens to report someone without having relevant suspicions. finally, we are opposed to the proposals put forward by the commission and the committee on age limits. according to the un convention on the rights of the child, a person is considered a child until he or she reaches the age of 18. regardless of age, the degree of vulnerability, the degree of violation and the nature of the abuse must be the guiding principles for the assessment of penalties - this is true regardless of age.

(sv) mr president, the confederal group of the european united left - nordic green left was unable to support the karamanou report on child pornography. we strongly condemn child pornography. we consider it a very important and serious crime. the commission's proposal is therefore better than the report submitted by the committee on legal affairs. it is important to have as clear a definition of child pornography as possible in a legislative text. these definitions should not be so broad as to be detrimental to police cooperation and other activities of the public authorities. if guardians confess to abusing children, their own or others, they should be treated as criminals and not given any form of special treatment. if the member states do not have the responsibility to report knowledge of the commission of such crimes, we hope that the commission will put forward a proposal to create such legislation. we would not, however, like to create a moral panic by inviting citizens to lodge complaints without having any substantiated evidence. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. according to the un convention on the rights of the child, a child is anyone under the age of 18. the basis for calculating penalties should be the harm caused, the degree and form of abuse, not age.

in writing. -{sv) we swedish christian democrats welcome the initiative to combat human trafficking. we believe that there is a real difference between the concepts of 'trafficking in human beings' and 'trafficking in human beings'. it is therefore preferable to deal with these situations in separate legal instruments, a view which is reflected in our vote. as the framework decision concerns the fight against trafficking in human beings and the sanctions against those who commit trafficking in human beings, we also believe that issues such as the treatment of victims should not be dealt with under this instrument. we have therefore decided to abstain on these points.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the report by mr davies, as i see it, is basically about the credibility of the eu's environmental policy and whether we should really try to achieve the objectives that the union has created. ground-level ozone is a serious air pollutant that poses significant problems for public health and the environment. the targets we set for reducing pollution must be binding, otherwise we risk making decisions a blow in the wind. it is therefore not good to have legislation which only speaks of the objectives to be achieved as far as possible, as the council of ministers expresses it. in this respect, we naturally support the committee's proposal for a clearer wording. our group also supports amendments nos 5 and 23, which state that the targets must be fully met by 2020 and that they must not be exceeded by more than 20 days per year until 2010. without these changes, the legislation will be toothless. another important aspect of the davies report is the stricter requirements for information to the public. this type of air pollution has significant effects on public health. it is therefore important that all available information is made publicly available to the public and voluntary organisations. there must be coherence between eu environmental policies in different areas. we have common air quality objectives and are currently negotiating with the council in a compromise on rules for national emission ceilings and large combustion plants. if this policy is to be coherent, binding rules on ozone emissions are also needed. with these words i would like to thank the rapporteur for a very good piece of work, which we will vote for in all its parts.
