mr president, mr president of the council and mr president of the commission! many important issues are pressing the agenda for the summit: the enlargement, the middle east, a possible new round of wto negotiations, etc. remember, this summit has been launched as the first "green" summit. thirty years after the stockholm un environment summit and ten years after the rio summit, the heads of state or government have a historic opportunity, a commitment and an obligation to adopt a credible strategy for sustainable development for the eu. but the strategy must also be the basis for wider global environmental responsibility and commitments ahead of rio+10 in south africa. the commission's proposal is an important first step, a minimum step, i would say. to achieve a real breakthrough towards sustainable development, much, much more is needed. major changes are needed in economic policy, transport and energy policy, chemicals policy, agriculture and fisheries policy. when confidence in the eu is waning, as is clearly shown by ireland's rejection of the treaty of nice, a thorough debate on the future of the eu is needed, a debate without preconditions in which european civil society is involved. the summit must set out such a path. it would be another setback if the summit did not lead to a credible outcome for sustainable development. it's got to be a good result! it's not just about us, it's about future generations. i wish you luck and a successful green summit!
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating the irish people on the result of the referendum on the treaty of nice. only one people in the eu voted against the treaty of nice and it said no. if more people had been allowed to vote, it is likely that more countries, not least sweden, would have voted no. the summit in gothenburg must make it clear that the irish 'no' must be respected. this means that the treaty of nice has failed. it is about the eu having to play by its own rules. the right of member states to reject treaty amendments is central to the eu's architecture. without it, the national democracies lose control of the union. that is why it is so incredible that the swedish government can say that the treaty of nice is firm. in doing so, they ignore the democratic rules of the game. if the swedish parliament had voted against a treaty amendment, would we not want the other eu countries to respect it? i think that the swedish foreign minister should answer that question. the irish voted against the treaty of nice. it is a no to more supranationality, more power for the big eu countries. a no to new steps towards the eu state and the militarisation of the eu. it was thus also a voice for a better and more democratic europe. however, ireland's 'no' was not a 'no' to eu enlargement. it is perfectly possible to enlarge the eu on the basis of the treaty of amsterdam. the influence of the new member states in the institutions can be regulated in the accession treaties as was done in the last enlargement. such a model could correct the discrimination to which some candidate countries were subjected in nice. if the 'no' vote is not respected, it sends a clear message that the will of small countries does not matter and that referendums are only respected if the outcome suits the ruling elite. it would be serious if that happened. i would also like to say a few words about the sustainable development strategy to be discussed in gothenburg. the commission's proposals were surprisingly concrete and good, not least in view of their weak proposals for environmental action programmes earlier this year. the proposal has many clear objectives, such as continued reduction of greenhouse gas emissions, measures to conserve biodiversity, demands to stop traffic growth and to eliminate harmful subsidies such as tobacco cultivation. the risk is now obvious that the concrete elements of the strategy will disappear during the negotiations, while the uncommitted rhetoric will remain. there is already a compromise proposal for conclusions circulating, a non-paper from the swedish government that looks exactly like that. i hope that the swedish government will fight for, and succeed in, maintaining the concrete content of the strategy.

in writing. - i voted against the report. the european parliament has once again failed to distinguish between the tasks of the eu and the competence of the member states. the eu must focus on what is at the heart of its raison d'être and on its essential tasks. the need for such an attitude should be more evident than ever, following the rejection of the treaty of nice by the irish people in a referendum on 7 june. this report deals with silicone implants, with proposals for rules and regulations on this subject, a minimum age of 18 for breast implants and the establishment of a european register of patients. it should be clear that this is not a matter for the european institutions to decide, and that this subject has nothing to do with the main task assigned to the european cooperation project, namely the promotion of peace in our part of the world.
mr president, i would like to thank you. my group shares the view of most other groups, namely that this is a good report and that it is time we had a working time regulation that also applies to road traffic. it's been a while. the last time we discussed supplementary groups - that time it was doctors and some other groups - a similar discussion was held, but the riders were not yet included. it's very good that that category is now covered. our group also believes that it is self-evident to equate employees with riders. we are quite certain that if you do not do this, there will be less careful transport companies who organise themselves in such a way that they can take advantage of formally independent drivers who are in fact a form of employees. we don't want that kind of a scam in our business. our group has tabled two amendments. we will support all of hughes' proposals, but on one point we want to move a little faster. we think that the three-year transitional period is unnecessary. first, the industry has known for a long time that changes were coming, so there has been time to adapt. secondly, there is a point of view which has been under-emphasized, namely consideration for fellow road users. it can be argued that the transitional period requirement is a way of taking account of the industry, but it certainly does not take account of the road users. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of certain substances in the manufacture of certain products. we therefore think it is equally advisable to take the step fully and implement the provisions without a transitional period. this is particularly important in the case of working time regulations relating to night work and other activities which may endanger road safety. we support the report as it is drafted in amendments nos 25 and 26, which are the last amendments and which deal with the removal of the transitional period from the proposal.
thank you, commissioner nielson, for a very detailed and in many respects interesting reply. i think we can all agree that this is one of the most serious epidemics and not only one of the most serious humanitarian disasters, but also one of the most serious economic disasters that has ever hit the world. even a decade ago, experts, based on the transmission routes, sexual habits, and widespread poverty in many african countries, were able to make predictions that showed a spread similar to the one we have today, with tragic consequences. unfortunately, today we can make the same predictions for other parts of the world where the infection reached later, and we get a similar panorama. the only way to go is, of course, prevention, with a mixture of support for those already affected and prevention, in the form of information and education and in the form of efforts to develop a vaccine. i agree with the commissioner, of course, that simply setting up a fund will not solve any problems. i can also agree with many of the requirements for such a mechanism as nielson sets out. at the same time, it is my experience from the building of unaids, on whose board i was, that there was always too little money and too few ambitious steps. during the recovery years, there was virtually no money to support national efforts in the hard-hit countries. at the same time, as mr nielson also pointed out, the normal budgets of the un agencies have been reduced, which means that we are suffering from a serious shortage of money. i am fully convinced that money is needed to achieve success in research and active action at national level. i therefore think that kofi annan's initiative is a good one, provided that this mechanism can be set up in a good and unbureaucratic way. i understand that the commission cannot magically come up with money on its own. however, there was a large surplus in the eu budget last year which has recently been sent back to the member states. it must be possible, in such an extraordinary situation, to get support in the council for a proper contribution. otherwise it would look very strange in the light of the very strong commitment of the commission over the last year and the initiative that mr nielson, among others, took in september-october 2000. so i'd like to get a little more of nielson's pulse. given that the probes for the new york meeting in just under a month are decent and given that this mechanism can be set up in a good way, what are the plans for us from the european union, both the member states and the eu itself, to contribute a decent amount to ensure that more power is put into the prevention work?
in writing. - (sv) as a representative of the swedish christian democrats, i choose to support the second reading on the working time arrangements for persons performing mobile road transport activities on a professional basis because this proposal is important, especially from the point of view of road safety. i have, however, voted against the following points in the report: the proposal to give the employee at least 24 hours' notice, the proposal to count time spent beside the driver as working time and the proposal to limit working time to a maximum of 8 hours in any 24-hour period in the case of night driving. my vote is on the principle that a good compromise must be found between road safety and reasonable conditions for the contractors.
in writing. -{j) as at first reading, we did not take a position on the skinner report on the minimum safety and health requirements for the use of work equipment by workers at work. this is an area in which the union should not have such a great influence. our basic attitude towards the european union is positive. european integration provides an opportunity to address crossborder issues such as the environment, trade, crossborder mobility, human rights and conflict management. we also believe in the principle of subsidiarity, that decisions should be taken as closely as possible to the people concerned. that is why we are actively studying the question of a constitution for the european union, which would clearly indicate to everyone the distribution of responsibilities. the citizens must be clear that the union should deal only with the issues it knows best - crossborder issues. we are convinced that the european union is not the most appropriate level to adopt legislation indicating, for example, how ladders should be used to carry out work at height. national conditions must be able to be decisive as to how scaffolding is erected and how work is carried out. we believe it is important that the union should concentrate on a few areas where it can really be useful. this field cannot be part of these topics.
in writing. -sv) regulating in detail is not the right solution. it cannot therefore be the responsibility of the union or the european parliament to adopt rules on the inclination of ladders, on how to place them in relation to buildings or on the fact that they cannot rest on a slippery surface. however fair and honourable such legislation may be, it cannot be a matter for european political competence. the european parliament can set the limit by exercising restraint in the cases it is examining and for which it is seeking key competences. a reasonable solution would have been a directive giving member states the responsibility to take appropriate and sufficient measures to ensure a certain level of safety at work. a common market must have certain minimum rules and common objectives to improve health and safety at work. it is, however, reprehensible that the details of how these objectives are to be achieved should be regulated at european level. we, the swedish christian democrats, consider that working towards a clearer division of powers between the european union and the member states is a priority task. european legislation must be free of rigid rules and new legislation must take account of the principle of subsidiarity. european directives should be aimed primarily at setting objectives and showing the way, not at legislating in detail. let the member states decide for themselves how to draw up the rules and regulations concerning the health and safety of workers in order to achieve the requirements laid down in the best possible way. meps, like national parliamentarians, are certainly expected to be proficient in a number of areas and to deal with a wide range of policy issues, but the rules on the setting of scales and support systems can hardly be part of that.
written. - (sv) we swedish christian democrats would like to stress the importance of the possible creation of the european school of diplomacy referred to in article 9 not leading to its replacing the diplomatic training of each of the member states. it is important that the external services of each country have their own basic training so that they can serve their fellow citizens fully, and above all in their own national language.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the swedish presidency for its great respect for and willingness to engage in dialogue with the european parliament. both in this house and in the committees, the presidency has been very much present and very much appreciated. dialogue, openness and participation are the prerequisites for the functioning of democracy. in gothenburg, there was an attempt at dialogue between politicians, various organisations and demonstrators. unfortunately, it was just a friendly attempt. in reality, the heads of state and government were behind the closures, and there was no opportunity for civil society and citizens to follow the negotiations on our common future. the closed rooms of this summit culture are giving rise to frustration and disempowerment, as expressed by peaceful demonstrators who tried to get their message across to the heads of state and government. unfortunately, even a small clique of provocateurs brought destruction and violence. the stones thrown by these provocateurs were not only aimed at the police, but at the whole of democratic society, and at all of us who were really trying to get a message across. that can be very sad indeed. it is also unacceptable that the police fired sharply at the provocateurs. now an investigation has been added to investigate how this could be possible in sweden today. i am also disappointed at the way eu leaders seem to be reacting to the frustration with developments in the eu. i also think that the result of the referendum in ireland has not been taken into account at all. instead, they are trying to make this an irish problem, when in fact it is, of course, a problem for the whole of the eu. the majority of the european parliament today also seems to be making the same mistake, having rejected the council's proposal to set up an open forum and only intending to propose that the next intergovernmental conference be a convention. i do not understand this reluctance to open up the eu, to engage in dialogue with civil society and with citizens. what does the swedish presidency intend to do in the future to bring our colleagues together and make them understand that this is absolutely necessary? of the three e's, it was certainly enlargement that was fought for and achieved. i am very, very pleased that there is now a fixed timetable for the accession procedure. however, i do not know how much gunpowder was poured on sustainable development. at any rate, the results were poor compared with what might have been expected. that was a good proposal put forward by the commission. i do not know whether this is due to the enormous reluctance of the eu's heads of state or government to understand what sustainable development really means. how can we now anchor a plan and a political process for the rio+10 conference in south africa, when we have not presented the concrete commitments on what will happen to the climate objectives after 2010, how to stop overfishing, how to change the devastating agricultural policy we have today? eu leaders must also take on a global responsibility. i believe that is what the rest of the world and the citizens of the european union are demanding. (applause) i would like to thank you.
madam president, i would like to thank you. sweden is sometimes described as a well-organized but not very exciting country. it is a picture that fits quite well with the swedish presidency of the european union. the presidency has worked well in terms of organisation. swedish diplomats have worked hard to forge various compromises. politically, it's been a low profile. everything has been done to show that sweden is a loyal eu member, both towards the eu system and towards the big countries that dominate the union. this was most clearly seen when the irish voted against the treaty of nice. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament. the treaty of nice would stand. it is in fact an attack on the right of any small country to stop changes to the treaty if it wishes to do so. sometimes compromises have been made at any cost. the new rules on access to documents became so weak that organisations of journalists in europe felt that the current rules were better. the swedish presidency gave priority to enlargement, employment and the environment. the presidency has worked hard on enlargement. the pace of negotiations on how to bring the candidate countries into line with eu rules has been maintained. this has been essential in order to avoid delays in enlargement. the eu's employment policy is often presented as a 'left-wing project' to counter the right-wing policies of monetary union. the stockholm summit addressed the issue, but the only concrete decision that is remembered from it was a decision on the liberalisation of financial markets. it's not much left-wing politics. the presidency has had some bad luck in the environment. the work on the most important environmental problem, the climate issue, has been effectively sabotaged by the new president of the united states. the gothenburg summit was able to adopt a good strategy for sustainable development, but when it came to the point, most of the radical environmental requirements had been removed from the text. in some areas the presidency has been successful. in addition to the enlargement negotiations, the decision to remove customs duties for the poorest countries is a clear step forward. the work of the presidency against trafficking in women and for peace in the balkans is also commendable. as usual, however, many of the important decisions are made more quietly. sweden has continued to work loyally towards the militarisation of the eu and thus in practice towards the abolition of sweden's neutrality policy. during this period, sweden and the other nordic countries have joined the schengen agreement, which is the eu's main tool for building new, hard borders against those living outside the union. in this area there is a real stain on the swedish presidency. sweden voted in favour of the proposal on fines for transport liability, a proposal which would involve a kind of privatisation of asylum policy, which in turn would mean that airlines and other carriers would be penalised if they do not sort out refugees who do not have their papers in order when they apply for entry into the eu. when the presidency was inaugurated, göran persson stood here and spoke of a policy of solidarity with refugees. we can only regret that the government has since then in practice acted in the opposite way. finally, a few words about gothenburg: in gothenburg, small groups of militants took the opportunity to attack the police and cause extensive damage. whatever these groups claim to stand for, it is an action that can never be defended. throwing stones and causing harm is not politics. it's crime. it should not be hidden, however, that in gothenburg there were also tens of thousands of people who demonstrated peacefully and with dignity for a better europe than today's with emu and schengen. in fact, what 50,000 people do is more important than what 300 violent people do!

observation that it risks being remembered for the anarchists who tried to stop the meeting by throwing stones and who mercilessly smashed the pleasant main street of gothenburg and several good cooperation. this is accompanied by a burgeoning debate which raises the big questions about the future of european cooperation. sometimes there is more talk about how the decision-making processes should be organised than about what we should cooperate on and what the eu should not intervene in. i think that all too often we talk above the heads of the citizens and far beyond their everyday lives. the rejection of the nice treaty by ireland is a lesson for us all and a concern not just for the irish government. the debate on the future of the eu must be conducted in more principled and fundamental terms. that is why it is not so important what we say and do here, but that we discuss it more in our constituencies. in my own country, if i may use an example, it is important to take advantage of the interest that the presidency has generated. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european union. this means that sweden must now also tackle the question of the euro. the single currency is so central to eu cooperation that the social democratic party and the swedish government must take responsibility for sweden's membership of emu. in other countries, other issues must be the focus. i think it is important that europeans should stand up for free markets and for a europe that is more open to the outside world. in some member states, europe-lovers should take greater responsibility for making enlargement a reality. since the swedish presidency was striving to be a presidency for the whole of the union, the most important thing was that we achieved a breakthrough in the enlargement negotiations. the fact that it was not done with greater enthusiasm and courage is not primarily the fault of the swedish presidency. it was someone else who was braking. i therefore hope that the negotiating results achieved have strengthened a successful enlargement and that the remaining major issues, agricultural and regional policy, will not interfere with and undermine the decisions taken in gothenburg. let us make sure that this concrete thing madam president, i would like to thank you. when i was flying home last week, i got hold of a swedish business newspaper, dagens industri, which does not often praise the swedish government. they gave the presidency a grade, and it was headlined "commendably accepted". i wish i could say the same. the swedish presidency had three important, big ambitions. there has been a lot of talk about enlargement. there is no doubt that this matter was handled very well. the important thing now for the future of us in this parliament, for sweden and other countries in the council, is to keep up the pace so that we can live up to the objectives we have set ourselves. as for the jobs, the results may not have been so great. the deregulation was definitely not great, because many on the right were disappointed that there was not enough deregulation. it was about taking the baton forward in the important process of jobs and growth. a few new targets were set. among other things, family policy and the older workforce were discussed. only a small part of this is available on the labour market today, and this needs to change for the future. there are two perspectives on sustainable development. one is a comparison with the commission document, and it is clear that in that perspective the final result was not quite achieved. the second is in relation to the situation. clear, tangible improvements have been made and, most importantly, environmental issues are being brought into the lisbon process. what is the problem with environmental issues today? well, it's that they're on the sidelines, that they always come last. they will now be weighed against economic and social policy each year in a continuous process. that, if anything, is a success. i would also like to say something about global responsibility. the transport policy, the agricultural policy and the fisheries policy were highlighted. i would also like to mention transparency, information and consultation, and especially foreign and security policy. i would like to say that it is gratifying that sweden has taken on such a great responsibility in this area. it is also gratifying that the swedish people have understood that this is not a matter of militarization but that it is, on the contrary, a matter of creating peace and stability in a future europe and its border areas. there is a very high degree of agreement on this issue, if opinion polls in sweden are to be believed. the debate on the future. the convention? yes, but it's not enough to have a convention. we need to broaden the debate beyond a convention to bring it to civil society. if we are to have the debate with civil society, we must do as prime minister persson said. we cannot start with institutional questions, but we must start with what we want the eu to do in five, ten, fifteen years' time and how we are going to do it. the institutional questions are then placed last. ireland, please. we must respect ireland, but we must also respect the other member states. some say we should stop now! shouldn't the swedish parliament or the other parliaments be allowed to discuss the treaty of nice? what is the view of democracy that we should stop immediately because the irish people said no? other peoples also have the right to discuss this matter in their parliaments or in the way they choose, do they not? then we shall see in 2002 what the final result will be, and it is up to the irish people to judge the situation at that time. and finally, a word about the riots: i fully support what enrique barón crespo said. we must distinguish between those who throw stones, who are petty, and those who want to express an opinion on the continuing work of the european union. we shall stimulate debate with civil society, but we shall be tough on those whose only argument is a stone in their hand.

commission. – as mr stenmarck mentioned, two complaints were indeed lodged in 1997 alleging abuse of a dominant position by the port of helsingborg. the complaints have been under active investigation by the commission since that time. this case raises many complex factual and legal issues and the markets involved have potentially changed significantly in the light of the opening of the øresund bridge in july 2000. the commission is fully aware of the situation of the parties. a series of meetings has been held recently with the main parties concerned, partly to obtain their views on recent market developments and partly to establish whether they see any prospect of reaching a mutually acceptable settlement. if there is no settlement, the commission will continue its investigation with a view to completing this as soon as possible.
i would first like to thank the commissioner for his reply. in the present case, where the complaint was lodged with the commission in 1996, that is to say five years ago, there is really only one acceptable response, namely that the commission should immediately take a position. the commissioner has not promised that, so i cannot say that i am satisfied with the answer. this is a matter of very great principle, namely that excessive charges have been levied on a port which is alleged to be in a dominant position. i would like to stress that i have no opinion on whether this is right or wrong, but all parties are waiting for the commission's decision and all parties are also highly dependent on the commission's decision. i think it is unreasonable that it should take more than five years for the commission to give its opinion. i would therefore like to ask the commission: when is a final decision expected to be made?

mr president, i would like to thank you. i am one of those who are rather disappointed with both the commission proposal and the kara report, because i think they concentrate entirely on the needs of the financial markets and ignore the needs of the employed and the future pensioners. i would like to point out two aspects in particular which i think are missing. when it comes to pensions, the issue of security is crucial for people. there should be at least a value guarantee ensuring that the capital contributed can be paid out even if it does not grow or increase. what do you do, for example, if such pension funds become insolvent, go bankrupt and fail to meet their obligations? there is not a word about this in the report. it is almost even more strange that another important aspect is missing. when we started discussing supplementary pensions, it was said that this was an important eu issue, because we have a common labour market and we expect workers to move between the different european labour markets. if so, however, they face many problems with regard to their pension. today it is very difficult to accumulate pension rights earned in different countries one after the other. how do you do that? this question is not answered. in many countries, long periods of accrual are applied. how do you deal with that in a mobile labour market? in many countries there are rules that impose double taxation. how do you solve it? there are a number of such questions. i therefore think that we would need a completely new proposal from the commission which takes its starting point from the needs of employees and pensioners and does not look only one-sidedly at the needs of the capital market.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to congratulate rapporteur rothe. i think she has done an excellent job and has gradually brought this process to an increasingly positive conclusion. i agree with mr turmes that the decision which we will hopefully take tomorrow is a historic decision. this means that the european union is taking a very important step towards a different energy system, one that takes climate issues seriously and the need to diversify the energy system and ensure that an increasing proportion of our energy comes from energy sources that do not adversely affect nature or the climate. the transition to renewable energy sources is an important step. at the same time, great efforts are needed to make our energy use more efficient. we have set a target in the directive that at least 22% of electricity should come from renewable energy sources. like turmes, i am optimistic in this case. once demand kicks in, we know that performance and quality will increase and costs will decrease. this is a rule of thumb for all new technology. such a development naturally requires that energy policy in general develop in the right direction. it requires the elimination of subsidies for fossil fuels, especially coal, the internalisation of external costs for different energy sources in prices and a real boost to research. mr president, i would like to thank you. the developments we are witnessing are important for europe. it is also important for the candidate countries and for the developing countries. i hope that within the framework of this programme and of the eu's overall activities we will be able to develop systems which support both the candidate countries and the developing countries in this direction.
in writing. (sv) the the question concerns the institutional rules applicable to european large capital when it acquires large companies. on the one hand, there is the anglo-saxon tradition of laissez-faire and open market principles, and on the other hand, the protectionist tradition of continental europe. it is no coincidence that one proposal was put forward by a german colleague and the counter-proposal in yesterday's debate was supported mainly by british colleagues. although mr lehne's proposal contains some protective-patriarchal elements for employees of large companies, we consider that none of the alternative proposals serve the interests of the workers. we reject both and demand that the commission return to the issue and put forward a proposal which ensures that the interests of employees and consumers are protected when large companies are bought up.
in writing. - (sv) the swedish christian democrats welcome the directive on the production of electricity from renewable energy sources. we accept that electricity produced by burning the biodegradable fraction of household waste should be included in the national indicative targets. we would like, however, that the incineration of unsorted household waste should not be included as a source of electricity produced from renewable energy sources. that is why we do not accept that the incineration of unsorted household waste is supported. we believe that the compromise which has been negotiated with the council on this subject is too weak in substance. in this context the council has taken a decision, but not in relation to its scope. we believe that the importance of adopting the directive as soon as possible, particularly in view of the pressing role that the eu has and must continue to play in the future climate change discussions, is nevertheless sufficient motivation to support the compromise that has been negotiated with the council in this area. we therefore choose to support the compromise.
mr president, commissioners, the council is in session! the fact that serbia's former dictator slobodan milošević is now on trial in the hague is a great victory for all who believe in human rights and international justice. it shows that genocide and ethnic cleansing cannot be accepted by the international community. the world has become a cooler place for dictators. however, the world's human rights situation does not look much warmer. every day human dignity is violated, every minute individuals and groups are subjected to various types of abuse torture, harassment, oppression. the eu has recently significantly increased its commitment to human rights, and it is clear that the eu is a community of values. this is reflected internally and externally, both in our new charter of fundamental rights and in the requirements we are placing on the applicant countries. the eu is united in its criticism of the death penalty, whether it is used in dictatorships such as china, iraq and saudi arabia or in democracies such as the united states. however, there is still room for improvement in coordination between the eu institutions and between the eu and the member states. in this year's report on human rights in the world, mr wuori has chosen to highlight the issue of freedom of expression. no democracy can work without free and independent debate, journalists can feel safe and citizens can express their opinions. but that is not the case. journalists are killed, threatened and harassed. the situation is particularly alarming in iran, belarus, china, colombia and cuba, but developments in russia are also cause for concern. in several countries, the regime tries to prevent or restrict citizens' access to the internet by censoring or closing websites. these regimes do so because they know that the internet makes it increasingly difficult to suppress their people. the internet is thus an important weapon in the fight for a better world. mr president, liberals all over the world have stood and still stand on the barricades to fight for freedom of speech and human rights everywhere and for everyone. we'll keep it going. my group would like to thank mr wuori for mr president, i would like to thank you. there is a great deal of agreement here about our position on climate issues, which i think is very important. we must use this consensus to put maximum pressure on the countries which are now deciding the future of the kyoto protocol. we are in fact risking a kind of collapse in the climate negotiations, a collapse that would risk taking us back several years in development. the worst part is that those who are causing the collapse, especially the united states, have no alternative to offer. therefore, everything must now be done to save what can be saved. the european union also has a credibility problem. as previous speakers have said, many of our member states are also not able to meet the requirements of the kyoto protocol. we know that these requirements alone are insufficient to meet the challenges of climate change. what we see in practice when we meet the council of ministers, for example in settlements on incineration plants and national emission ceilings, is that they are not prepared to take the decisions that are taken. when the decision was taken in gothenburg on a strategy for sustainable development, several of the most important points had been removed. of course, the eu's credibility would also be enhanced if we showed that we are prepared to meet the demands we ourselves want to meet. i think this is important in the run-up to the meetings we are now going to have with other countries to decide on the future of the kyoto protocol.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. first of all, let me thank the rapporteur, mr costa neves, for his proposal and for the good cooperation. we have reached a common position in the committee on budgets on the vast majority of the subjects. if i may guess, i think that this year the budget debate will be dominated by questions of principle rather than by individual budget lines, questions which will be even more important in the coming years. i am thinking in particular of the enlargement and the development of the common foreign and security policy and its budgetary implications. i am thinking of the transformation of agricultural and fisheries policy. i am also thinking of internal reform, not least the commission's ability to implement the decisions of the budgetary authority. the premise is rather troubling. public confidence in the eu is waning in many member states. at the same time, we are facing perhaps the most important change ever: eastern expansion. in the face of this challenge, the eu's ability to implement its priorities must be significantly strengthened. it is therefore worrying, even if some national budget ministers applaud it, that the rate of implementation of the budget is so low that we now have to pay back 11 billion euros to the member states. the problem of promises of aid and of measures which are delayed or not implemented is a black-foot for the whole union. it is therefore good that the commission now wants to increase payments and reform the work towards a more modern, efficient and transparent management. the council cannot change that. as far as the substance is concerned, i would first like to refer to agricultural policy. although this is a big and complex issue, we must now accelerate the pace of change. before we adopt the 2002 budget, and especially before we fully conclude the negotiations with the future member states, we must be clearer about the future agricultural policy. in this context, i would like to draw attention to the view of the committee on agriculture. in its amendments, this committee wants to confirm that agricultural expenditure must be kept within the budgetary framework, but it also wants, despite technical difficulties, not least in the light of enlargement, to reallocate aid to the comprehensive rural development, category 1b. that is good, and i think we should continue along that path. the question we are asking now is why the commission wants such a large reserve for bse and foot-and-mouth disease when it can still adjust the draft budget itself. we want to wait on this until we have more answers to our questions before we take a further position. let me also make a very brief comment on the rupture of the fisheries agreement with morocco. the fact that this cannot be done has consequences for the fishing fleets of several countries. i am one of those who are somewhat sceptical about the eu's large subsidies in the agricultural and fisheries policies, but we must of course take into account the problems of fishermen who have counted on new opportunities for their livelihood. we must support an offensive restructuring and consider the possibility of creating funds for this under budget headings other than category 4. in addition, the commission will have to submit detailed proposals. although the reconciliation we are now going to discuss concerns mainly headings 1, 2 and 5, we would like to take this opportunity to make a few other remarks. last year parliament called for an extra effort to be made on employment and small businesses. after the stockholm summit, the lisbon process has been further developed and there is every reason to stick to this priority. we must also eliminate some of the problems that bartho pronk mentioned earlier. in addition, we are now pointing in particular to the area of e-learning which we believe must be given resources in order to develop into the key area it must become if we are to both increase growth and ensure that it is more evenly distributed across europe. finally, the commission we are also concerned about the external measures. they have been the main difficulty in 2000 and 2001. these problems have not disappeared. we have made huge commitments to kosovo and yugoslavia, while we have great and backward needs in other, even poorer parts of the world. this is one of our most important tasks, but we have major problems that we must be able to solve in order to implement everything we have promised. although there are great challenges ahead, i believe that with goodwill we will be able to solve them together.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we are all familiar with the debate on the eu's own resources and the eu tax. it comes back every year. the positions are well known and somehow it seems rather unnecessary to repeat the debate and decisions year after year. apart from the question of principle, one may wonder whether it is really higher and new taxes that the eu member states and the eu citizens need most. we're all heavily taxed. some of the member states are among the most heavily taxed in the world. i myself come from one of them. if you look at these countries, they are hardly the ones that have had the highest economic growth in the last decade. it is true that the report says that the tax will not increase in total. however, this is theoretical reasoning that has very little to do with practical reality. i also think that it is fair to say that if you introduce a tax, especially as in this case a completely new level of tax, it will certainly increase. it's about to become inevitable. i also note that the rapporteur wants to 'strengthen the links between the union and its citizens'. it is a matter of great heart and urgency, but i think it should be done in a very different way. i very much doubt that citizens would feel that their ties to the eu are strengthened by receiving higher taxes than they already have.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. the background to this report and to the commission communication it deals with is a number of very serious accidents which have occurred in the mining industry in recent years. in 1998, a mine collapse in spain had very serious ecological consequences. another similar accident occurred in romania in 2000. both of these accidents had international environmental consequences. in recent years we have also seen similar but less extensive accidents with collapsing mine dams, for example in northern sweden and in wales. this has led the eu to review its legislation on this type of mining. at the same time, we have very large quantities of old mining waste stored in various places, which release heavy metals and other toxins into the environment. the content of this waste and the extent of the problem are not fully understood. so mine waste is one of our major environmental problems. safety in mining is also a major environmental problem. the commission's proposal sets out its ideas on how to improve eu legislation in this area. in the report we have essentially concluded that the approach is good. we have a number of views on what we think this should contain more concretely and what we want the commission to propose. we stress that the responsibility for accidents should be borne exclusively by the company, including the environmental consequences, and that the mining industry should be included in the forthcoming liability directive. we want more research in this area. there are major shortcomings in the research on the management of mining waste, and we want to develop the techniques currently available in different countries. we also want the eu to review its own funding for projects in this area. as regards the main proposals, my report proposes that the current inventory should also include the candidate countries. the inventory involves going through old mining waste and seeing where it causes serious environmental problems. we know that the situation in many candidate countries may be more serious than in the present member states. we also stress that we want a common methodology to enable results to be compared in different directions. we believe that in cases where it is found that there are major problems, these must also be followed up with real action plans. the main element of the commission's proposal is the proposal for a new framework directive on the management of mining waste. i, as rapporteur, and the committee on the environment, consider that to be the right approach. a specific directive is needed, as neither the waste directive nor the landfill directive is particularly suited to the management of mining waste. we also stress that this directive must deal with both old mining waste and existing production in progress. this directive should also include a rule requiring member states to have legislation on the restoration of installations that have been used for mining. such legislation exists today in several member states but not in all, especially not in the candidate countries. we want to have special rules on dust safety that directly affect these accidents. we also want a direct ban on the method used in romania, in baia mare, where cyanide was used in open pits in the extraction of metals. it is an extremely dangerous method which risks seriously damaging the surrounding environment if the dam breaks, as was the case in romania. a third part of the proposals is to review the so-called seveso ii directive. the aim is to extend the scope of that directive to include requirements for contingency plans and information for mining. the only amendment to the report in this area is from the conservative ppe-de group. the amendment proposes that the obligation under the seveso ii directive be limited to mining operations involving the extraction of ore, i.e. metal extraction. i think it is premature to draw such a line before the commission's proposal has even been tabled. it would be unwise to do so at this time. instead, all types of mining should be kept open to the scope of this directive. when we discussed this matter in committee, there was a great deal of agreement. i thank my colleagues for their comments and for the support they have given me. we have also had very good cooperation with the commission and are now looking forward to receiving the first of the promised proposals quite soon, not least this revision of the seveso ii directive. we hope that the commission will listen to us on this point in future proposals. thank you for your cooperation.

in writing. - i voted against the report. the swedish christian democrats reject points 5 and 11, which provide for the european union to have its own taxation system. we refuse to raise the level of citizens' taxes. swedish taxpayers pay taxes to the municipality, the region and the state. the creation of an additional budgetary authority at european level will inevitably lead to higher taxes in the long term. we cannot support such a development. if the eu achieves what the report calls 'financial autonomy', it will achieve a degree of political independence from the member states and their governments, with all the dangers and lack of control that this entails. we cannot support such a development.
in writing. - (sv) as a swedish christian democrat i voted against paragraphs 78 and 82-84, which are aimed at administering the legislation of the member states in the field of family policy. this is a national issue, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, which has no place in a report on human rights in europe. i also voted against paragraphs 119 and 120 concerning the right to vote in the european parliament for persons who do not belong to one of the eu member states. responsibilities and obligations should be linked to rights and eligibility. this must be seen as a self-evident principle and rule of life.

mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you to the rapporteur for a good report! the main conclusion can be seen as simple: echelon is real. however, it took a long time before we could determine that. let me highlight two aspects of the report. in my view, there is not enough concern about privacy and the protection of individuals. it is therefore good that the rapporteur has specifically highlighted these issues in his speech. the economic interests of the companies are of course very important, as has also been pointed out, but they are not everything. there are indications that the surveillance system is not only used for industrial espionage, but also for eavesdropping on individuals or organisations. echelon has the capability to intercept communications such as e-mails, faxes and phone calls between individuals. british journalist duncan campbell even suggested that echelon was being used to intercept amnesty and the red cross. if this is true, it is extremely serious. in this case, the eu must be able to act, as mentioned earlier, as well as national parliaments and governments. in a democratic state governed by the rule of law, the protection of the individual must always come first, as the president-in-office of the council has pointed out earlier. my second comment is about encryption. encryption is the only effective defense against eavesdropping. however, the encryption technology is still in its infancy and there are several export barriers for civilian encryption systems. however, the large-scale exchange of information in europe raises the issue of common standards and rules for encryption. the eu institutions must be able to act together on this issue.
in writing. we have not voted in favour of mr brok's resolution. the resolution reflects a development of european cooperation which we do not support. we do not believe that the development of a common defence is in the interests of either the present or future member states and we are opposed to involving nato in a resolution on eu enlargement. we think that the resolution overlooks too lightly the problems that schengen is causing to a number of candidate countries, for example, with regard to regional cohesion in the form of trade and other relations with neighbouring states outside the union, including the question of the future of kaliningrad, which is not seriously addressed by the rapporteur. the resolution also barely addresses the problems raised by the transitional arrangements that the eu requires, for example on the free movement of workers. such a system will, on the one hand, prolong the problem of illegal labour from candidate countries in the present member states and, on the other hand, give the population of the candidate countries a feeling of being second-class members of the eu. we also think that the resolution is weak when it deals with the agricultural and structural funds. we welcome the rapporteur's emphasis on the need to include both the environmental and the social consequences of the regulations, but if we want enlargement to be fair, a minor adjustment in these two fundamental areas is far from sufficient. finally, the author's arrogance towards the population is absolutely incomprehensible and we want to emphasize the right of the population of any country to decide for itself.

in writing. - (da) we voted against the report of the temporary committee on the echelon interception system. the report has one positive aspect, but there are far too many negative aspects. the good news is that the commission has acknowledged echelon's existence. among the negative aspects, we would like to highlight the following three points: 1. the despite the commission's conclusion on the actual existence of echelon, it was unable to explain its precise political consequences. we believe that this is the least that can be expected from a politically elected assembly. 2. the the commission has decided to concentrate on the illegal interception of private companies and it completely ignores the serious violation of the right to privacy, as stated in the european convention on human rights. the commission has no other political conclusions to draw here than to point out that the obligation laid down in the convention on human rights to guarantee the fundamental rights of citizens will also benefit all citizens, who are invited, through encryption, to protect themselves against illegal interception. 3. the the commission notes that a member state is participating in the eschelon cooperation on illegal wiretapping, but it does not draw any political conclusions from this either. on the contrary, it proposes to speed up the process of creating a common european cooperation for intelligence research - a cooperation in which britain, as part of echelon, can freely participate if it so wishes. we are disappointed with the outcome of the committee's work. parliament has not lived up to its responsibilities in strengthening human rights. the struggle for human rights is clearly more visible in official speeches. when it comes to taking concrete action, the rights of citizens are overshadowed by private economic interests.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. we are all affected daily by the risks of the new computer crimes. it happens every time we make a credit card transaction, pick up the phone, open an email, or go out and fly. it is about the traditional crimes that reach us with the help of information technology: child pornography, counterfeiting, xenophobia, trafficking in drugs, people, organs, weapons, etc. however, it is also about new types of crime against the functioning of society: virus spreading, illegal intrusion, hacking, denial of service attacks, etc. traditional methods of combating crime are not enough, even if online crime is as criminal as offline crime. the underworld is threatening society with new methods from a digital platform. the criminals exploit the differences in legislation between member states, and the eu must stand together. the commission has put forward proposals which the committee is encouraging and developing. we call for common definitions to resolve conflicts of jurisdiction, the approximation of criminal and procedural law and the mutual recognition of pre-trial procedures. we want to make europol and eurojust more efficient, but at the same time we want to set clear limits. now, there are also controversial demands for the interception, recording and storage of our communications. the committee calls for the introduction of a european standardisation system for the quality of food and for the establishment of a european standardisation body for foodstuffs. the committee also calls for the prohibition of such privacy attacks, unless the measure meets all the following requirements: it is necessary, it is well justified, it is the only possible solution, it is proportionate to the crime, it is limited in time and it is decided by a court. nobody wants a big brother society. we in the european union must follow our own demand for cheap internet access for all citizens, which means that we cannot pass the costs of this on to businesses. we want to encourage voluntary cooperation and have therefore proposed the creation of an open forum where the experiences of different actors can come together and create synergies, a forum where understanding can increase, awareness of these problems can be developed and best practices promoted. this is where industry, law enforcement and consumers can develop a code of conduct and develop early warning and crisis management systems. preventive technologies and protective measures can be developed and disseminated here. we also need coordination within the eu to ensure that our actions are not disrupted at global level. member states and candidate countries should be invited to develop a common tactic and a coherent eu line within international bodies such as the un, the oecd, the council of europe and the g8. the committee was unanimous in its support for the report's message of better respect for privacy and data protection in the face of the need to develop increasingly effective measures against computer-related crime. finally, a big thank you to all those who have helped to draw up this report; you are many, as there are many conflicting interests that have been brought together. the two amendments tabled to the report are brief. they can be seen as technical. one is to add the word 'terrorism', which was unanimously adopted by the committee as an oral amendment, and then somehow dropped from the text for the plenary. the word 'terrorism' should of course be included in a report on cybercrime. we also want to change the term 'labelling'. this amendment is proposed because 'labelling' is clearly less comprehensive than 'certification', which we want to introduce. the term we use in english, "labeling", gets a little weird in translation. so this is mainly a translation issue. i now come to the cappato report. since i do not have much time, i will devote myself entirely to the opt-in/opt-out debate. it is too early to abolish subsidiarity and force everyone to abandon the status quo of freedom of choice and to introduce an opt-in clause instead. it is a matter of the european parliament. we can have a new 'cucumber debate' with criticism of the eu's regulatory activities. this action could harm future legislation in the field of information technology and make our existing legislation inconsistent, as it would be contrary to two heavy-duty directives in the field of information technology. it would also damage the debate on constitutional issues, as such action could lead to a loss of confidence in the eu. this applies in particular to the european parliament, if we adopt inconsistent positions. we are in the midst of a process of technological development, with filters and broadband, which could make this opt-in/opt-out debate in three years old and outdated. i do not think we should be stuck in a position of obsolescence and at the same time abolish the option of choice without gaining any added value. a european opt-in solution does not eliminate spam, as much of it comes from sources outside the eu. the united states itself, for example, has not harmonised its legislation. opt-in brings with it problems of demarcation between commercial information on the one hand and community information and ngos on the other. it could also hinder the development of online newspapers and create a nuisance for small businesses who have to store their information in case they were prosecuted and how would the punishment be meted out? finally, mr president, we politicians should not legislate as soon as we see a phenomenon we do not like. we politicians do not always know best what is best for each individual and each company at each particular moment. we politicians must speed up the development and dissemination of the technical products that clean up spam and the requirement for revision, as the amendment by palacio vallelersundi does. finally, we politicians must defend freedom of expression, not unnecessarily and unwarily restrict it.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i am very pleased that the rapporteur, mr ries, and the environment committee have sharpened the commission's proposal on brominated flame retardants. we know that pentabde, octabde and also decabde are resistant, toxic and also bioaccumulative since the discovery of high brominated flame retardants high up in the food chain. it is in swedish environmental research that it has been established that even dekabde is bioaccumulative. the whole group must therefore be banned. the precautionary principle requires that we act now and not wait for the risk assessment which is under way, and which is proposed for the december in amendment no 16. decabde accounts for 80% of the use of brominated flame retardants. waiting until 2006 to ban would mean the release of tons of high-risk chemicals into the environment, which would clearly be contrary to the precautionary principle. i therefore welcome the green group's amendments which seek to ban the whole group of brominated flame retardants now.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. this own-initiative report is certainly not about a new problem, but about a problem which has recently been brought to the attention of the public. the scale of workplace bullying is quite large, according to the dublin foundation. eight per cent of eu citizens, some 12 million inhabitants, have felt that they have been bullied at work in the last 12 months. what is the reason for this and are there any links to be clarified? yes, we know, for example, that unsafe working conditions play a role. we also know that those who are bullied are more prone to stress than others. we know that those with high-stress jobs are also more vulnerable. it is a question of safety at work and of work organisation. what are the effects of bullying? of course, bullying has consequences for the individual, who feels worse, is absent from work more often, and is sick more often. the whole team, even the people who aren't being bullied themselves, are performing worse. bullying has consequences for companies in terms of lower productivity, lower profitability and worse working conditions within companies. i have been asked why the eu should be concerned with these issues and what does this have to do with the community? while bullying at work is a problem common to all member states, the attention paid to it varies greatly from one place to another. in recent years we have increasingly started to talk about more jobs and better workplaces, i.e. quality of working life. quality of working life is about health and safety and about work organisation, and then workplace bullying is a problem. the eu has long had legislation on health and safety at work. what do we need to do? we must first of all review the definition and, in doing so, seek a common definition. we need to get a much better statistical base. the dublin fund and eurostat have a role to play in this respect in the future. we must also work on the open method of coordination. the member states need to develop their legislation, their regulations, etc., and then best practice must prevail, i.e. looking at each other's solutions. the lisbon process also includes quality indicators. how does the legislation come in? should we say in advance that legislation is not needed in this area, since it is linked to an area where the eu is already legislating today, namely the area of health and safety? we are currently having a great debate on health and safety, which means that it is not only the physical but also the psychosocial working environment that matters, and that will become increasingly important in the future changing world of work. women are particularly affected by atypical work, precarious working conditions, etc. the legislative issue is raised in two points in the report, although it does not say that we will definitely have legislation or how such legislation would be designed. the commission is invited to take account of the problem of bullying at work in its communications on a community strategy on health and safety at work and on improving the quality of work and social policy, and in its green paper on corporate social responsibility, and to consider the need for legislative initiatives. it is possible, of course, that the commission will say that we do not need and should not have such legislation. in that case we shall have to make an analysis, but i find it rather incomprehensible to rule out the possibility of legislation in advance. it is likely that the framework directive on the working environment also covers aspects of the psychosocial working environment, but we would like clarification on this point. if we get such clarification, there is no problem, but if not, for example, the scope of the framework directive must be extended to cover the 'new' essential area. in this context, it is also necessary to examine whether any type of regulation or legislative initiative is needed in relation to bullying in the workplace. let me take an example from my own country. we have a framework legislation that states that the employer has a responsibility to address bullying in the workplace when it occurs. the details of how this is to be done are not given, but it is clear that there is an employer's responsibility. paragraph 24 of the report calls on the commission to draw up a green paper on the situation of bullying in the workplace and to submit an action programme. this is probably the most important point in the whole report.

madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the chairman of the committee and the committee for a well-balanced report. the increasing flows of refugees in recent years demonstrate the need for a common refugee policy within the eu, and also globally. the more or less panic-stricken reactions of the various member states to the desire of people to find a refuge, a dominate. it is true that there is a need for common asylum rules within the eu, but there is also a need for a more generous margin of appreciation for individual member states. this is the space that the committee has guaranteed. the committee also strengthens the rights of the individual in accordance with the geneva convention. this concerns the status of children, the possibilities for holding refugees in detention, increased skills among those handling refugee cases and reasonable processing times. it is simply a matter of increasing the certainty of the law. madam president, i in writing. -{v) we are concerned that the concept of safe third countries and countries of origin is about to be introduced at community level. all states are potentially capable of committing human rights violations, and therefore no country can be systematically considered safe. an asylum seeker must have the right to a fair hearing, regardless of his country of origin. the report gives member states the opportunity to decide for themselves whether they wish to use lists of countries listed as safe or not. however, there is reason for concern about this development of the concept of safe country at community level. it is extremely important that we should emphasize together the principles of the geneva convention which require that everyone has the right to an individual examination of his case.
in writing. i voted in favour of this very welcome report, which seeks to defend the inviolability of the dignity of every human being, whatever his or her identity. it is a question of expressing the common fundamental values on which european cooperation must be based. female genital mutilation has irreversible consequences for the health of the victims and can even lead to death. whatever the degree of mutilation inflicted, it can only be defined as an act of violence which violates the fundamental rights of women and girls, the right to personal integrity, physical and psychological health, the right to sexuality and maternity. such violation and mutilation of the human being cannot be justified by respect for a religion such as islam, nor for any customs or initiation rites. religious freedom and female genital mutilation have nothing to do with each other. female genital mutilation is a crime against the dignity of women and children as guaranteed by several international conventions. the criminal laws of the member states regard this practice as a criminal offence and condemn it. it is also contrary to the principles laid down in the european charter of fundamental rights. it is therefore time to criminalise all forms of female genital mutilation in the member states of the union - whether the woman has voluntarily undergone it or not - and to criminalise those who aid, encourage, encourage or advise such practices.
in writing. -sv) we abstained in the vote on the report by mr jan andersson on harassment in employment law. our position in principle towards the european union is positive. european integration gives us the opportunity to solve cross-border problems, for example in the environment, trade, free movement between countries, human rights and conflict resolution. we also believe in the principle of subsidiarity, that is to say, that decisions should be taken as close as possible to the citizens concerned. that is why we are actively working to promote the idea of a european constitution in which the distribution of responsibilities is clear to all. it must be absolutely clear to all citizens that the eu should deal only with the issues it knows best, namely crossborder issues. of course, harassment in the workplace is a problem, and we must do everything possible, one way or another, to combat it. however, we do not believe that the european level is the best place to take action to remedy this. it is important for us that the eu focuses instead on areas of action where it can really do useful work. that cannot be the case here.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in my profession, i meet people with health problems caused by noise. however, both injuries and unpleasant noise experiences are very individual, which makes the noise issue difficult. for example, living near a shooting range with the type of noise it produces can be very unpleasant, even if the range is rarely used. low-frequency noise and its vibrations are very unpleasant to some people. i am convinced that we need more research and knowledge about the noise problem. one of the reasons why our common europe is so fascinating is the many differences that exist in our part of the world. changes in the european landscape environment and changes in nature are examples. rural areas vary from densely populated areas in central europe, such as the netherlands, to sparsely populated areas in northern europe, sweden and finland. we must take this into account when taking action on the noise problem. airplanes, cars and trains move between different countries and environments. it is therefore reasonable that they should meet common noise requirements, but industrial areas and airports are fixed installations and their surroundings change considerably. it is therefore important that we do not get stuck in a future noise legislation that equalises measures from a central european demographic perspective. the noise regulations must allow for flexibility. local and national authorities are better able to take environmental factors into account in the case of fixed noise sources. we must respect the principle of subsidiarity and develop noise control measures that can be applied throughout the eu without excessive local consequences.
madam president, i would like to thank you. support for the european union is declining in virtually every member state. in the candidate countries, enthusiasm for enlargement is waning. trust in the european institutions is low, and the distance between 'judgment in brussels' and 'us at home' can be felt very wide by the individual citizen. we are all painfully aware of this, and i am pleased to find such a great deal of self-criticism in the commission's white paper. the liberal group welcomes the white paper on governance in the eu. these changes are an important part of the major reform work we face in the coming years to make the eu more accessible, efficient and democratic. we are very positive about the approach in the white paper. it identifies the problems and points out where changes are needed. some of the overall proposals are in line with what the liberals have been pushing for years. we need to increase openness and transparency. the new rules adopted earlier this year under article 255 are a first step in this direction. decision-making processes need to be simplified and made clearer. the regions, including the local level, must be involved at an earlier stage in the decision-making process and we must find mechanisms to consult them regularly. after all, it is in the regions and municipalities that the majority of eu decisions are to be implemented. it is also very positive that the commission wants to work more on framework legislation and strengthen the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality. however, the level of concretisation in the white paper is low. how exactly is this to be achieved? how can the individual citizen feel more involved in decision-making? and, most importantly, how will the commission deal with the change of attitude, the cultural revolution, which must take place within the commission for all reforms to become a reality? we know that many of kinnock's excellent reform proposals met with formidable resistance. bureaucracies the commission is a big bureaucracy change slowly. many are eager to see results. we can't afford to fail. the white paper is now on the table. i hope that many will feel compelled to respond and comment on part two. parliament will draw up a report. it was also very positive to hear that mr prodi proposed a joint interinstitutional working group to follow up on this. i hope that the commission, together with parliament, will also work actively to spread awareness of the white paper and not expect citizens to find it on the website by chance. thank you for this first part. we look forward to part two.
madam president, i would like to thank you. there is every reason to develop ideas on how europe should move forward, but we also need to know what to develop. it should not cover everything, but only those matters which cannot be better managed at national, local or individual level, such as the internal market, the single currency, external and internal security, borders, customs and certain environmental issues. so far, the proposals for democracy are not sufficiently concrete. we therefore need cross-institutional cooperation to move forward. we must base ourselves on a clear division of powers, where political responsibility can be claimed. the objectives need to be clarified and the initiatives adapted accordingly. the possibility of holding accountable is necessary in a democratic system based on the rule of law and it requires a common set of values with clear guidelines. an effective european democracy requires discussions across language and national boundaries and an adapted school and media. the proposed multi-level democracy can contribute to legitimacy and improved trust, but it must not degenerate into negotiating democracy or corporatism, which so often involve corruption. cooperation with civil society can make policy actions more effective, but the role of politicians must be clearly separated from that of civil society. we now hope that the commission will continue to follow the values of the proposal and ask four questions before each initiative. is the measure proportionate, i.e. legal, in relation to the problems to be solved? does the solution reflect european democracy? is the proposal in line with the eu's vision and basic principles? can responsibility be claimed? the more citizens are involved in european cooperation, the better. but do not think that legitimacy itself creates democracy, it is democracy that creates legitimacy.

particular matter, on which parliament has no influence. we think it is totally unacceptable for parliament to take a position on a regulation proposed by the commission on the basis of a document
