mr president, i would like to thank you. ladies and gentlemen, we have already dealt with a very excellent report by mr korhola a few months ago on public access to environmental information. this time it is about public participation in the preparation of plans and programmes relating to the environment. the common feature of these reports is that the commission's proposal is based on the so-called aarhus convention. article 8 of the convention provides that each country shall endeavour to promote the effective participation of the public in the work of public authorities on such rules at a time when it is still possible to influence decisions. article 9 of the convention requires the member states - and i would like to stress this - to provide the public concerned with the possibility, within the framework of their national legislation, of having the validity of decisions of public authorities reviewed by the courts. as a liberal, i am of the opinion that the decisions taken and the proposals drawn up by the public authorities should be made available to all those who are interested and concerned to the greatest possible extent. anything else would be unthinkable in a democracy.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. this is the end of 12 years of dealing with the so-called ucits directives! last february, we had our first treatment. in july, a year and a half later, the common position was finally adopted, and now it is our turn. the first proposal concerns, as you know, the fund company itself and the investments that may be made. the second updates and extends the provisions on the actual management of the funds. both proposals received broad support in parliament at first reading. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. of these, 30 were adopted in whole or in part by the council. the council has accepted in full or in part 20 of the 24 amendments tabled by parliament on ucits-1. the council also made some of its own amendments. the main one concerns otc derivatives, which the commission was opposed to from the outset. the commission also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the basis of article k.3 of the treaty on european union. the council followed suit. in several cases, the council took an even more cautious stance than parliament on otc derivatives, considering that investments in otc must meet stricter quality requirements. as regards the committee system, the common position does contain provisions on committee procedure in the field of financial services. i would stress, however, that there is no obvious link with the proposals put forward by the lamfalussy group. a 1985 directive set up the so-called contact committee to advise the commission. the original commission proposal under discussion today proposes to extend the committee's powers. the commission proposes that the committee be given new powers. parliament adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia. similarly, both the commission and the council accepted parliament's demand for limitation of delegation to the committee. i believe that parliament has thereby accepted the use of the committee system and has also successfully changed the scope of its powers. i therefore see no reason to bring this matter up again at this time. there is, however, an amendment tabled by mr goebbels, mr jonckheer and mr herzog. if it were to be accepted, i fear we would be faced with a settlement which we do not know where it might end. the lamfalussy settlement risks being delayed and stopping the ucits directives. no one can wish this! i hope goebbels shares my opinion. in ucits ii, the council has largely taken over parliament's comments at first reading. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. the most controversial issue concerned the capital requirements for management companies. the council's final compromise is in principle in line with parliament's position. the committee also calls for the introduction of a european standardisation system for the quality of foodstuffs. there are two reasons for that: firstly, the council's compromise is very fragile, and it is in no one's interest to open pandora's box by introducing detailed, technical or politically sensitive amendments. a compromise would delay this review, which is already needed, and it might also stop the legislation. secondly, there is a good balance between investor protection and freedom of investment in the two common positions. as i said, most of the amendments we proposed last time have been introduced. there are, however, some unresolved issues. these, in my view, can be solved more easily in a broader context. the committee was kind enough to follow my recommendations. therefore, there is only one proposed amendment to ucits-1 with guidelines for a future review. it is also proposed that existing funds should not have to comply with the new legislation during a transitional period, the so-called grandfather clause. a proposal to amend ucits-2 is presented to coordinate the periods for the review of capital requirements and the general review. after the committee's consideration, i have had contacts with the council, as recently as a few hours ago, and with the commission. i therefore propose, together with mr goebbels, a new amendment which reduces the transitional period. this will enable the ucits directives to be implemented in the member states by 2005, the year in which the financial action plan is to be implemented. this is to avoid compromise. the work on the renewal of the regulatory framework for funds has been going on for 12 years and is a clear example of the need to reform decision-making in this area. if europe is to have a chance in competition with, say, the us economy, changes of a more technical nature must be made much easier and faster. there is now a good balance between a well-functioning market and reasonably secure consumer protection. parliament has been pushy and has shown a capacity to act quickly and to compromise when it really counts. let us continue on the path we have taken! thoughtful, fast and effective legislation - that's what europe needs! i would like to conclude by thanking all those involved: the commissioner, his skilled and kind staff, the colleagues who have all really stood by us during this difficult period. i hope that tomorrow we can put an end to 12 years of work for the benefit of europe's consumers and investors.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i recommend that the urgency procedure be applied to this document, as it would also make it easier for china to participate in the forthcoming meeting in doha.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. the same argument for urgency as in the previous case applies in this case, namely that taiwan could also participate in the doha meeting.
the committee on women's rights has already adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you. gender equality is a prerequisite for economic growth. 25 million fewer women than men participate in the european labour market. this is a huge untapped resource which must be tapped to achieve the lisbon employment targets and to ensure economic growth. at the same time, the birth rate is falling dramatically. demographers predict that europe's workforce will be made up of significantly fewer people in the future. thus, women will have to work more and have more children. how are we gonna make this equation work? a basic prerequisite is a gender perspective in all four pillars of the employment guidelines, and i am therefore very pleased that barbara weiler has been so responsive to the proposals put forward by the committee on women's rights and gender equality. perhaps most importantly, ensuring that european women and men can reconcile work with children and family. member states must provide childcare and offer parental leave. european women should not be forced to choose between work and family. the commission's focus on better jobs is very encouraging. the precondition for more women in the workforce is precisely better jobs and better working conditions for women. uncertain, low-paid, low-skilled jobs, where women are overrepresented today, are not attractive. quality, which is the constant theme of the 2000 guidelines, must also apply to women's work. overcoming gender discrimination in employment and social security systems is a huge challenge. discrimination against women in the labour market is not only unproductive, it also destroys opportunities for individual women, for women as a group and for society as a whole. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to promote the integration of women into the labour market. gender equality is an investment.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to thank barbara weiler for a very good report. the commission reports which have been the basis of this debate assume that there will be no major changes this year, as a major evaluation of the first five-year period will be carried out in the coming year. we therefore do not want to start making changes now. this is of course also the case in the present report, which is about what we have discussed in previous years. i think the rapporteur adds important aspects: quality of work, education, vocational training, lifelong learning, gender equality, etc. the committee on economic and monetary affairs tabled a number of amendments to the report, some of which were adopted and incorporated into the report. i am concerned about these amendments because some of them put such a strong emphasis on price stability policy, budgetary restraint policy and the like. i'm concerned because we're entering an economy that's not as strong anymore. the luxembourg process has been very much focused on improving the supply of labour, but this requires that there are jobs, and that the economic policies pursued create jobs and counteract the tendency towards economic recession. i therefore believe that the amendments in question, as they stand, are contrary to a european employment strategy. i would also like to say a few words about the future. a number of evaluations are underway. i have had contact with people working on some of the research projects under way and i think that one of the things that will be highlighted is the lack of implementation of the policy in the member states. there is a relatively broad consensus among those who pursue labour market policies in the member states, but despite this, these policies are not implemented in the member states themselves. the labour market administrations have little to do with the process itself. they're making their own policies. the parliaments have no influence at all, but they are the ones who are supposed to finance the national measures we are calling for. it is therefore extremely important that more attention be paid to the parliaments in particular, but also to the whole political process in the member states, if the result is to be effective. i think that will be the main task of the next evaluation. it's not enough to just emphasize indicators and stuff. the bodies that raise funds and then administer the measures must also play an active role in the process.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for an excellent report. looking back on the relatively short time we have been working on employment policy in the eu, we must say that it has been relatively successful. the employment rate has increased, the unemployment rate has fallen, and that is a success. however, now that the economic situation is deteriorating, the moment of truth has come, and i believe that the need for a coordinated employment policy is even greater than before. although the situation is positive, we still see shortcomings. one shortcoming is the proportion of women in the labour market. not only are women much less than men, but women are over-represented in atypical and precarious jobs, even in countries with very high female employment rates. there are also shortcomings, which have been noted in recent years, in the older workforce. it is a shame that so little of the population between the ages of 55 and 65 is working. in this area, a whole range of measures are needed in the field of working environment, skills development, etc. if we are to raise these figures. there are also very large regional differences, and for groups such as immigrants and the disabled, employment levels are still very low. i welcome the discussion on quality of work, that in addition to creating more jobs, we should also improve the quality of work. however, a wide range of measures is needed in this area. this is of course the case with the working environment, not least the new problems of stress, exclusion, burnout and the like, but it is also the case with lifelong learning. i believe that this is perhaps the most central issue for us to have economic growth and create more jobs in the future, and that is why i support this general right. i would like to see an agreement, a framework agreement, at european level, where the social partners agree on this right. i do not know if that is possible, otherwise the commission will have to act, but that would be the best solution. this right can then be adapted to the development of skills at national level in different sectors. finally, a view on the open method of coordination. the open method of coordination exists alongside legislation in the field of labour law and the working environment. the open method of coordination is good, but it lacks democratic control and transparency. we must resolve the european parliament's influence in this process. its importance has been growing for a long time, it has worked relatively well, but democratic control is lacking.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. like many others, i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr costa neves. we've been working really well together. as the 'shadow rapporteur' of the socialist group, i have always been greeted with a very constructive attitude, and i am grateful for that. at this stage almost all the specific proposals from parliament have been discussed, and i shall therefore deal with a few more fundamental points. the budget is the ultimate expression of democracy and social progress. it is through the budget that we determine that our public institution needs to come in with tax money to somehow impact society, and when it comes to european cross-border issues, it is the eu budget. there is a constant struggle between the ambition to achieve important things and the need to respect budgetary discipline and to ensure that taxpayers really get their money's worth. in the eu, this balancing exercise is unusually complex in that it has to be carried out between two budgetary authorities and within each authority. in the case of the council, it must even be carried out within each of the 15 governments. unfortunately, the problem often becomes that one side of the council, the agriculture ministers and the foreign ministers, pledges a lot of money for agriculture, for example, bse and foot-and-mouth disease, for the conversion of poor regions, for fishing fleets, for the balkans, for afghan refugees, etc., while the other side, the finance ministers, says no to increased spending. the problem is not helped by the fact that those who promise aid are less likely to ensure that the commission, which is to implement all these measures, has the resources necessary to make the promises come true. commitments are often large, but implementation rates are low and funding is inadequate. despite this criticism, i believe that the taxpayers are essentially getting their money's worth. i am a member of parliament for sweden, the most eu-sceptic country in the union, and at the same time the country where the individual taxpayer contributes the most to the eu. this situation makes it not always easy to convince the public that the money is actually being well spent. the myth of the eu as a hopeless colossus is so strong that i am hardly believed at home when i tell you that even for sweden the net cost of the eu is only 1 per cent of the state budget, that the budget in real terms is not increasing and that implementation means that the swedish treasury gets back a large part of its contribution every year. in fact, one tenth of the total budget is paid back to the member states. i think, as i said, we are getting our money's worth, but there are still a lot of shortcomings. we must do a fair bit more in the future to convince the citizens that the funds are being well used. firstly, i think we need to simplify the budgetary procedure itself and make it more transparent. the new financial regulation and the activity-based budgeting should contribute to this. secondly, we must speed up internal reform. we have now put some services into reserve, but the aim is not to slow down but to push forward the reform. i am also convinced that, as commissioner schreyer mentioned in his speech, we will need to increase the flexibility of the budget and increase our ability to react to new developments. to achieve this, i also believe that a new spirit of cooperation between the two arms of the budgetary authority is needed. perhaps the most visible of these shortcomings is in category 4, external expenditure, where the council has added new expenditure year after year: aid to kosovo, aid to serbia, new fisheries agreements, etc. this year we have had a wide-ranging discussion on aid to refugees, the global health fund, other measures to combat aids and so on. in both 2000 and 2001, we solved this problem with the flexibility instrument, which brought us 200 million new jobs per year. the problem this year is that the programmes for kosovo and serbia which forced this flexibility instrument are still to be implemented at a high level, but without us being able to use the 200 million from the flexibility instrument this year. the council nevertheless wants to prevent us from using the funds that are not needed in the fisheries agreements, and i do not understand that. why can't we use them when we actually have high costs for the programs we have agreed on? this is not gonna hold! with this policy the council is forcing us to do something i would like to avoid, namely to say no to some of the large expenditure for the balkans, even though we are of course protecting the most sensitive areas: serbia and kosovo. let us have an open and trusting dialogue on this matter! we understand the council's priorities on the balkans and aids, etc., but we also ask for some understanding that we cannot prioritize all our heart issues. we want to save the balkans! let's get to the negotiating table!
mr president, i would like to thank you. let me begin by thanking carlos costa neves for his very good work on next year's budget. i am pleased to note that for the first time in several years there has been no serious talk of revising the ceiling. this is a sign for me of a rapporteur who really chooses to respect and apply the budgetary plans as a ceiling on expenditure and not as a spending target. i think this bodes well for future negotiations. however, there is reason to be wary of an overly expansive budget, particularly for category 3 and 4. the limited margin in these two categories is a cause for concern, especially as we see in the wake of the terrorist attacks of 11 september an increasing need for humanitarian assistance support to afghanistan, and for action in the area of justice and home affairs. the european union is already costly to the taxpayer. i think it is obvious that we must make sure that it does not become even more expensive. i take it for granted that at this stage parliament is creating room for negotiation with the council. however, there is reason to call for a joint austerity measure before the second reading, both from the council and parliament, in order to be able to deal with the situation created by the terrorist attacks in the united states from a purely budgetary point of view. let me conclude by briefly summarizing the buitenweg report. the rapporteur has made progress in the preparations for enlargement in 2004. this is good, and there is reason to thank her for it. i must say, however, that there is still a great deal to be done to prepare and adapt administration and management for enlargement, which is only two years away.
the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you. the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection warmly supports the council's decision to grant a community guarantee to the european investment bank to cover losses in connection with the lending of funds to environmental projects in the russian part of the baltic sea basin. we are deeply concerned about the environmental situation in this area, which is in the immediate vicinity of the european union. we believe that environmental investments in this area are urgent and in some cases even urgent. we note that the european investment bank has already started negotiations on participation in projects for the treatment of waste water from st petersburg and kaliningrad. it is important that the problems in these areas can be solved as soon as possible. it is also worth pointing out that environmental investment in this area can be more effective than many investments within the community, and that greater positive impacts on the health and environment of citizens of the european union can be achieved. we in the committee on the environment, public health and consumer protection can therefore wholeheartedly support the proposal that has been put forward.

that the ceiling is quite stretched.

mr president, i would like to thank you. what if men in europe earned 75 percent of women's wages? what if every second man was sexually harassed? what if europe's men were overrepresented in precarious, low-skilled part-time jobs and were fired when they told their employers they were going to have children? what if only 20% of the members of the european parliament were men? the view is lifting. now, however, it is the women, not the men, who are discriminated against in this way. "a new way of thinking is needed to solve the problems created by the old way of thinking", said albert einstein. i would like to congratulate hautala for working for a new way of solving problems with the eu's outdated equal treatment directive. the proposal we are voting on tomorrow lays the foundations for effective and clear legislation in the member states to protect women and men from gender discrimination in the world of work. countries must take active action to promote gender equality in practice. perhaps most importantly, hautala's demands that public and private employers in europe must systematically and systematically prevent gender discrimination, harassment and abusive treatment. it is no longer enough to react after employees have complained. employers are encouraged to draw up annual gender equality plans containing statistics on the ratio of women to men at different levels, the pay gap and proposals for measures to address it. employers should be responsible for ensuring that their employees are not harassed, that they have equal pay for work of equal value, career opportunities and, not least, a balance between work and family life. i assume the council is prepared to accept our proposals. only then will we have an instrument to eliminate the widespread discrimination against women in the world of work.
in writing. -{{v) we regard the weiler report in a generally positive light. we particularly appreciate the emphasis on quality of work. however, we find several of the amendments proposed by the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy to be critical, since they contain ideological and circumventing formulations on the market economy and its management. we believe that this is an economic policy which runs counter to the european employment strategy, particularly during periods when the economy and labour demand are not in an expansion phase. since these amendments were adopted by the house, we chose to abstain in the final vote.
mr president, i would like to thank you. just as we in parliament are changing our working methods and the commission is changing its own through its white paper, so, of course, must the council change its own, both in the light of enlargement and because these working methods, as so many point out, are not working so well today. poos has a long and rich history of how the council works. i hope the council will take up his views. i assume that this question will be raised at the convention and that, as verhofstadt said this morning, there will be no taboos. we have no reason, of course, as liberals to have any opinion on the precise form the council should take, since it is not our job to regulate this. there are some issues, however, that we feel we can engage in. transparency is one such issue. we believe that transparency in the work of the eu is important for all institutions. this is a key to regaining citizens' confidence in the european project. today the council sits behind closed doors and passes laws for millions and millions of europeans. this is not acceptable. everyone is talking about it, and everyone is criticizing it. small, small reforms are being made, but they are not enough. the legislation must be made public. a separate department within the council must be set up where ministers meet in full openness and publicity to pass laws. there must also be proper protocols afterwards. the members of this legislative council must, of course, be politicians, not officials, with clear mandates from their respective governments. another issue that concerns us a little bit, and which poos also raised, is all the 'mini-meetings' that are being hoaxed up and are becoming very important. a lot of decisions are made in these meetings with a little bit of dubious status. we hope that they will be toned down and instead turned into working meetings. there is in the council, not so much in the present presidency but in many others, not least in sweden, a growing tendency towards intergovernmentalism and a desire to strengthen the council at the expense of parliament and the commission. we are very concerned about this development, because increased intergovernmentalism and more decisions taken behind closed doors will hardly lead to a more democratic europe.
mr president, i would like to thank you. citizens have the right to know how their government representatives have acted and voted in the council. it is highly unacceptable that legislation is being passed in a democracy behind closed doors on behalf of 380 million people and this without transparency! the main position of the european parliament's report on the reform of the council is therefore contained in paragraph 26 on the need for transparency. it states that the european parliament requires that both deliberations and votes be public when the council acts as legislator, and in particular that public debates be held at the beginning and at the end of all legislative procedures, as provided for in article 8 of the council's rules of procedure, and that article 9 (1) of the council's rules of procedure be applied. the report also mentions the publication of the results of votes and explanations of votes on legislative texts. it is through this future openness that the european union will be democratized. this will lead to greater electoral pressure and increased national parliamentary control of the council. it's time! this in turn brings with it growing demands for the concentration of eu tasks and a need to delimit the union's decision-making powers. in other words, the report we are adopting today has long-term implications. i believe, mr president, that we are thus much closer to the objective formulated by the former president of the commission, jacques santer, in 1995: do less, but do it better.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in doha, the eu must give up its colour. on which side does the eu really stand in the global north-south conflict? it looks pretty bad. despite the outcry from india and malaysia, the least developed countries, the g77 and china, and a host of environmental and solidarity movements, the eu and the us are going to try to push through the wto's free trade doctrine, not just for traditional trade, but for virtually all economic life. the eu's main objective is to open up developing countries' markets to eu companies, commissioner lamy said in a statement earlier today. it is also clear that the eu is on a collision course with the developing countries in the field of trips and gats. in this global struggle, which is more crucial than the fight against terrorism, some so-called progressives believe that the eu could be a tool against neoliberal globalisation. the eu must not be allowed to continue to be a party to the conflict, but it must be able to play a role in the peace process. the 'naked interest policy' is then put forward, i.e. the policy which places the eu alongside the united states against the majority of the world's poorer countries.
mr president, i would like to thank you. international trade is of central importance in creating the conditions for growth and development throughout our world. the rules of this trade determine whether growth and development will be shared by all mankind or will remain with the industrialized world. the désir report is an excellent instrument for achieving more global justice in world trade. the developing countries must be given the same opportunities as the industrialized countries to participate in the joint work of the wto. for me, as a social democrat, global justice is an end in itself. the terrorist attacks in the united states also highlight the need to reduce the basis for hatred and fanaticism. less injustice means more security for all of us. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. democracy can be deepened by the establishment of a parliamentary assembly in the wto. social dumping must be prevented and workers' rights respected. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment from harmful substances. we must stop dumping our surplus food in developing countries and instead give farmers there a chance to develop their production and livelihood opportunities. that way, we can make our contribution to a more just and secure world.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank mr lamassoure for his hard work. i would like to ask the commissioner responsible for enlargement, mr verheugen, the following question: is it possible for turkey to become a member of the european union with the continued turkish occupation of northern cyprus, with the continued influx of turks from anatolia to northern cyprus and with the documented destruction of the historical heritage in the form of monasteries, churches and cultural buildings that has taken place in this part of the island? if in the future it becomes possible for a country like turkey to continue to occupy part of another future eu country, cyprus, and still become a member, what has happened to the democracy and the idea of reconciliation which are the very basis of values and the very soul of european cooperation? if so, where has our credibility in the peace process gone? i also believe that turkey needs to be as ambitious as germany in dealing with the dark chapters of the 20th century. turkey can no longer deny the armenian genocide of one and a half million christians. it cannot be denied that christian assyrians, chaldeans, and syrians were also subjected to genocide, with hundreds of thousands of people being murdered after a 1914 fatwa of holy war against christians by the supreme muslim leader.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. the accession of the people's republic of china to the wto is undoubtedly one of the major experiments and risk projects of our time. the people's republic of china, still a country formally ruled by a communist party, is now to join what might be called the bastion of the free market economy, the wto. if the commission is to be believed, this is a very big victory from the eu's point of view. it is said that this is a one-way process of market liberalisation. the eu, it is argued, will gain a great deal from china's accession to the wto in terms of the possibility of asserting its rights without any major change in its own commitments vis-à-vis china. it is indeed a fairly straightforward process as described in the commission proposal. china will have to adapt. the eu does not have to do much. certain quantitative restrictions concerning china are to be abolished. this is relatively small, however. if the commission's description in paragraph 13 is to be believed, wto accession is good for the chinese economy. i have a few questions about that. the commission is clearly the guardian of the eu's interests, but the question is whether we have not passed the point in the age of globalisation where trade policy can be compared to football matches, where it is a matter of winning and where the other side is not taken into account. i therefore find it quite remarkable that the commission in its proposal does not even suggest that this could still cause problems for the other party, namely the people's republic of china. i shall briefly mention some facts which suggest that there may be some problems for this large but still underdeveloped country. in china, there is criticism. the last time i visited china as president of the delegation of the european parliament to china, i was able to meet, on the sidelines of that visit, the authors of books which are very critical of china's accession to the wto. both books are published by legal publishers and of course in chinese. one of the books is called collision globalisation and china's reality choice. the author, an economist at beijing aeronautical university, argues that china will lose out to a system dominated by international corporations. his sympathies, he says, lie with the lower classes of society and he believes that chinese companies will be swallowed up by the international giants. this will affect the poor groups in china. his main argument is that china is not ripe for wto membership. china's economy is not strong enough and it will be the poorest people who will pay through unemployment and social insecurity. this book got some attention in china when it came out last year. it was notified in the internal gazette of the ministry of commerce. it got a lot of positive reviews. the second book, china in the shadow of globalisation, warns that wto membership could lead to a five-way split in china. this is one of the things that the chinese leaders fear the most. the chaos that could follow wto accession could lead to the dismemberment of the country, which strikes a chord in chinese self-image. even though china is not a democracy and - to put it mildly - does not have full freedom of speech, and even though the chinese government is in favor of wto accession, this type of books are still allowed to be published. it means there is a concern a strong concern in chinese society. there is also a reasonable concern within the ruling party about the consequences. all you have to do is go online. there are many websites: china online, south china morning post and so on. there is a wealth of information on what various observers, including outside china, think could happen as a result of wto membership. for example, china online reported in august this year that it estimates that 18 percent of the total number of employees in the state sector are redundant. they will be even more superfluous through wto accession, it is said. unemployment in urban areas is already estimated at over 15 percent. this report by china online further claims that unemployment will further increase in the context of wto accession. the prospects for new jobs are very slim, as those being laid off are often the older, unskilled workforce. according to china online, 30 percent of the agricultural workforce is redundant, which means that 120 million farmers will have to look for work in the cities. agriculture was one of china's most important negotiating points. china made some concessions. however, many observers still believe that opening up china to imports of agricultural products and foodstuffs from the united states, for example, will lead to enormous changes in rural areas and have enormous consequences. in china, nine hundred million people are employed in agriculture. the chinese government itself, not exaggerating the difficulties, estimates that 9.7 million farmers will lose their jobs because of cheaper grain imports in the seven years following wto accession. i could go on and on citing and referring to various sources which show the difficulties facing china, but i think these examples are sufficient. the purpose of the report was to emphasize once again that i find it strange that the commission is submitting to the council and parliament a document in which not a single word is said to suggest that wto accession could lead to difficulties in the world's most populous country. this is a strange information policy, i think. china's accession to the wto could of course lead to problems for us too if the stability of china is seriously disturbed. what conclusions do i draw? i do not conclude that we should say no. even if china is not a democracy in our sense, i think it is unreasonable for us to try to take on the responsibility of the chinese government and say that what the chinese government has judged to be right would in fact be wrong. i think, however, that we should be aware that we are playing a very risky game. it is a great demand that european companies, now that they are entering china with the freedom that wto membership gives them, take responsibility and not abuse the opportunities they are given. despite these serious criticisms and these serious concerns, i naturally recommend that we vote 'yes'. i have only a short time left to speak on taiwan. it was deliberate, because taiwan is in a very different situation. taiwan is a developed modern democracy, a developed modern market economy industrial state, the world's tenth largest trading power. i see no real problems with taiwan's accession to the wto. this is a completely different problem, which cannot be compared. taiwan is granted a side agreement with china and becomes a member the day after china's accession. i have no comment other than that we should of course say yes.
in writing. -{sv) we voted against the committee on budgets' proposal to increase agricultural expenditure in category 1. we do not think it is right that about half of the total budget of the union is used to subsidize the production and export of agricultural products. the current agricultural policy is not only costly for the union. it also contributes to higher consumer prices. the agricultural sector is also well protected from foreign import competition. this has the effect of distorting competition and destabilising the world economy. the eu's common agricultural policy is a problem for developing countries. agriculture is indeed a field in which these countries are competitive with industrialized producers. the eu's common agricultural policy is creating serious obstacles here, in particular by subsidising this agriculture and subsidising it against import competition. the fact that the union grants aid to tobacco cultivation also deserves attention. these subsidies for tobacco cultivation must be abolished as soon as possible. the eu budget and activity should rather be used to tackle crossborder problems that member states cannot solve on their own. the common agricultural policy is fundamentally opposed to solidarity. it therefore calls for rapid reform.

not bureaucratic and unnecessary. progressive and far-reaching transparency rules in parliament will show that we are for the citizens and not the other way around.

court of auditors. – mr president, on behalf of the members of the european court of auditors, i would like to thank you for inviting me to present the 24th annual report of the court for the 2000 financial year. as in previous years, this presentation marks the start of the discharge procedure. the report follows the format used for the past three years, with chapters covering revenue and each of the expenditure areas under the headings of the financial perspectives. there is an additional chapter this year which marks the development of the union, which deals with pre-accession aid for which a separate financial perspective heading exists from 2000 onwards. you will also – as usual – find a chapter dedicated to the statement of assurance and, attached to the report, you will find the court’s observations on the implementation of the sixth, seventh and eight european development funds. the different chapters of the annual report address four main concerns. first, there is an analysis of the budgetary implementation and of the budgetary management practices for each specific financial perspective heading. then, you will find the follow-up of observations of the court in previous annual and special reports. thirdly, the specific appraisal in the context of the statement of assurance with information per financial perspective heading. finally, a new element this year is that the principal findings and recommendations in the special reports adopted by the court since the last discharge are summarised in the chapters according to the wishes of this parliament. this means that the annual report this year contains all the principal findings and recommendations concerning the implementation of the general budget and the european development funds that the court has published since the last discharge. you will also find, for the first time – not in the copy of annual report that you have in your hands today, but in the official journal version that will come when the official version has been printed, something that parliament has asked for many years – the text of the court in one column and the answers of the commission on the same page so as to make the report, at least in this respect, more user-friendly. concerning the budget management for 2000, one particular feature of budget implementation was the emergence of a very large surplus of revenue over expenditure, amounting to eur 11.6 billion, equivalent to more than 14 % of final payment expenditure. the main factors which led to this situation were a higher revenue yield than budgeted and – perhaps even more important – lower payments on structural measures, in particular those for the 2000-2006 programming period. the court recommends that the commission examine how it can better use the supplementary and amending budget procedure to avoid excessive budget surpluses, in order to avoid distorted revenue positions for the following year caused by the carrying over of surpluses. the court, responding to the wishes of the discharge authority, has continued to follow up the observations in previous annual and special reports. the main purpose of this is to review what action has been taken, principally by the commission, in response to the observations of the court, the recommendations of the council and the resolutions of the european parliament. in all of the subjects examined, some action has been taken. the extent and effectiveness of this action, however, vary considerably. in general, the commission, in particular where it agreed with the earlier observations and propositions, has adopted measures designed to respond to them and to improve the situation. in some cases corrective action has been slow, in others, staff and financial resources allocated have been insufficient to achieve the desired level of improvement. effecting improvements in the management and control of complex programmes involving many organisations at different levels is difficult and takes time. structural rigidities have often to be overcome, and in the areas of shared or decentralised management, which account for more than 80 % of the general budget, the commission requires action from others, such as member states. notwithstanding that achieving change can be difficult, the commission should ensure that it takes all the measures within its power to implement the recommendations of the court and of this parliament. in early 2001, as part of its follow-up work, the court reviewed progress in the replacement of the former anti-fraud unit (uclaf) with the european anti-fraud office (olaf). the new arrangements for olaf provide a more appropriate administrative framework, with greater independence for the office than its predecessor. the court continues to work closely with olaf. it has established formal procedures to ensure that the relations between the two organisations are efficient and effective. it has also established a constructive dialogue with the committee established to supervise the work of olaf, under the chairmanship of prof. delmas-marty. the court has recently prepared an opinion, soon to be published, on a proposal for a directive of the european parliament and the council on the protection of the community's financial interests. this directive is an important step forward in the fight against fraud. i would like to thank the chairman of the committee on budgetary control, mrs theato, on behalf of the court of auditors for her impressive work and dedication over the years to make this important directive possible. (applause) with regard to the statement of assurance, the court is of the opinion that the revised accounts for the financial year ended 31 december 2000 reflect reliably the community’s revenue and expenditure for the year and the financial situation at the end of the year. as far as the legality and regularity of underlying transactions are concerned, the audit of the principal management and control systems applicable to agriculture and structural measures revealed weaknesses in the functioning of control procedures aiming to secure the legality and regularity of the transactions. the audit of operational expenditure revealed an unacceptable incidence of error affecting the amount of the payments or the reality or the eligibility of the underlying transactions. in view of the results of its audit work taken together, the court is of the opinion that the transactions underlying the financial statements are, taken as a whole, legal and regular in respect of revenue, commitments and administrative expenditure but declines to provide this assurance in respect of payments in operational expenditure. since the last annual report the court has adopted eighteen special reports to be taken into account in the discharge procedure, containing the results of audits which focus on a wide range of specific areas in which the community’s financial management can be improved. the results of the court’s audits indicate significant positive findings. in the agricultural area the reformed clearance of accounts system and the integrated administrative and control system have contributed to improved management of large amounts of european union funds. the commission’s strategy for dealing with bse is basically sound. with regard to structural measures, the urban community initiative has helped the implementation of many urban development projects and has enabled local authorities to access union funds. in the external actions area, the work of the agency charged with the reconstruction of kosovo, was found to be both efficient and economical. the tacis cross-border co-operation programme is an instrument which has the potential to play a useful role in addressing issues relating to the new eastern border following the next accession. however, it was also found that major objectives have either not been achieved, or only limited progress had been made. further, it was frequently found that there was either insufficient or no evaluation by the commission of the achievement of the objectives. the commission’s reform programme is increasing the focus on results and performance measurement, within a resource allocation framework reflecting predetermined priorities and defined objectives. the court’s findings on recent programmes show that the commission still faces major challenges in this area. one of the most important issues in the discussion that we had last year here in parliament, when i presented the annual report for 1999, concerned the situation in the member states. the results are striking. the court has concentrated a large share of its resources on the audit of control systems in member states as a follow up to this debate. persistent weaknesses were found in member states’ checking of community operations concerning programmes where management is shared between the commission and the member states. this was the case for the integrated administrative and control system (iacs). it was also the case for export refunds, and concerning the control regulation for structural measures. improving the control arrangements in the member states is central to ensuring the correct use of union funds. it is also important if the court is to place some reliance for its own audit purposes on the checks carried out by the various audit and supervisory services in the commission, and in the member states. i will come back to this specific problem at the end of my speech. the recast of the financial regulation, which is an important element of the commission reform programme, maybe the first step that was taken, now seeks to clarify and strengthen the provisions relating to methods of implementation of the budget, advance payments, procurement, presentation of the accounts, and the offices. the introduction of new provisions intended to contribute to the establishment of a coherent legal framework in the field of budgetary execution, based on the primacy of the financial regulation, is another important improvement within the recast. however, as you will see from our annual report, some important matters of principle stated in the court’s opinion on the proposed changes have not been followed, which may lead to serious complexity in financial management. regarding enlargement, this year’s annual report has a new chapter dealing with pre-accession aid for which a separate financial perspective heading exists from 2000 onwards. in accordance with parliament recommendations in its 1999 discharge resolution, the court has been improving and intensifying the co-operation with the supreme audit institutions of the candidate countries during the past few years. furthermore, in liaison with the supreme audit institutions the court has been intensifying the exchange of information at different levels of national administrations. the setting-up of national systems of internal control and the external audit of eu funds made available to the candidate countries require closer co-operation between the institutions even before accession. enlargement will bring about changes in the way the council, the commission and the court of justice will work. this is also true for the court of auditors and for parliament. today, no one questions the provisions of the maastricht treaty to make the court of auditors one of the five independent institutions of the union. the nice treaty will mean substantial organisational changes in the near future, also for the european court of auditors, where the court will have the possibility of establishing an organisational structure in chambers with a more effective decision making, even if we will then consist of 25 member states. the court is actively preparing itself for this challenging reform. the court is developing its relations with the commission’s new internal audit service, with discussions covering a number of areas where we can work together. the court is giving high priority to assessing how it will be able to use the work of the internal audit service and the audit capabilities in the directorates general. one very promising sign of progress in this field was the conference, under the challenging heading of "verstehen" held at the initiative of the internal auditor, mr muis, on thursday and friday which mrs schreyer and mrs theato attended. i should like to say, as the president of the court of auditors, that this initiative might mean an extremely fruitful, concrete and promising step forward in the idea of a single audit system concerning european funds. the treaty states clearly that the european court of auditors assists the european parliament and the council in exercising their powers of control over the implementation of the budget. ladies and gentleman, i am soon to retire as president and member of the european court of auditors. during my seven years in the court i have been privileged to contribute to the court's growing importance, and to the continuous efforts it makes, in collaborating with parliament and meeting its needs in optimising the assistance it provides. the court is addressing the need in its statement of assurance methodology for more information on the nature and causes of the problems affecting the management of community funds by broadening the range of evidence in support of its conclusions. the recent reforms in the area of financial management and internal audit will enable the court to make greater use of the commission’s work, not least for its work on the statement of assurance. the court intends to continue to develop specific appraisals for each major area of community activity as a complement to its statement of assurance. the specific appraisals are now envisaged in the nice treaty and are an important tool to help the discharge authority to make appropriate recommendations with a view to improving management. one of the major tasks of the enlarged european union will be the collaboration between european and national supreme audit institutions. this has long been a key preoccupation of this parliament. but, as we all know, this co-operation has advanced slowly and parliament has repeatedly urged the court to improve the situation. after having followed progress for a number of years i have arrived at the conclusion that a good result in this respect requires initiatives at a higher level than purely technical co-operation between the supreme audit institutions of the member states and the european court of auditors. i would like to quote a member of this parliament, mr jacques santer who pointed out, when he was president of the commission, the importance of collaboration between the national parliaments and the european parliament. it would also be a key factor in the field of financial control. the supreme audit institutions act independently and have a strong collaboration with their respective national parliaments. so, collaboration between national parliaments and the european parliament could have a major influence on national audit bodies and the european court of auditors, in particular in the matter of financial control. the way of intergovernmental collaboration in this field is not the most practical route to take since the supreme audit institutions of the member states are, with the exception of my own country, independent of governments, as they are controlling them. but maybe the solution can be found in increased cooperation between this parliament and the national parliaments of the member states. let me finally add one little word. as parliamentarians in the european parliament, as commissioners in the european commission, also as auditors in the european court of auditors, we have a double role. we have to fulfil our tasks according to the treaty; to fulfil them as commissioners, as parliamentarians and as members of the court of auditors. but we also have another role in common. that is always to defend the idea of europe. (applause)
court of auditors. – mr president, on behalf of my outgoing colleagues and myself i would like to assure you that i have never regretted my resignation as much as at this moment. i wish i could resign every time we met in order to listen to this lovely music. so thank you very much for all the nice words. not least, when i go home to sweden i shall tell them of these kind words from denmark. thank you, mr. blak. i realise there is a heavy agenda, but i would like to take this opportunity to say a few words. i shall start with what mr kuhne has just said. it seems that a number of issues have been put behind us and there is a feeling – which i also had yesterday in a meeting of the committee on budgetary control – that we are now able to address some major issues for the future. the first is one which underlies the budget surplus problem. it is not so much the revenue i am thinking of, as the under-utilisation and the under-implementation of funds, which, in turn, has a lot to do with our problems in achieving good management and fighting fraud – namely the over-complexity of union funds and administration and regulations. i have had a long discussion on that with the discharge rapporteur, mr bourlanges, and i feel confident that this problem will be addressed now. my personal advice to parliament is to do everything in its power, together with other institutions, to simplify things. on all the major issues, such as the pre-accession funds, structural funds and export refunds and on the market regulations in agriculture, we have built a mountain of complexity which, if i may for once step into the realm of political judgment, raises the major question of the european citizens' confidence in this union. i would say that if i had to choose one of the tasks that mr kuhne mentioned as being the most important, it would be: simplify, for heaven's sake! in writing. - sv) as i did in the vote on this report on 6 september, i am choosing today to oppose the position adopted by my group on the rules concerning the sending of unsolicited advertising by e-mail to consumers. electronic advertising is generally a nuisance to the consumer, particularly because of the time it takes to open and delete advertising messages. in addition, the consumer must bear the cost of receiving and reading the advertisement. the distinction proposed in the report between advertising by e-mail on the one hand and advertising by sms, fax or automated telephone call on the other seems artificial to me. whatever of these technologies is used, the inconvenience to the consumer is the same. the opt-in solution is therefore preferable for all these advertising methods, as it is the most effective protection against unsolicited advertising. in a context where it is important that this report be adopted by parliament as soon as possible, the existing compromise must nevertheless be considered an acceptable alternative, even if it is infinitely less desirable. that is why i choose to support the report in the final vote, whatever the outcome on the question i have just raised.

(sv) mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i believe that public transport is incredibly important for europeans in both towns and sparsely populated areas. in this vote, i am therefore going to support the basic ideas behind the committee's proposal, which i believe points in the direction of better public transport. however, i oppose many of the simplistic statements made about tendering. our experience from sweden shows that tendering can be very good. account can be taken of the environment and of social considerations, safety regulations can be established and a good relationship can be enjoyed with the social partners. as a prerequisite, politicians must have responsibility for the tendering and establish these criteria openly. that is possible, as is shown by experience from sweden. we should be able to get rid of many of the simplistic statements about tendering. as i say, i am prepared to support the basic idea that regions and administrative districts should have freedom of choice. i also believe that it will, in practice, emerge that tendering can be used because it saves money that can be used for other purposes within the framework of public expenditure. finally, i want to say that i do not believe that the rule of reciprocity referred to in the committee's report should operate at european level.
(sv) mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i come from sweden where we have quite a high degree of exposure to competition and privatisation of various services. the lesson to be drawn from our experience is that the crucial issue is not really market competition or privatisation, as such, but quality, greater efficiency and good, cleverly designed organisation. these are aspects to which the commission has given insufficient consideration in its proposal. i want to express my thanks to mr jarzembowski who, as rapporteur, has come up with many criticisms of the commission's proposal. i should like to make a number of important exceptions, and i shall point out a couple of these. quite a few speakers have referred to pilotage. i believe that pilotage is quite clearly a service for the benefit of everyone. pilotage must therefore not be regarded in terms of the same economic criteria as ordinary production, but from a safety perspective. taking responsibility oneself can have very serious consequences when it comes to loading and unloading. that especially applies in view of the fact that flags of convenience are a common phenomenon. i am in favour of transparency and public scrutiny where ports are concerned. i am in favour of self-financed ports. i want to see quality and efficiency brought about through ports preparing their own development programmes instead of being subjected to extensive forms of control from brussels. such programmes must be carried out in cooperation with staff and clients at the ports.

the rapporteur: mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the report on the strategy for the future chemicals policy, which we are to discuss and adopt positions on in this debate, is extremely urgent and important because it will form the basis for a draft legislative text and for new framework legislation for the eu's chemicals policy. gratifyingly enough, the european council decided, at the gothenburg summit in june, that a new chemicals policy would have to be introduced by 2004 at the latest and that this time limit would have to be respected. the commission must therefore table its draft legislation in the course of 2002. the debate on deficiencies in the current chemicals policy has been going on for quite some time. among the criticisms made is the fact that there has been a lack of information about the majority of the 100 000 existing chemicals, that the work has proceeded far too slowly and that the present policy has, in practice, failed to protect human health and the environment. the commission's survey of the legislation on existing substances shows that human beings and the environment are potentially exposed to a large number of chemical substances whose dangerous characteristics are not known. we know that the human body contains measurable quantities of 350 chemicals. we also know that the quantity of brominated flame retardants in breast milk has been increasing each year. even if pcb and ddt are now surrounded by tough restrictions, it remains the case that high quantities of these chemicals are still found in the fatty tissue of human beings and animals throughout the world, even in the polar regions where these chemicals have not been produced at all. new chemicals are added to the list, for example pfos or perfluorooctane sulfonate, which does not bind in fatty tissue but, rather, in blood protein. these chemicals are found in a number of products, for example shampoo. through the regulation adopted by the council in order to overcome the deficiencies, detailed risk assessments should be carried out and recommendations made as to measures, etc. which might be taken. we now know that there are only 140 chemicals on the 'priority list' and that risk assessments have been completed in only eleven cases. even worse is the fact that not a single measure has been decided upon to reduce the risks, despite the fact that it has been shown that such measures are needed for the majority of these substances. the commission's white paper, produced by the environment and enterprise directorates-general, is trying to create a high degree of health and environmental protection, while maintaining the competitiveness of the european chemicals industry. this is done, for example, by saying that chemicals policy must stimulate technical innovation and the development of safer chemicals. i share the view of the majority of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy that the strategy should focus on the protection of the environment and human health and that the starting point for the commission's white paper should be the debate in the council and the growing unease about the fact that the current chemicals policy cannot offer adequate protection of the environment and human health. naturally, the economic and social aspects must be accorded a fair degree of importance as parts of a strategy for sustainable development. if a majority of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy voted for a proposal that goes further than the commission's and shows how an effective, open and consumer-friendly control of chemicals should look in the future, that is completely in line with, and receives strong political support from, the european council summit in june when the conclusions drawn by the 15 member states were significantly stricter and more far-reaching than the commission's proposal. there is therefore an express political will to adopt the policy for a new chemicals strategy proposed by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, above all when it comes to the scope of the reach system. as currently desired by the industry and the group of the european people's party and european democrats, only cmr 1 and 2 and pops are to be included which, in that case, cannot be said to be much in the way of a strategy for the future. even among industrial companies there is a desire to go further, for a competitive industry cannot be achieved through the unacceptable situation we have at present but only by producing safer chemicals and chemicals that protect human health and the environment. i therefore believe that we should stick to the compromise reached between the various political groups and to the proposal backed by the majority.
(sv) mr president, the eu's chemicals policy requires a thorough renovation and a single, new and comprehensive set of regulations. mrs schörling's report from the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, which we are now discussing, provides an excellent point of departure for a new chemicals policy. with this report as a basis, the commission should quickly be able to table proposals for a new directive. the strength of the report is that it really puts the environment and public health first, and that must be our main task. we also believe that industry has a lot to gain from strict regulations in as much as they encourage modern manufacturers, promote innovation and mean that old chemicals that have not been investigated are no longer promoted, as they are in fact at present. the following points in the report are especially important to our group. first, the proposal as to which chemicals are to be included under the requirements for registration, evaluation and authorization should be made more comprehensive, as proposed. secondly, tough requirements should be made of chemicals, and of products containing chemicals, when these are imported. thirdly, everything must be done to minimize the number of animal experiments. fourthly, the substitution principle should be made a crucial component of chemicals policy. great importance should be attached to this so that safer alternatives will always be sought. fifthly, the use of the most hazardous chemicals of all should be considerably restricted. our group is pleased with the committee's proposals. we nonetheless have some minor amendments we believe could further strengthen the report. we wish to emphasize the demand that it should be those departments of the commission with responsibility for environmental protection that should approve new chemicals. it is also a question of further tightening up the requirements made of the most hazardous chemicals of all. in committee, the rapporteur chose a very sound and open method of working which gave all the political groups the opportunity to compromise at an early stage, and i think that is something positive.
(sv) mr president, i would like to thank europe needs a new strategy for dealing with the issues surrounding chemicals. we are concerned here with the health of all of us, as well as with that of future generations. the commission's white paper is a good point of departure for continued work on the strategy. mrs schörling's report creates good conditions for bringing about necessary changes. it is exceptionally important that parliament adopts the report in such a form that it has the same thrust given to it by the rapporteur. in the debate that took place while the report was coming to fruition, many objections expressed the fear that stricter regulations would put jobs and economic development at risk. that is an anxiety which must, of course, be taken entirely seriously but, at the same time, it is necessary to raise one's sights and look beyond jobs and the economy. economic growth and access to jobs can never be invoked to prevent necessary requirements concerning the use of chemicals, work on achieving sustainable development and a high level of protection of public health and of employees. what is the use of jobs and a sound economy if we poison our countryside and destroy what is essential for future generations? in writing. -{sv) research and development are of crucial importance if we want europe to be a continent of economic growth and prosperity. international competition, particularly in the united states, increases the need for new and more extensive efforts in favour of researchers. in its proposal for the 6th framework programme, the commission has identified several important problems and research areas on which europe must focus. it is of course important that european researchers cooperate and that research efforts are coordinated in some way. it is also of great importance that we not only promote the physical mobility of researchers but also facilitate the dissemination of their research results. we are concerned that the framework programme provides for a very considerable centralisation of research. significant resources are invested in a small number of selected areas. this is tantamount to directing research in an unwelcome way, since the particular research orientations of the individual member states will be governed by the priorities defined by the commission. for small countries with only a modest research budget, the eu will thus risk becoming the main source of funding for this work, which is a centralisation which i consider to be negative. as liberals, we are committed to the principle of freedom of inquiry. from the point of view of efficiency, open research has proved its superiority over research commissioned by political authorities. a society which allows its researchers a wide margin of manoeuvre in the choice of subjects and methods will, in the long run, achieve better results than a society where the orientations of research are defined by the political class. the principle of freedom applied to the human quest for knowledge has, for centuries, strongly influenced research in europe. higher education institutions and universities play an important role in cooperating with the public sector and economic actors, but their main task should not be to take orders from each other. we believe that the sixth framework programme places too much emphasis on free research. we have therefore decided to abstain in the vote on the caudron report.
in writing. -sv) the caudron report takes a position on the priorities and frameworks to be assigned to research in europe, research financed by community funds through the sixth framework programme. as a christian democrat, i believe that our society needs ethical frameworks. the same is true of research. the most fundamental aspect of this is the question of human dignity. however commendable the goal pursued, it does not justify using humans as a means to achieve it. research involving embryonic stem cells is a highly controversial subject from an ethical point of view, and in addition, this practice is prohibited in four of our member states. this is not the case with adult stem cells. i therefore believe that research using adult stem cells should be given priority and that funding from the framework programme should not be allocated to this highly ethically controversial research on embryonic stem cells. under the principle of subsidiarity, national governments will be able to choose how to support research. the resources allocated to the framework programme are nevertheless limited. we have to prioritize. research which is ethically controversial and, moreover, clearly in conflict with the legislation of several member states should not be supported. that is why i have chosen to support amendment no 334, which i am one of the signatories of, and which reads in paragraph 4: 'work on embryonic stem cells cannot be financed from community resources, as no agreement has been reached between the member states on this matter'.

mr president, i would like to thank you. on 5 december 2000, the council decided that the granting of state operating aid of up to 9% to the shipbuilding industry would cease for all member states at the end of 2000. this is for two reasons. the state aid had not been shown to have a major impact on the industry's viability and there was a principled belief that state aid should be reduced as much as possible and preferably be phased out. parliament shared this view as recently as last year. state aid to shipyards is also contrary to international trade agreements, as is shown by the commission's attempts in the autumn of 1999 and spring of 2000 to reach a negotiated settlement with south korea for breaching existing trade agreements by means of large-scale state aid. when this fails, the commission instead proposes a so-called temporary protection scheme for the shipbuilding industry with operating aid of between 6 and 14%. the committee proposes that this should continue until the end of 2003, and at the same time that a wto dispute settlement procedure against south korea for breach of existing trade agreements should be opened. the reintroduction of large-scale state aid within the eu is unlikely to improve the conditions for resolving the dispute with south korea. it would be more correct to reject the commission's proposal and to focus all our efforts on the wto procedure against south korea. in conclusion, i would like to put the following question to commissioner bolkestein: how do you see this proposal in relation to the eu's competition policy, where we try to minimise, preferably eliminate, state aid to industries in order to create fair and healthy competition between companies?

in writing. -{sv) given the risks that chemicals pose to human health and the environment, the swedish christian democrats believe it is obvious that chemicals which have not been subjected to certain tests should also not be allowed to be marketed, according to the principle 'no data, no marketing'. we also support the substitution principle, which has long been part of swedish chemicals policy. similarly, we believe that the burden of proof should be reversed for substances whose use is of concern, which means that it is the industry, not the authorities, that has to prove that the use of the substances concerned can be accepted. since risks can be induced even at low doses, we believe that chemicals which are imported or produced in the european union in quantities of less than one tonne should be covered by this new system. we shall therefore differ on several points from the position adopted by the epp-ed group.
