the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen of the commission! i think i may have broken some kind of record here today. i have been given a full 15 minutes to speak because i am rapporteur on three different but rather similar cases. eventually, i won't need all this time. finally, it seems that the balkans are moving more steadily in the right direction. we finally have something like peace, albeit under armed surveillance, albeit with continued inter-communal violence and a long way to go to genuine peaceful coexistence. the disarmament of armed groups in macedonia has been successfully implemented, not least thanks to a major eu contribution. we can therefore be optimistic, even though history has shown us all too often that peace is fragile, not least in the western balkans. democracy is also making progress. last autumn the milosevic dictatorship was overthrown by the determined action of the yugoslav people. in both croatia and bosnia and herzegovina, although with some problems in the latter case, democratic forces have made progress. last week's elections in kosovo were conducted impeccably. it was a privilege to be present at this historic event as an observer from the european parliament. however, much remains to be done. it is a matter of a great need for purely physical reconstruction, but also for the reconstruction of the infrastructure and the economy that were allowed to decay in the 1990s. perhaps most importantly, a genuine reconciliation process is needed to address the ethnic cleansing that has occurred in large parts of these countries and to resolve the major remaining problems of refugees and displaced persons. it is against this background that we in the committee on budgets have adopted three reports on the extension of the time-limit, and in some cases even the extension of the time-limit, for macro-financial assistance to macedonia, bosnia and herzegovina and yugoslavia. first, something about macedonia. in november 1999, the council decided to grant macedonia macrofinancial assistance of eur 80 million, partly in the form of loans and a grant of up to eur 30 million. the decision was taken in the context of the war in kosovo, as this crisis was causing great costs in macedonia, where hundreds of thousands of people had fled. the aim has been to facilitate the implementation of structural reforms and to remedy the consequences of the economic disruptions caused by the kosovo conflict. the aid is of course conditional, in particular on continuing its programme of economic and public administration reforms and on implementing the ohrid agreements earlier this autumn. as you know, macedonia was hit by this internal armed conflict. it has, of course, created further problems and delayed the implementation of aid. only some of them have been paid within the timeframe. that is why we now want to extend the legal basis for these aids. we're fine with that. however, the commission is also proposing an increased financial contribution, as problems are beginning to arise with macedonia, where developments have been otherwise positive. the problems have been exacerbated by the conflict. that is why the commission is now proposing to increase the contribution by 18 million. the committee on budgets considers that the commission's proposal is well balanced in the light of this political context. nevertheless, we have tabled some amendments to emphasize that this aid should be managed by the commission but in close consultation with both the foreign and security policy committee and the economic and financial committee. the background to this is that we consider it important to control not only the purely financial management of aid but also more political aspects, for example to ensure that no part of the aid is used to equip the macedonian army. we also stress that this type of aid, i.e. grants combined with a long-term 'soft' loan, should be seen as exceptional and should not become the norm for the future. then there is the matter of bosnia and herzegovina. in may 1999 we decided to provide this aid, a loan of 20 million and a grant of up to 40 million. this was of course also done to ease the economic situation and to support reconstruction in bosnia. the conditions were obviously that the economy and democratic institutions would continue to be reformed. we've had some problems with the implementation. a contribution of 15 million is therefore still to be paid. in order to implement it, we therefore need to extend the legal basis. we must also take into account that the elections held in bosnia in november 2000 led to a very long delay; we had negotiations, the high representative had to intervene, etc. this has delayed both reform and legislative work, and hence also the implementation of aid. economic growth has not been very good. the commission's proposal to amend this council regulation to extend the deadline until the end of 2002 is therefore supported by the committee on budgets. however, the same conditions shall apply in this case as in the case of macedonia in respect of the committee on foreign affairs and security policy. finally, a word on the third matter, yugoslavia. we decided just this year to provide this macrofinancial assistance of up to 300 million, again in the form of loans and grants. the aim is, of course, first of all to support the economic, social and political stability of yugoslavia, but also, as i would like to emphasize, to promote the role of yugoslavia as a stabilizing factor in the whole region. this year, it has become clear that the economic situation was perhaps a little more strained than we previously thought. in particular, the privatisation process has taken longer, which has also led to the loss of revenue from privatisation to the state budget. we also know that it has been a difficult process to find a solution to yugoslavia's relations with the world bank, the imf, etc. if money is not added, there is obviously a great risk that the economic situation will deteriorate further. we must bear in mind that yugoslavia already has an extremely high level of debt, over 140% of gdp. the commission therefore wants these new amounts - an increase of 45 million - to be in the form of grants. the committee on budgets agrees, but stresses that this must nevertheless be seen as part of the eu's multiannual programme and that it is exceptional support. we have also added that the aid will be paid on condition that yugoslavia continues to develop into a state governed by the rule of law and that it continues to cooperate closely with the international court of justice in the hague. in all three cases, we also believe that the ordinary budgetary procedure must be followed in order to provide new resources for the current financial year. it can therefore only be implemented through a parallel process with a transfer of funds from other budget lines, which requires the decision of the budgetary authority. mr president, i would like to thank you. the commission requested that these cases be dealt with as a matter of urgency before the last partsession in strasbourg. i said that i thought there were two things that had to be done first. firstly, we wanted to see the commission's concrete proposals for transfers to produce the necessary 45 and 18 million respectively, so that we could also see where the funds would be taken from. these proposals for transfers are now available and have been discussed in the committee on budgets. secondly, we wanted to be able to reach an agreement with the council on next year's budget so that we could ensure that these measures can be combined with a continued high level of other measures for the western balkans, for reconstruction, etc. we have now reached an agreement with the council, which means that these increased macroeconomic aid will be seen as front loading of next year's budget appropriations. at the same time, we are unanimously accepting the commission's preliminary draft budget for the balkans, which i also hope will be the result of the second budgetary debate in the next partsession in strasbourg. in this context, we therefore support these three proposals. finally, i would like to say a few words about my opinion and that of the committee on budgets on the lagendijk report on the european agency for reconstruction. i myself have had the opportunity on several occasions to experience the work of the agency and i consider it to be exemplary, as mr lagendijk also believes and as the court of auditors' report has confirmed. i think there are some specific reasons for this success. one of them is that, as parliament has called for, a more decentralised model of decision-making and implementation of eu assistance has been implemented, and that the heavy-handed, double decision-making that we had previously in the traditional committee system has been avoided. despite these excellent results of the agency, the committee on budgets wishes to emphasize in its amendments the importance of correct budgetary principles in distinguishing between administrative and operational expenditure, in particular in order to enable us to carry out a proper and qualified evaluation of the activities. we also want to introduce a reference to article 274 of the treaty to make it clear that this is an operation for which the commission, not primarily the member states, have the responsibility for implementation.
in writing. - s7v) we decided today to abstain from voting on the report on the criminal protection of the community's financial interests. we are not opposed to the establishment of a community public prosecutor's office for financial matters, but the report goes far too far and even goes into details which are not relevant at this stage. the rapporteur would also like to convert the commission's directive into a regulation, which we oppose.
in writing. -{v) the egyptian government must take measures to improve the situation of the 8 to 10 million copts in egypt, who are discriminated against in legislation, in professional life, in social life and in the exercise of their religion. at present, copts are second-class citizens in the eyes of the state's policy on recruitment. religious freedom is not a fully recognized right in egypt, since christians are denied the possibility of repairing their churches or building new ones. it is high time that the christian minority could also experience the impartiality of the judicial system.
in writing. -sv) the fight against terrorism is and must be one of the eu's priority tasks. however, it must not be imposed at the expense of judicial security. we are concerned that eu governments are now rushing to coordinate national legislation to combat terrorism. different legal traditions will merge in a very short time. this could undermine judicial security. this is particularly the case with the commission's proposal for a definition of the concept of terrorism. we believe that this definition is far too broad and leaves too many interpretation possibilities open. people who protest, for political reasons, against the current structures of society, and who use illegal means to do so, may well fall under the proposed definition. of course, it is not reasonable to label as terrorists anyone who protests against an unjust organization in society. we need more concrete definitions than those given so far of what should constitute the basis of terrorism. the more specific amendments proposed by parliament are welcome in this respect. the european parliament and the national parliaments must also have a real opportunity to exert their influence before the final decision is taken in the council.
in writing. -{sv) the fiori report takes a position on many sensitive issues relating to human genetics, and in particular on the ethical principles to be applied in this field. as a christian democrat, i believe that society needs ethical frameworks. the most fundamental aspect to be considered is respect for human dignity. as commendable as the end may be, it does not justify using human beings as a means. therefore, cloning is not justified in any form, whether one chooses to call it therapeutic or reproductive. it is not possible to draw such a line, since therapeutic cloning is no different from reproductive cloning at the beginning of the process, except that it is decided at the outset to destroy the embryo resulting from the transfer of the cell nucleus. the embryo already contains all the necessary information to make a human being. development up to birth, and beyond, does not tend to make our beings human - it is the development of beings as human beings. research using embryonic stem cells is an ethically controversial practice, and is prohibited in four of our member states (germany, austria, ireland and portugal). this is not the case with the use of adult stem cells. i therefore believe that priority should be given to research on adult stem cells and that the very controversial research on embryonic stem cells should not be given funding from the framework programme. in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity, this does not preclude national governments from making their own choices in the allocation of research funding. the funds for the framework programme are, however, limited. we have to prioritize. the commission has therefore decided not to support such a controversial type of research, which is in clear conflict with the legislation of several member states. that is why i have decided to support the report as it stood before it was put to the vote in plenary, and to remain faithful to the line which led me to vote for the caudron report on the sixth framework programme.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i think it is a bit amazing that we have now achieved such good legislation. two years is a long time, but i personally thought it would take longer. it is extremely important that we talk about security, and on security we can reach a common understanding. this is measurable. the most important aspect of this new legislation is openness and transparency. no matter what we say or how we act, new crises will arise. there will be new scandals. we will have new scientific propaganda, of one sort or another, which will frighten the population, rightly or wrongly. the only way to deal with this is to do it with total transparency and openness. the commission has already made a number of proposals. now, anyone who is interested - consumer organisations, environmental organisations, the media - will be informed immediately. this is not just the information from a majority of scientists on a panel, but also the views of a minority. this means that no one can say that the union is hiding anything from its citizens. in conclusion, i would like to say that all this is good, but i wonder what will happen to compliance and control. where is the possible system of sanctions against those member states and companies which do not comply with safety standards? this is a step, it's a big step and the first one. but let us never forget that it must be implemented, otherwise it will be just words on paper.
madam president, i would like to thank you. in our group we fully agree with the approach of this report. we also think it is right to re-submit some of the amendments which have not been incorporated into this common position. we also believe that these amendments are of such a nature that it should then be possible very quickly to find a common solution together with the council of ministers and the commission. the most important amendment by the european parliament is the one which makes it clear that this is an authority for food safety in particular - not for food in general. for us, this is important. it is important to focus on security issues. amendment no 4 concerns the compatibility of eu food rules with other international agreements and regulations. of course, the eu will cooperate in this area with other parts of the world. the development of the world trade organisation, in particular, is liable to lead to a number of conflicts between different regulatory systems. in that case, we believe it is extremely important to assert the right to stricter regulations for health and environmental reasons. amendment no 22 concerns the financing of the authority. we consider it a matter of principle that an authority should be financed from the budget. it shall not be financed by fees charged for its services. that is why amendment no 22 is important to us. some amendments are about transparency, and we naturally support them. amendment no 41 provides that the meetings of the board of directors shall as a rule be public and that the exception to the closed door procedure shall be made. we think that's very important. we also think that the proposal for a broad geographical anchoring of members is a good one. the proposals we are now discussing have their background in the serious crises in the eu in recent years in terms of food safety. it has been about dioxin, bse and salmonella, to name a few areas. these scandals are closely linked to a highly industrialized farming system and an agricultural policy in which large-scale farming and yields have become more important than the environment, animal welfare and food security. if the eu is to tackle the root causes of the problems in this area, it will first need to reorganise the cap to put sustainability, the environment and animal welfare first. only then can we really tackle the root causes of the food safety problems in our union.
mr president, i would like to thank you. my colleagues have made a number of justified criticisms of the procedures for today's programme. however, let me say that it is positive that there is a kind of evaluation of the year that has passed and that there is a more strategic thinking in the way the commission intends to work. but let's talk about the future. president prodi outlined the way forward on the question of the annual programme. as rapporteur for this parliament, i have had occasion to follow this matter for quite some time. i hope that we can conclude this soon and start work on the 2003 programme. as the codecision procedure has developed, the parliament and the council must play a greater role in the preparation of the annual programme. this does not mean that the commission's right of initiative is being called into question, but that the three institutions are moving more in tandem, which is conducive to both the transparency and the efficiency of our joint work. it will also make it easier for national parliaments and citizens to follow the process. as for the timetable, it seems we may now be able to agree. an initial presentation of the general priorities will be followed by a close dialogue between the commission and the committees concerned to determine the details. it is important that the parliamentary committees are involved at an early stage, because it is the committees that then deal with the proposal. then we can present the final proposal by the beginning of november at the latest. as regards the content of the programme, the criticism of last year does not mean that we do not want any legislative proposals. on the contrary, our joint framework agreement states that this is the annual legislative programme. parliament has called for clear proposals with a hierarchy of priorities, reasons for the decision, timetable, budgetary reference, legal basis and responsibility. the monitoring and evaluation must also be improved. as we have seen, many proposals are being reintroduced, while others are being dropped. a common database could be set up to follow the decision-making process in each case. mr president of the commission! a clear, transparent and predictable process would benefit all the institutions and also the citizens we are here to serve. it is our joint responsibility to ensure that this happens.
the committee on development and cooperation has been asked to submit an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you. i have a limited speaking time, but i would still like to raise a few important issues. in the field of aid, where i've worked, there's definitely been a breakthrough this year. we have reached an agreement with the commission both on the way in which the reporting will be done and on the focus of our future support. i thank the commission for the constructive spirit in which our discussions have been conducted. parliament's transparency and control over aid money will be considerably increased. the conditions are now much better than before for bringing order to an activity which unfortunately is often characterized by the opposite. the fact that the commission has accepted as a benchmark that one third of the aid should be used for social aid is also a definite success. the agreement guarantees a greater focus on programmes and activities of particular importance in the fight against poverty. i would also like to thank mr costa neves for his way of handling the work. it has been marked by openness, kindness and efficiency in a very difficult job.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to begin by thanking the rapporteurs costa neves, buitenweg and others. they have not only sought broad solutions in parliament but have also insistently defended our positions vis-à-vis the council, which is not least important. i would also like to thank the council and the commission. we have sometimes been critical of the forms, not least in connection with the first reconciliation, but in the end we found a constructive tone. some difficult questions have been resolved. it is of course extremely important that we have been able to provide substantial additional support to afghanistan in view of the enormous needs that exist. i am personally pleased that we have also been able to channel some of this support to women's participation in the construction of the future afghanistan. i am also pleased that we have found solutions for the spanish and portuguese fishing fleets and for the support for the border regions. so, although the budgetary work has gone well, we still see a number of problems ahead. one of the main problems is that the eu budget is not sufficiently flexible. every year we see events in our world that require the eu to provide resources quickly. in 2000 it was kosovo, in 2001 serbia, and this year it is afghanistan. we have been able to raise money for these contingencies, including through the flexibility mechanism, but the process has been very cumbersome. now we see the shortcomings. this year we were able to use the flexibility mechanism for other operations in category 2. when a crisis like the one in afghanistan occurs at the same time, the problem becomes clear. we should then of course seriously discuss the path indicated by the interinstitutional agreement, namely the revision of the budgetary ceiling in the budgetary plan. the council is stubbornly refusing this, even though we have a budget in which large sums are never spent! the problem then becomes that parliament is left with only one choice: either to budget every penny to the roof and leave no margin for unforeseen events, or to cut back on other of parliament's heavy priorities and thus let aid recipients in other parts of the world in practice pay for our new needs in the balkans or in afghanistan. we can of course solve such a dilemma once, perhaps even twice, but when these efforts tend to become permanent, when the situation in kosovo, serbia or afghanistan gives rise to multiannual programmes that are superimposed on each other without the other needs disappearing, then the situation eventually becomes unsustainable. i think one of the big issues for next year will be to find new ways of increasing flexibility. i therefore welcome the commission's ideas as a starting point for this discussion and hope that the council will show the same openness. the poor implementation of the eu budget is also a fundamental flaw. we have now launched a number of new methods, which not only involve increasing payments, but we are trying to identify what we call the abnormally lagging areas, we are trying to clarify the requirements for the implementation profile, we have had extensive discussions on what services are needed to enable us to implement actions in different policy areas, and finally, as mr wijkman pointed out, we have also launched a new benchmarking method in the area of aid. these are important advances, but i believe they need to be developed further in the future if we are to regain confidence in the way we handle taxpayers' money. as next year's rapporteur, i naturally see some important areas that need to be worked through. the expansion is getting closer. we simply have to look not only at the short term perspective during the preparations, but also at the long term perspective when enlargement really hits the budgetary plans. finally, we must also look seriously at the reform of the commission. if we fail to do this - and it is not just a matter of reviewing the rules, but above all of looking at evaluation, monitoring and how we use taxpayers' money - then i do not think we will succeed in changing the way citizens perceive the eu. we must have an efficient administration and restore the citizens' confidence in the union. this is one of the most important tasks for future budgetary work.

the rapporteur. the commission is not prepared to accept this. this report has gone very well. the commission and the council adopted most of the parliament's requests at first reading. the directive now covers, among other things, the user interface, and we have an aggregate concept so that we know what we are talking about. all products intended for animal feed are now defined by a common term in all feed legislation. it is very good that the important rules on, for example, the dilution ban are now being implemented. this means that you cannot take a lot with too high levels of dioxin, for example, and dilute it with something else to get under the limits. i personally think it is extremely good that we have something called action limits in the directive, and this, ladies and gentlemen, is in practice the precautionary principle. this means that if, for example, a dairy discovers that the level of mycotoxin, a mold poison, is increasing in the milk, an investigation can be launched even though the limit for when the substance becomes dangerous has not yet been exceeded. then you can start to investigate why the levels of mycotoxin are rising in the milk and go back in the chain and stop and prevent before the limit of poisoning has been reached. the council has accepted everything except one question which we in the committee have now been forced to take up again, namely whether or not an unsuitable consignment of feed should be returned to the third world, for example. i and the committee believe that it is deeply immoral to simply send products which do not comply with the requirements of the directive on this way, when we do not know where the products end up, whether they will be used in the poorest areas or whether they will simply return to our own market in a different form. the committee therefore decided to table amendments which remove this possibility of re-export. we also chose to maintain a transitional period of six months after the entry into force of the directive. the commission and the council then came under the belgian presidency and offered negotiations. we tried to reach a compromise, which we did. i would like to thank both the belgian presidency and the commission for the excellent and constructive cooperation with me and my co-rapporteurs in this matter. we agreed on a compromise solution whereby re-exports would be regulated in the same way as in the general food law which we have voted through in parliament today by a very large majority. the aim is to cover technically a time-gap so that, when the general law enters into force, its provisions will apply to re-exports under this directive. it is not a new proposal, but a bridge over time. the compromise amendment we tabled has exactly the same wording as the whitehead report. but tonight, when everything was clear, we were informed that the council did not want to accept this compromise. i realize with deep regret that we must withdraw our compromise proposal and thus be forced to compromise. i would like to say to the council of ministers that this is a clear abuse of the institution of conciliation. this is not a dispute of such quality and seriousness that we should have to go that far, but i and my co-rapporteurs find the situation such that parliament is forced to highlight it. when five party groups sign a compromise, we mean business!

mr president, i would like to thank you. it has not been an easy task for the belgian presidency. years of preparation, careful planning - all of it was brutally overturned after september 11, 2001. the developments in afghanistan and the fight against terrorism have completely dominated the agenda. i was going to congratulate the belgians because they have done a very good job. perhaps someone could tell the president of the council this. as regards laeken, my group is pleased that the declaration was so ambitious. it asks the right questions and allows the convention to work constructively and with an open mind. many of the issues raised, such as the way forward for a constitution and a clearer division of powers, are in line with the liberal group's agenda. the three persons appointed to the bureau, three well-known statesmen with long experience and great commitment, nevertheless raise certain doubts. can this trio of men, at a fairly mature age, really inspire the citizens, especially the young, in the debate on the future? it would have been good to have a slightly more mixed age structure and, as many here have pointed out, a woman on the bureau. after all, more than half the eu's population is made up of women.
